Question: Regarding making up prayers at sunrise and sunset — and regarding voluntary prayers at these times on a Friday — is that permissible or not?

Answer: It is makrūh on Friday and at other times as well. And furthermore:

<!-- page 92 -->
Whoever prays the later ʿIshāʾ prayer with the imam while still owing Maghrib should first make up Maghrib, then spend the Sunnah of Maghrib and the ʿIshāʾ in the Hereafter as make-ups, and the optional prayer of the Hereafter ʿIshāʾ is performed until midnight — and this is called the night prayer. Whoever prays the Dhuhr prayer while still owing Fajr should first make up the Fajr prayer, then the Sunnah of Dhuhr, then the optional Dhuhr prayer as make-ups.

Question: A man who did not pray the obligatory prayers in his youth and then repented after a long time — what should he do? Should he perform only the obligatory prayers, or should he also pray the Sunnah and optional prayers?

Answer: Whoever abandons the prescribed prayers for a long time without a valid excuse is not considered a Muslim during that period, because the Prophet — may God bless him and his family — said: between a servant and disbelief is the abandonment of prayer. No legitimate act of worship is accepted from such a person, in accordance with the saying of our master, the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī

Ṭālib — may God's prayers be upon him: "Prayer is the pillar of religion; it is the first thing God examines from what the son of Adam has done — if it is sound, He looks at the rest of his deeds, and if it is not sound, no deed of his will be regarded." And: "There is no share of Islam for one who does not pray, and he disbelieves in it." Nothing is accepted from one who does not pray, because by abandoning prayer he becomes like a disbeliever — one from whom the jizyah is taken, or who is killed, or whose descendants are taken captive, or who is considered ritually impure like the disbeliever, or whose sacrificial animal is not permissible to eat. Yet all of these rulings are nonetheless the rulings that apply to a Muslim, because the two testimonies of faith have brought him under the rule of Islam. Whoever was in this state and then repents

<!-- page 93 -->
and resolves to perform the prescribed prayers with sincere intention for God's sake, seeking His pleasure — free from hypocrisy and ostentation — God will accept his repentance, for He said: ⟪ar:يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا تُوبُوا إِلَى اللَّهِ تَوْبَةً نَّصُوحًا عَسَىٰ رَبُّكُمْ أَن يُكَفِّرَ عَنكُمْ سَيِّئَاتِكُمْ وَيُدْخِلَكُمْ جَنَّاتٍ تَجْرِي مِن تَحْتِهَا الْأَنْهَارُ⟫ — such a person is truly a Muslim and all his legitimate deeds will be accepted. What he must do is repeat the prayers he missed in the period before his repentance, and pray all the prayers he encounters after his repentance along with their Sunnah and optional components. Even if he makes up the prescribed prayers he missed before repentance and asks God for forgiveness for those sins, he will be rewarded. But if he deliberately broke his fast during the month of Ramadan in the period of his abandonment of the prescribed prayers — before his repentance — he must both make up and expiate for each obligatory fast he missed, just as is required for any missed prescribed obligation. He should not pray the Sunnah of any prayer in its time until after he has fulfilled the obligatory prayer. The meaning of the verse ⟪ar:يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا⟫ — "O you who believe" — refers to those who have affirmed the two testimonies of faith; thus faith in this verse means affirming that there is no god but God and that Muhammad is the messenger of God.

On another matter: There is nothing wrong with delaying prayer if food is prepared and one will not miss the prayer, as the Prophet — may God bless him and his family — said: "When the evening meal is served, begin with it." This is taken from the text of the book of commentary on the Pillars: a man should pray a prayer in a time other than the time he intended — that is, a different prayer from the one he meant — and if he missed the time he intended, the first prayer is still valid for him from his family and his wealth. It is stated in the book Mukhtaṣar al-Muṣannaf that whoever wishes to combine


<!-- page 94 -->
Whoever wishes to combine Dhuhr and 'Asr due to a valid excuse should pray Dhuhr before the sun declines and pray 'Asr after it. If someone reverses this order and prays 'Asr first after the sun's decline and then prays Dhuhr, he is permitted to do so for Dhuhr, but he must repeat the 'Asr prayer. Likewise, if he prayed Dhuhr after the sun's decline without ablution and then performed ablution and prayed 'Asr, both prayers stand. However, if his Dhuhr was invalid for any reason, it is not permissible for him to have based his 'Asr upon it. As for one who is uncertain of the time due to clouds or similar conditions and has not arrived at certainty, praying at the end of the time with certainty is better than praying at the beginning of the time with doubt. Whoever prays some or all of his prayers before their time and later becomes certain of this must repeat them. It is also stated in the transmitted reports that whoever misses a prayer should pray it whenever he remembers it, except at the time of sunrise, at its zenith, or at the time of sunset. It is further reported that there is scholarly consensus that no prayer is to be performed after Fajr until the sun rises, unless one is making up a missed obligatory prayer or performing an obligatory act such as the prayer of ihram, the two rak'ahs of tawaf, the two Eid prayers, and the like. Voluntary prayer in those times is disliked, though they held that on Friday it is not blameworthy. It is stated in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf that it is permissible for those who have missed prayers to make them up outside their designated times, with the adhan and iqamah, in congregation. There is also a matter transmitted from a book of questions and answers found in the handwriting of our master and Mawlana Hassan bin 'Abd al-Malik — may Allah sanctify his soul — concerning what has been said about the Prophet, peace be upon him, that volunteering in prayer on Friday is permissible from the beginning of the day until the afternoon prayer, alongside the Dhuhr prayer at the beginning of its time.

<!-- page 95 -->
After the 'Asr prayer has been prayed, however, it is not permissible to offer voluntary prayers. Before that point, one may pray the Dhuhr prayer and then volunteer in additional prayers as he wishes until the end of the 'Asr time. Once 'Asr is delayed to the point that it becomes very late, voluntary prayer is no longer permissible. The book of Clarification states that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, combined Dhuhr with 'Asr, and Maghrib with 'Isha', without being on a journey, without illness, and without any pressing need. It is also stated in the aforementioned book of questions and answers: Question — What do you say, O one initiated into the divine mysteries, regarding the two rak'ahs of the midnight prayer? Does one pray them after the midpoint of the night, when one rises from sleep, or only upon awakening? Answer — The time of the end of the night is best. It is stated regarding the matters asked of Sulayman ibn Hasan, and the answers given by our master Dawud bin 'Ajab — may Allah sanctify their souls and grant us their intercession — that the question concerned one who had abandoned prayer for a period of time and then repented sincerely to Allah Almighty, but did not know how many prayers he had missed — even if he had prayed at some times and abandoned prayer at others without keeping track — and what he was therefore obligated to do. The answer is: if he has repented and entered into the Holy Covenant, he should not abandon prayer any further, and there is no sin upon him for what has passed. Whoever returns to Allah, Allah accepts his return, and whoever reverts, Allah will hold him to account. Our master, Mawlana and our father Muhammad bin al-Fahd bin Salah — may Allah sanctify him and grant us his intercession — wrote in his letter,

<!-- page 96 -->
that honored letter attributed to him without reservation, which serves as a guide for seekers and an exposition of intricate matters in the questions of fiqh and religious affairs. The first question: I asked — may Allah aid you — regarding a man who died and bequeathed three gold letters' worth of payment to those who would make up on his behalf the prayers and fasts he had not performed, noting that the specified fee was insufficient for what needed to be made up. Who bears the obligation in that case? The answer is: You are required to act accordingly, and may Allah, the Exalted, bless you with the truth of the matter. It has been mentioned in the books of the saints of Allah that whoever has missed the intercessory prayers and Witr and then the voluntary prayers — the first obligation is that the stated wage must be used to make up all the obligatory prayers, then to make up the missed fasts. This is what was stipulated, even if the obligatory fasts were not made up by the prayer. Whatever remained of the stated wage after the fasting was not sufficient to cover everything else. It should not be hidden from you that if the deceased is obligated to make up prayers, only one of them is designated first to make up the obligatory prayers, then the intercessory prayers and Witr, then the Sunnah prayers — to the extent that the available sum covers, whether it amounts to half or less or more. Allah, the Exalted, is my guardian against transgression, and acceptance belongs to Him. The Most High said in the aforementioned letter: The second question — I also asked Allah, Exalted be He, about a woman who had children from her first husband and then married another man, by whom she also had a son. Then her other son killed her, and the ruling did not reach her in time. He said: and when the order to execute the ruling was given, there was no one to carry it out, and she died, leaving behind her estate.

<!-- page 97 -->
Her children from the first marriage are entitled, by the argument of Islam, to all the obligatory dues owed to them. Then, regarding her other son who killed her — he later regretted his act and asked what he must do by way of the command that would save him before Allah, the Exalted, from the consequences of his deed, and what good he might do on her behalf, and whether he is required to pay any portion of the blood money to his brothers, including those mentioned above, or not. The answer is: You are required in this case as well to act accordingly, and Allah, the Exalted, is the one who is right in the matter. If his question is confined to what saves him before Allah, the Exalted, from the consequences of his action, then he must address it by untying and dissolving it in the manner known in the books of the saints of Allah, peace be upon them. If he also desires to attain good deeds, he should give charity from some of his wealth in ways that benefit the believers continuously, or through other doors of goodness and righteousness — especially Hajj, about which Mawlana al-Sadiq, Imam Ja'far bin Muhammad, peace and blessings be upon him, was asked by one of his companions. The companion said to him: O son of the Messenger of Allah, I am a man of wealth. I have fulfilled the obligation of Hajj and I have heard that there is reward for volunteering in Hajj. May I instead give an equivalent amount — or more — than the cost of Hajj and receive the reward of Hajj? He looked toward the mountain of Abu Qubays and said to him: If you were to give in charity the weight of this mountain, you would not attain the reward of Hajj as one attains it by performing it, and its reward returns to him through the mercy of Allah, the Exalted, toward him. If she was a believing woman who prayed, then even if she was not praying or was a believer, her prayers should be made up,

<!-- page 98 -->
because prayer is the first thing Allah, the Exalted, examines from the deeds of the son of Adam. If prayer is sound, He looks at the rest of his deeds, and if it is not sound, He does not look at any of his deeds. One should make up her prayers as much as he is able from all the obligatory duties, or from some of them. Whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, and whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it. Then, the Most High — may He be sanctified — speaks to the matter of blood money, which will be mentioned in its proper place, God willing. Our master, Judge al-Nu'man bin Muhammad — may Allah sanctify him and multiply His forgiveness upon him — wrote in a letter to some judges, in response to questions they had asked him about the time of Dhuhr and 'Asr. He stated that when the sun declines, that time has entered, and it is not forbidden to pray 'Asr during the presence of the sun, which is the beginning of the seventh hour. He said in the book of the interpretation of the Sharia: Whoever forgets a prayer should pray it whenever he remembers it. In it he also said: There are five concealed prayer times known only through proof. The time of Dhuhr is when the shadow of everything equals its own length. The time of 'Asr is when the shadow of everything doubles its own length. The time of Maghrib is at sunset. The time of 'Isha' al-Akhirah is at the disappearance of the white twilight. The time of Fajr is from the break of dawn until the sun is about to rise. These are five times for five prayers — the times are hidden and not directly perceived, while the prayers are apparent because they consist of perceptible movements and sensible positions. There are thus ten aspects in every day and night: five outward and five inward. He said in it

<!-- page 99 -->
that prayer has four boundaries, like the boundaries of a house: 1 — time; 2 — the qibla; 3 — wudu'; 4 — intention. He said: the obligatory elements of prayer are seven: 1 — takbir; 2 — recitation; 3 — bowing; 4 — prostration; 5 — praise; 6 — tashahhud; 7 — the sealing. He said: it is not permissible to pray except within the prayer's time. He said: Fajr is a prayer for which its proper performance is known. He said: the Maghrib prayer begins when the sun's disc sets. He said: the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: when the heat is intense, delay the Dhuhr prayer until it cools, for the intensity of heat is from the heat of Hell. He said: Imam al-Mu'izz was asked about the Fajr prayer — whether it belongs to the night prayers or the day prayers. He said: it belongs to neither, because if it were from the night prayers, it would be permissible for the fasting person to eat at its time, and if it were from the day prayers, it would not be recited aloud. The prayers are five prayers from the day's portion and two prayers from the night's portion, and the intermediate prayer between the four is neither from the night's portion nor the day's portion. This is of great wisdom. The Fajr prayer is obligatory as two rak'ahs after the Sunnah, because it is a call following an earlier call, and there is no call after it — therefore there is no prayer after it. Then he said: the night prayers are to be recited aloud. Then he said: every prayer in which recitation is performed aloud belongs to the night prayers and shares in their character. Then he said: the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said regarding the morning prayer:

<!-- page 100 -->
"The angels of the night and the angels of the day are both present." It is stated in the book of the hidden inner matters: the time of the first prayer is seven hours of the day. Then, after a speech he mentioned concerning the inward meaning, the time of 'Asr comes after the time of the first prayer during the day. Then he said: there is no prayer after 'Asr. Then he said: after that comes the 'Isha' prayer, meaning Maghrib. Then he said: then after it comes the darkness prayer. Then he said: the time of the 'Isha' al-Akhirah follows the disappearance of the twilight. Then he said, after further speech: following the night prayer comes the Witr prayer, which consists of three rak'ahs. In the first rak'ah, after Surah al-Hamd, one recites Surah al-A'la — that is, ⟪ar:سَبِّحِ اسْمَ رَبِّكَ الْأَعْلَى الَّذِي خَلَقَ⟫ — and no other surahs of the Qur'an. Then he said: in the second rak'ah, after al-Hamd, one recites Surah al-Kafirun — that is, ⟪ar:قُلْ يَا أَيُّهَا الْكَافِرُونَ⟫. Then he said: in the third rak'ah, after Surah al-Hamd, one recites Surah al-Ikhlas specifically and no other surah — that is, ⟪ar:قُلْ هُوَ اللَّهُ أَحَدٌ⟫. Then he said: in the Witr, the qunut is observed — the worshipper extends his hands after bowing and raising his head, and supplicates while standing in the du'a' al-qunut. Then he said: after these three Witr rak'ahs comes something additional. Two rak'ahs are then prayed while seated. He said: in these two rak'ahs of the shaf' al-fu'd, one recites Surah al-Falaq — ⟪ar:قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ الْفَلَقِ⟫ — in the first rak'ah, and Surah al-Nas — ⟪ar:قُلْ أَعُوذُ بِرَبِّ النَّاسِ⟫ — in the second rak'ah, with these two surahs specifically and no others. Then he said: the Fajr prayer follows. Then he said: it is the middle prayer.

<!-- page 101 -->
About which Allah said: ⟪ar:حَافِظُوا عَلَى الصَّلَوَاتِ وَالصَّلَاةِ الْوُسْطَى وَقُومُوا لِلَّهِ قَانِتِينَ⟫ — "Guard strictly the prayers and the middle prayer, and stand before Allah in devout obedience" — and qunut is performed in the Fajr prayer. Then he said: when it is four rak'ahs, two are nafl and two are obligatory. The author of the hidden epistle — may Allah sanctify his soul — stated in it: Allah has enjoined upon His servants five prayers in the day and night. They total seventeen rak'ahs, and He commanded them to observe the Fajr prayer from the beginning of the morning until sunrise; the first prayer for seven hours of the day, from the sun's departure from the horizon until it nears its decline; the 'Asr prayer for nine hours, which is when the shadow of everything doubles its length; the Maghrib prayer at sunset, when darkness appears; and the 'Isha' al-Akhirah at the disappearance of the twilight and the onset of darkness. These are its outward times, in which Allah, the Exalted, has commanded prayer in two modes of obedience. He said: there is no prayer after 'Asr until the sun sets. He said: there is no difference between the adhan and the iqamah for Maghrib as there is between the call to prayer and the prayer for the other four prayers. In the book of questions — attributed to the late Shaykh Shimon bin Muhammad Ghori al-Hindi, may Allah be pleased with him — and the answer given by our master Badr al-Din Hassan bin Idris: Question — What do you say, O one initiated into the divine secrets, regarding the two rak'ahs of the midnight prayer? Does one pray them after the midpoint of the night when the person rises from sleep, or when he has composed himself? Answer — The time of the end of the night is best. Our master said:


<!-- page 102 -->
Mawlana Hibatullah ibn Ibrahim, in responding to questions from a Mamluk and his most wretched servant Muhammad Ali ibn Saleh al-Badri, answered him from the island of Yemen to the island of Hind in the year 1145 AH on a separate matter. He wrote: O our master, may God perpetuate your eminence — the books of the Sharia state that missed fasts are made up for men by men. However, nothing similar is mentioned regarding prayer. Should women therefore make up prayers on behalf of men, or not? Kindly provide a clarifying answer. And further: if a weak man is unable to make up the prayers he has missed, should someone else make them up on his behalf, or not?

Answer: With the help of Allah and the blessing of His guardianship upon them — peace be upon you. The ruling on making up prayers is like that of fasting: no one makes them up on behalf of a man except the man himself. If he has reached an age at which he is too weak to fulfil his missed prayers, he prays to the extent of his ability, and others may not substitute for him. This applies after he has bowed and acknowledged his incapacity.

**On the Call to Prayer and the Iqama**

In the first book of questions and answers mentioned earlier — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book on the Pillars of Islam — the following is recorded. Question: What do you say about a man employed by the Sultan of al-Jur or his agents as a frontman for the mosque, or as its muezzin, teacher, or in some other capacity, whether through an obligatory assignment or a jizyah arrangement, when the

<!-- page 103 -->
people know that the one who holds the obligation takes from the public funds whatever he sees fit rather than what he is entitled to — is it permissible for such a man to accept the position or not? Answer: If the funds come from the public treasury and are disbursed in accordance with the judgment of those appointed as guardians by God, then it is permissible for him to receive it; otherwise, it is not.

From a summary derived from the words of Ja'far ibn Muhammad ibn Ali, peace be upon him, regarding a man who gives the call to prayer while walking or mounted: he said that when the time of the Tashahhud comes, he should face the qibla, and that is acceptable. He should also unite in what he has learned. And I found in what I have gathered from books attributed to the Ahl al-Bayt — may Allah's prayers be upon them all — that the call to prayer does not divide the highest of what is said while standing.

From the book of abbreviated rulings: when the muezzin says "I bear witness that Muhammad is the Messenger of God" twice, he should say to himself, "I bear witness that Ali is the guardian of God." When he says "come to prosperity" twice, he should say to himself, "The family of Muhammad are the best of human beings." The book also states that it is not permissible for someone who is inside the mosque when the muezzin gives the call to prayer to leave until he has prayed, unless he leaves out of necessity and intends to return. If he knows he will be aware of the prayer upon his return, or if he goes out to perform ablution and endeavours to return in time for the prayer but does not make it, he may pray alone or with another congregation in a different mosque, and there is nothing wrong with that.

It is stated in the book on the Interpretation of the Sharia that it is permissible for the muezzin to give the call to prayer without being in a state of ritual purity, but the iqama may not be given except by one who is ritually pure. He said: face the

<!-- page 104 -->
qibla and make the intention. He said: do not pray the obligatory prayer when the call to prayer has been given and pray

the obligatory prayer when the iqama has been performed. He said: the supererogatory prayer is accompanied by the adhan and the obligatory prayer by the iqama, and the Sunnah precedes it. He said: the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said: whoever is given access enters the paradise of the people of truth.

Our master and Mawlana Idris ibn Sayyidina Hassan stated in his book known as *Uyun al-Akhbar*, in its sixth section on the matter of the call to prayer, that the phrase "I testify that our master is the guardian of God, Muhammad, and the best of human beings, and the best of them" was included in the adhan by the remembrance of Mawlana Amir al-Mu'minin, the Ruler by God's command, may Allah's prayers be upon him and his pure forebears. He mentioned that during the caliphate of the Commander of the Believers, the Ruler by God's command, peace be upon him, the muezzins added to their call — after the testimony that Muhammad is the Messenger of God — the phrase "I testify that our Mawlana Ali is the guardian of God," in response to the Prophet's proclamation on the Day of Ghadir, in which he declared the allegiance owed to himself and the guardianship of his guardian. May Allah bless them and their families. They then added to the adhan the phrase "Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings, and their 'atrah are the finest of perfumes" — a proclamation of the virtue of the Messenger of God, in whose glory they took pride. He was exalted in glory to the highest degree, and none was closer to him than his brother and guardian Ali ibn Abi Talib, his daughter Fatima al-Zahra, and his grandsons al-Hasan and al-Husayn, and the pure Imams whom God chose from among the descendants of His Messenger and made guides for His creation. When Abdullah ibn Abbas was asked about the meaning of "relatives," he replied that they were Fatima, al-Hasan, and al-Husayn. Surely Abdullah ibn Abbas, with all his learning,

<!-- page 105 -->
his knowledge, his kinship, and his close relation to the Prophet, would not have narrated from Abu Bakr ibn Abi Quhafa that he said "we are the 'Atrah of the Messenger of Allah." For my life, Abu Bakr was not among the relatives of the Prophet, peace be upon him — he was merely of the Quraysh, and that is far removed from the lineage of Ali, Fatima, al-Hasan, and al-Husayn, peace be upon them. That distinction belongs first to them, and thereafter to those who accompanied and drew close to them. Al-Kumait said: "Bani Hashim are the kindred of the Prophet Muhammad — to them he showed pleasure many times, and to them he showed displeasure." This indicates that they were his kinsmen. We have already mentioned this, along with what has been narrated from Ibn Abbas, may Allah be pleased with him, on that point.

Many people incorrectly deny that Ali is the best of human beings and argue that while Muhammad is the best of human beings, they cannot accept the same for Ali because there are prophets among mankind. This is the position of those who are ignorant of its basis — an argument born of arrogance that refutes itself in a circle. All Muslims are agreed that the community of Muhammad is the best of communities, for Allah Almighty says: *you were the best community brought forth for mankind* — and among the communities before them were prophets and saints who were preferred. If they say this is by way of metaphor and glorification, we say as they say, and every aggressor is left to his loss. The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said in a rigorously authenticated narration — narrated from Hudhayfah ibn al-Yaman and others — "I am leaving among you that to which if you hold fast you will not go astray: the Book of Allah and the people of my household. They are like the Ark of Noah — whoever boards it is saved, and whoever remains behind is drowned." The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said that the best of mankind — and whoever refuses this is a disbeliever — this is sufficient as a guide for one who has found the path of truth.

<!-- page 106 -->
The community that is the best community brought forth for mankind has been explained by the Imams, may Allah's prayers be upon them, and we have mentioned this in this book. In the narration attributed to Ali ibn al-Husayn al-Qari in Cairo, it is recorded that he said: I was with Imam al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah, the Commander of the Believers, may God's prayers be upon him, near a mosque known as al-Qada'i, and he was standing at it when he heard a great uproar and commotion. He said to some of the riders, "Go ahead and see what this clamour is, then report back to me." The young man went and returned saying, "O Mawlana, they are the people of Atfih, and they have split into two groups." He said, "Bring them to me." The young man went and brought them, and among them was a lion that had seized hold of a man and was holding him tightly. He — may Allah's prayers be upon him — commanded them to release the man, and he was released. The Commander of the Believers then said to the lion,

"Who are you?" He said, "I am the preacher with a good word." He said to him, "And who is the other man?" He said, "The caller." He said, "What has happened between you?" He said, "O Mawlana, something appeared in our land

<!-- page 107 -->
that we had never heard before." He said, "And what is it?" He said, "In the adhan: 'Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings, and their 'atrah are the finest of perfumes.' We had never heard this adhan before, and it may be permissible that Muhammad, peace be upon him and his family, is the best of human beings — but it cannot be permissible to say the same of Ali, because among human beings there are prophets: Adam, Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus." Mawlana said — may God's prayers be upon him — "I will show you, O Sheikh, that peace be upon him" — meaning the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him — and said: "Have you read any of the hadith?" He said, "Yes, O Commander of the Faithful."

He said: "Present a sound hadith, and if it is sound I will accept it, and if it is false, refute it until I bring you another." He said: "Yes, Mawlana, may Allah's prayers be upon you." He said: narrated to us Ibn Khallad from Sajja ibn Suwaid, narrated from al-Ma'mun al-Abbasi from his father al-Rashid, from his father al-Mahdi, from his father al-Mansur, narrated from Abdullah ibn Abbas, who said: We were gathered one day with the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, as he spoke to us and instructed us in our religion, when his daughter Fatima al-Zahra, may God's prayers be upon her, came to him with tears flooding her eyes. The Prophet said, "O my daughter, what makes you weep?" She said, "O father, I have lost my sons al-Hasan and al-Husayn, and I feared my heart would break from their loss, O father." The Prophet then turned toward the heavens, raised his hands, and prayed, whereupon Gabriel descended — the trustworthy — and said, "O Muhammad, peace be upon you. Do not be anxious — peace, peace. God Most High sends you His greetings and says to you: your good sons al-Hasan

<!-- page 108 -->
and al-Husayn went to the courtyard of Bani al-Najjar, and they played there until they fell asleep. An angel from among the angels was sent to them — a compassionate, tender companion — who covered them with his right wing and shielded them with his left." He recognized them and kept watch over them. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, set out with his family and companions to the courtyard of Bani al-Najjar, and he saw al-Hasan and al-Husayn, may God's prayers be upon them, sleeping while the angel guarded them by the command of God Almighty. The Prophet carried them on his shoulder, and Abu Bakr said, "Allow me to carry them, O Messenger of Allah."

He said, "Their mount is the finest of mounts, and the finest of riders are they." So the Prophet carried them on his shoulders and hastened to bring the glad tidings to Fatima, peace be upon her, until he reached the mosque and encountered a gathering of people. They were startled by what they saw, and he said, "O people, shall I tell you who are the best of people in terms of father and mother? They are these two — al-Hasan and al-Husayn. Their father is Ali, the best of guardians, and their mother is Fatima, my daughter, the best of the women of the world. Shall I tell you who are the best of people in terms of grandfather and grandmother? These two, al-Hasan and al-Husayn — their grandfather is the Prophet of God, and their grandmother is Khadija, the first to believe in Allah and His Messenger. Shall I not tell you who are the best of people?" The Prophet, may Allah's prayers be upon him, said: "These two — al-Hasan and al-Husayn — their uncle is Ja'far ibn Abi Talib and their aunt is Umm Hani bint Abi Talib. I have not turned away from God for even the blink of an eye without telling you who are the best of people in terms of uncle and aunt — these two, al-Hasan and al-Husayn. Their paternal uncle is Qasim, the son of the Messenger of God, and their paternal aunt is Zaynab, the daughter of the Prophet of God." He then said, "O God, You know that they — along with their father, their mother, their grandfather, their grandmother, their uncle, their aunt, their paternal uncle, their paternal aunt,

<!-- page 109 -->
and their Shi'a and their lovers — are in Paradise." He then asked, "Is this hadith sound?" The Sheikh replied, "By Allah, O Commander of the Faithful, it is." The Commander of the Believers said: "And in the hadith, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: 'My sons are safe in their companions — these are the true Imams, whether they rise or sit — and their father is better than them.'" He said, "Yes." And the Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, said: "My sons — these are the masters of the youth of the people of Paradise."

Is that news true, O Sheikh, or not? He said, "It is true, O Commander of the Faithful." Is it true in the reports that the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said that the people of Paradise are young and there is no old age among them? He said, "Yes, that is true." He said, "Yes, O Commander of the Faithful." He then said to him, "O Sheikh, will Adam, Noah, Abraham, Moses, and Jesus be in Paradise or not?" He said, "Yes, O Commander of the Faithful." He said, "O Sheikh, if al-Hasan and al-Husayn are the masters of the youth of the people of Paradise, and their father is better than them, it has thereby been established that the best of human beings — alongside the Prophet — is ali." The Sheikh said, "Then I believed and I was convinced." Mawlana al-Hakim then turned, the Commander of the Faithful — may God's prayers be upon him — and the da'i said, "O Sheikh, then do as you wish — no one will oppose you."

He mentioned and commanded the Commander of the Believers Ali, peace be upon him, and commanded the call to prayer accordingly. We present herein a complete message addressed to our master, Mawlana, and father Muhammad ibn Fahd — may God be pleased with him — in Jerusalem. It is the message designated by the expression *On the Discussion*, which contains a lesson for those who reflect on the question of whether the phrase "Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings, and their 'atrah are the finest of perfumes" may be said in the adhan — among those who hold that this phrase is not permissible under the laws of the true religion, and that it is not among what has been narrated in transmitted reports — and it has been shown by those who maintain that this is something the holy book has commanded and for which authority rests with those who command it.


<!-- page 110 -->
In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful — and I seek His help.

Praise be to God, Lord of all the worlds, and may God bless His messenger, our master Muhammad, and his family. All praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds; blessings upon the best of His messengers, Muhammad al-Amin, and upon his guardians — the most honourable of guardians — and upon all those who are of his household, the righteous and the pure. Peace be upon them all.

A discussion took place between two jurists, and we are obliged to quote from it the words of the questioner — who was himself a jurist of knowledge — along with the response of the other, so that we may know which of them stood upon the truth and which of them departed from it. The reasonable conclusion is that one of the two jurists held the correct position.

One of the two jurists said to the other: "How is it that the people of your school say in the call to prayer, 'Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings, and their fragrance is the finest of perfumes'? No other sect of Islam says this." The other jurist — the one responsible for defending that position — replied by appealing to a verse of God Almighty, His saying that whoever does good does so for himself, and whoever does wrong bears that himself. "If we say in the call to prayer, 'Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings, and their blessing is the finest of perfumes,' this falls under good deeds done for ourselves — even if it were otherwise, and even if it were something blameworthy, it would return upon us alone — especially given that God Almighty has said: 'No soul shall bear the burden of another.'"

The questioner then said to him: "I accept what you have cited from these two verses, and I accept those who hold that view — but I ask you for a clear and decisive argument,

<!-- page 111 -->
a proof. What you have mentioned is that Muhammad — peace and blessings of God be upon him — and Ali are the best of human beings, and that you liken Ali to Muhammad in this regard. Yet what you have grown accustomed to saying in the call to prayer is something else entirely, because it is well established that Muhammad, may God bless him and grant him peace, is the best of human beings — as is confirmed by his being the master of all the messengers. But as for Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, it is not established that he equals the Prophet in this distinction until you prove it to me with clear evidence."

The said respondent jurist answered him, saying: "You have posed a question, and I shall address it with illuminating proofs, to the extent of my ability and capacity — and above all stands the All-Knowing.

**1 — The first proof:** We say, with the help and success of God, that when the word of God — 'God only wishes to remove all impurity from you, O people of the household, and to purify you completely' — established for the Ahl al-Bayt an equality in purity shared with the Prophet, and since the best of the Ahl al-Bayt after him is Ali ibn Abi Talib, the Commander of the Believers, may God's blessings be upon him — who is his guardian, his trustee, may God bless him and his family, and his successor in his community — it follows that what is proven for him in this regard makes him the best of human beings alongside him, and their fragrance the finest of perfumes, for they share with him in purity. This is one of the clear and unambiguous matters, as it comes from the saying of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood cannot come from before it or from behind it — a revelation from the All-Wise, the All-Praiseworthy.

<!-- page 112 -->
**2 — The second proof:** We say, with the help and success of God, that when the word of God Almighty in His clear Book — 'Peace be upon the family of Yasin; thus do We reward those who do good' — establishes that the family of Yasin, according to God Almighty, are the family of the Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, the virtuous ones who are his household, and since the first and best of them was the Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him, who chose him and his family, and since Ali ibn Abi Talib was among them — it is therefore established that he is not the best of that family, whom God has blessed and described as those who do good, except one who is himself the best of human beings. Likewise, his successor from the Ahl al-Bayt, who are the family of Yasin, is among the best of human beings, and the foremost among them. This is one of the clear and unambiguous matters, from the saying of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood cannot come from before it or from behind it — a revelation from the All-Wise, the All-Gracious.

**3 — The third proof:** We say, with the help and success of God Almighty, that when the word of God Almighty in this noble verse — 'You are the best community ever brought forth for humanity: you enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong and believe in God' — is addressed to the people of the household of the Messenger of God, may God be pleased with him, telling them, 'You are the best community ever brought forth for humanity,' they are positively affirmed in this, and there is no alteration to what is — for they are the best community ever brought forth

<!-- page 113 -->
for humanity. The best and foremost among them was the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, according to what has been established in the previous two proofs. It is therefore true and proven, and then further confirmed as true and proven, that he is the best of human beings after the Prophet — and alongside him the Prophet, peace and blessings of God be upon him. This is one of the clear and unambiguous matters, from the saying of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood cannot come from before it or from behind it — a revelation from the All-Gracious, the All-Wise.

**4 — The fourth proof:** We say, with the help and success of God Almighty, that since God did not withhold from His Holy Book the request of His prophet Moses, peace be upon him — who said, 'And appoint for me a minister from my family, Aaron, my brother; strengthen my back through him and let him share in my affair, so that we may glorify You much and remember You much; indeed, You have ever been watching over us' — and the answer from God Almighty was: 'Your request has been granted, O Moses'; this makes it clear that Moses, peace be upon him, asked God Almighty to make his brother Aaron a minister through whom his strength would be reinforced and his affairs shared, as indicated by the purpose of his saying, 'so that we may glorify You much and remember You much — indeed, You have ever been watching over us.' Then the word of God Almighty came in another place in the noble Book, relating the words of Moses, peace be upon him: 'And my brother Aaron is more eloquent than I in speech, so send him with me as a helper to confirm me, for I fear that they will call me a liar.' God's response to that was: 'We will strengthen your arm through your brother, and We will grant you both authority, so that they cannot reach you. With Our signs, you and those who follow you shall be the victors.'

Then to the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him, in another verse of His Holy Book: 'Those are the ones God has guided, so follow their guidance.' And in another verse: 'Nothing is said to you except what was said to the messengers before you.' And in another verse: 'He has ordained for you of religion

<!-- page 114 -->
what He enjoined upon Noah and what We have revealed to you, and what We enjoined upon Abraham and Moses and Jesus — to establish the religion and not to be divided in it.' It is therefore obligatory upon the Prophet to appoint the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, as his guardian, his minister, his brother, and the one through whom his strength is reinforced and his affairs are shared, and that God Almighty has strengthened him and granted him and for him the authority mentioned — especially given that God Almighty said to him in a place in His Holy Book: 'You will never find any change in the way of God,' and His saying in another place: 'You will never find any alteration in the way of God.'

Since this is true, and it has been proven that Ali, peace be upon him, is the minister of the Messenger of God, his brother, and his partner in his affairs and authority which God established for them — this is further confirmed by what has been narrated about him from what he said: "You are to me as Aaron was to Moses, except that there is no prophet after me." When this is so, it is true and proven, and then further confirmed as true and proven, by these noble verses of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood cannot come from before it or from behind it — a revelation from the All-Gracious, the All-Wise — which says in it: 'This Quran guides to that which is most upright.' It is therefore firmly established that the said Prophet's affirmation that Ali, the Commander of the Believers, is among the best of human beings after the Prophet, given that God has associated him with him in his command and authority, is one of the clear and unambiguous matters, from the saying of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood does not come from before it nor from behind it — a revelation from the All-Wise, the All-Praiseworthy.

<!-- page 115 -->
**5 — The fifth proof:** We say, with the help of God Almighty, that when God Almighty said in His noble Book: 'Your guardian is only God and His Messenger and those who believe — those who establish prayer and give zakah while bowing in worship' — and: 'Whoever takes God and His Messenger and those who believe as guardians — then the party of God, they are the victors' — it is firmly established that those whom God Almighty has named in this verse as those who believe, and whom He has described as establishing prayer and giving zakah, are the victorious party of God, who are among the people of virtue of whom God has spoken. He also said of them: 'The bounty is in the hand of God; He gives it to whomever He wills, and God is of great bounty.' No human being was more deserving or more foremost in this virtue than the family of the Messenger of God — of whom God said that He wishes to remove all impurity from them, the people of the household, and to purify them completely, for His will cannot be turned back by any created being. God also said of them: 'You are the best community ever brought forth for humanity; you enjoin what is right and forbid what is wrong and believe in God.' And God also said of them: 'Peace be upon the family of Yasin; thus do We reward those who do good.' The first and best of them was the one whom the Prophet made his brother, his guardian, his minister, and his successor after him in his community. It is therefore right and proven, and then further confirmed as right and proven, that Ali ibn Abi Talib is the guardian after the Prophet — as specified in the mentioned verse — and his partner in guardianship over the believers. How, then, could he be anything other than the best of human beings after him, given his partnership in authority? This is one of the clear and unambiguous matters, from the saying

<!-- page 116 -->
of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood cannot come from before it or from behind it — a revelation from the All-Wise, the All-Praiseworthy — especially after the clarification given by the Messenger of God on the day of Eid Ghadir, which is the day of the declaration that God made him the guardian of the believers during his lifetime, through his saying: "Whoever I was his master, this Ali is his master. O God, support whoever supports him, and oppose whoever opposes him, and grant victory to whoever grants him victory, and abandon whoever abandons him, and turn the truth with him wherever he turns." This is among the verses and arguments we have mentioned, and it is sufficient for whoever has a heart, or who listens while being present as a witness.

**6 — The sixth proof:** We say, with the help of God Almighty, that when God Almighty said in this noble verse of His Holy Book: 'Those who believe and do righteous deeds — they are the best of all creation' — it is established that the people of the Prophet's household are the best of the righteous, for they are the best of those who believe in God and do righteous deeds. And God's saying of them, 'You are the best community ever brought forth for humanity,' is also established. It is likewise known that Ali, the Commander of the Believers, is the best of the Ahl al-Bayt. It is therefore right and proven that we say in the call to prayer that Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings, and their fragrance the finest of perfumes — a truth that is beyond doubt, as it comes from the saying of the glorious Quran, to which falsehood cannot come from before it or from behind it — a revelation from the All-Gracious, the All-Wise.

**7 — The seventh proof:** We say, with the help and success of God Almighty, that when the word of God came in His Holy Book saying: 'God chose Adam and Noah and the family of Abraham

<!-- page 117 -->
and the family of 'Imran above all the worlds — descendants of one another, and God is All-Hearing, All-Knowing' — He made clear that God chose the family of Ibrahim and the family of 'Imran just as He chose Adam and Noah, singling them out for the message and prophethood. Since it is known that the Messenger of God is better than Adam and Noah, it follows as true that his family is better than the family of Ibrahim and the family of 'Imran, peace be upon them all. When this is the case, it is true that our master the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, being the best of the Prophet's family, is true and proven — and then further confirmed as true and proven — that our saying in the call to prayer, 'Muhammad and Ali are the best of human beings and their fragrance is the finest of perfumes,' has its authenticity and proof testified to by the verses of the glorious Quran, to which al-batil la ya'tihi min bayni yadayhi wa la min khalfihi tanzilun min hakimin hamid.

**8 — The eighth proof:** We say, with the help and success of God Almighty, that when the word of God Almighty came in this noble verse — 'And We saved the Children of Israel from the humiliating torment; from Pharaoh, who was indeed high-handed, one of the transgressors. And We chose them knowingly above the worlds' — He made clear that God preferred the Children of Israel in their era above the worlds, choosing them over all others, the worlds here meaning all of humanity. The family of the Prophet were the best of human beings in their era, and indeed surpassing even those who came before them in earlier eras, for it is established that the Messenger is the master of all the messengers and prophets — the most honoured and the best of them all. When this is the case, it is true and proven, and then further confirmed as true and proven, that his family — who are his household — are better than the family of every prophet and more honoured than


<!-- page 118 -->
Ali is the best of the Messenger of God's family, the most honored among them, and this ruling represents the true judgment, as attested by the verses of the Holy Book cited here, as well as others not quoted, all of which bear witness to what this noble verse declares — verses of the glorious Qur'an that falsehood cannot touch from any direction, nor any revelation from behind it, from the Wise and Benevolent. Having completed our presentation of these eight answers in their stated order, along with the proofs drawn from the truest of sayings — those that illuminate the heart and dispel doubt from the hearts of believers — let us conclude this treatise, which we have filled with praise of God, Lord of the worlds, and with prayers upon our master Muhammad, master of the messengers, and upon his pure family until the Day of Judgment. Peace be upon them all. God is sufficient for us, and He is the best trustee.

In the name of God, the Most Gracious, the Most Merciful.

On this matter, some objectors to the honorable school of thought have fallen into delusion, claiming that what Omar ibn al-Khattab omitted from the phrase "live on well" renders the call to prayer unacceptable, since no such narration exists in their books. Furthermore, after the concession, it is not considered ugly or wrong, for it represents sound ijtihad — even if it were

<!-- page 119 -->
deemed an error on his part. Similarly, some claim that adding the phrase "I testify that our master Ali is the wali of God" is likewise wrong, on the grounds that any addition to the call to prayer is equivalent to any omission from it.

We say: this is a delusion and a confusion of illusions, as we will explain and clarify in what follows, with the help and success of God. Even granting that increasing or decreasing the call to prayer constitutes an error by way of obligation, we do not accept that the addition and the omission are equal in degree of error. Saying they are equivalent is objectionable and misleading for several reasons:

The first is this: consider the ratio of what was omitted from the call to prayer relative to its followers compared to the addition — it is as one to a hundred thousand or less. The omission thus produced enormous loss and created widespread error, making it a far greater wrong than the addition.

The second is that this addition is attributed to our master Imam al-Hakim bi Amr Allah, may God's prayers be upon him. If that is the case, then the one who was previously in error and misguidance led the community astray for more than four hundred years, since this phrase was not introduced until after the fourth century. One who is in error from an earlier time bears a far greater degree of wrong, and the two cases are not equal.

The third is that if the omission were merely equivalent to the omission, they would share one degree; but beyond the omission, it added — in his view — a sentence that is objectionable.

<!-- page 120 -->
He presents it by saying "prayer is better than sleep" — glory be to God, where is prayer and where is sleep, that one would liken them in virtue and inferiority as someone saying "this sword is better than wood," not knowing whether this is praise or blame, for it contains two errors. The addition to the call to prayer, by contrast, contains only one error, and the two are not equal.

The fourth is that he applied his own ijtihad yet did not command the abrogation of the Prophet's saying at the five prayer times, attaching his phrase only once, in the morning prayer. How many objectionable aspects this sentence contains we have left aside for fear of undue length.

As for attributing this addition to our master al-Hakim bi Amr Allah, may God's prayers be upon him — given that the twelve Shi'a, despite differing with him in school of thought, use this phrase in the call to prayer — this is plainly invalid and a baseless claim, nothing but confusion and delusion. As for his statement that attributing this omission to Omar ibn al-Khattab is unacceptable because it does not appear in their books: this is not a sound argument, because the books attributed to authenticity, such as Sahih al-Bukhari and the rest of the five and the complaint, are themselves disputed by their own people in terms of their soundness. The scholars of truth have sufficient grounds to reject and dismiss the hadiths contained in them and to deny them as evidence. The narration transmitted by our master Qadi al-Nu'man from our master

<!-- page 121 -->
Ja'far al-Sadiq predates the composition of those books, and their scholars have been able to see it and enter into debate against it — yet they suppressed and erased it out of fear of appealing to the people of truth. This is how their practice has proceeded. This, then, is the response concerning the addition to the call to prayer.

As for the origin of attributing this addition, the answer is that the phrase of testimony to guardianship was uttered by the Commander of the Faithful, Ali ibn Abi Talib, for himself, when the matter of guardianship came to him after the Prophet — just as the Prophet testified for himself regarding his prophecy and message, and just as God Almighty testified for Himself that "Allah bears witness that there is no god but He" — and this came after the verse of guardianship was revealed, stating "But Allah and His Messenger are your guardians," and so on. They differed regarding the revelation of the guardianship, some denying it, yet nonetheless they are charged with the testimony revealed concerning the Commander of the Faithful, may God's prayers be upon him. In this is sufficient guidance for the one who seeks it; as for the arrogant, they have nothing but seduction. Every vessel flows with what it contains, and God guides whom He wills to a straight path. Done.

Written by the pure and loyal Diaa Ali ibn Ibrahim al-Haydar Abadi, may God forgive him his sins and conceal the flaws in what he has written, and may what is right in it be attributed to the honorable loyalists, on the twenty-second of Jumada al-Awwal, 1306 AH, in the town of Baruda, under the quiet call.

---

**Mention of Mosques**

In the first question-and-answer book mentioned, arranged according to the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*, the following is recorded. Question: What do you say regarding what has come in the Shari'a about the prohibition of praying between graves and the dislike of building a mosque

<!-- page 122 -->
near graves, given that the Messenger of God said: "The earth has been made a mosque and a means of purification for me, except for the pigeons, the cemeteries, and the graveyard"? Explain the details of this to us. Answer: Based on what has been stated, one does not pray in any of these places, nor should a mosque be built near graves.

Question: What do you say about a permissible mosque situated above a house or a shop — may its owners remove and demolish it or not? Answer: If a person owns it alone and has not made it permissible for others, he may demolish it. If he has made it permissible for others, he may not demolish it. If it is demolished, there is nothing against him.

Question: What do you say about a man who wishes to build a mosque and other structures on his own property — must he seek permission from the governor for that or not? Answer: He may do as he wishes and need not seek permission.

Question: You state that in the Book of Purity it is written that there is no prayer for the neighbor of the mosque except in the mosque — meaning no prayer is as virtuous as that of one who prays in the mosque — and it also states that a person's prayer alone in his house counts as one prayer. So what distinguishes a neighbor of the mosque from others, if a person's prayer in his house is one prayer? Answer: The neighbor of the mosque is one who can hear the call to prayer, and a solitary prayer for such a person counts as one prayer in congregation. It is disliked for the neighbor of the mosque to pray alone in his house without an excuse.

Question: What do you say about what is stated in the Book of Purity regarding the Prophet's Mosque, may peace be upon him and his family — when he stood to establish it, they said, "O Prophet, if God were to strengthen him, he would say: no bower like the bower of Moses." What is meant by "no

<!-- page 123 -->
bower like the bower of Moses"? Answer: This means it is not like the bower of Moses, which was firm and solid.

Question: What do you say about a man who hears the call to prayer in the mosque while praying, with another person also present who listens — and then prays in his house with another person? Have they missed the merit of the congregational prayer or not? Answer: Praying in the mosque is better, as the Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, said: "There is no prayer for the neighbor of the mosque except in the mosque."

Question: What do you say about a mosque in which the imam leads the noon prayer, while another imam leads the people in prayer on the roof — is that prayer valid, and should the greeting be offered by those below to those above and vice versa? Answer: There is no objection to greeting from above toward those praying below.

Question: What do you say about a place where there are several mosques, and a man leaves his house at the time of prayer — may he bypass the nearest mosque and go to one farther away because the farther one is of greater rank? Or is that forbidden? Answer: Yes, it is established that this is permissible, and there is nothing wrong with one who does it.

Question: What does our master say about a mosque that a person establishes in his house without making it open to people for God's sake — may he change its location or expand it into the house, and must the greeting prayer be prayed in it or not? Answer: If the mosque has been made open to people, the greeting prayer is to be prayed in it. If it is confined to the house and not open to others, then the greeting prayer is not prayed in it, and he may modify it as he wishes unless he has made it open.

Question: What does our master say about a man who arrives at the mosque at the time of dawn while the imam is leading

<!-- page 124 -->
the obligatory prayer in congregation, and the latecomer can still pray the Sunnah and join the prayer — should he pray the greeting and the Sunnah first, or should he enter the congregation? Answer: If he can join the prayer before the imam rises from bowing, he should pray whatever he wishes of the Sunnah before the imam raises his head from bowing.

Question: What does our master say about a man who enters the mosque before the prayer concludes in order to make up an obligatory prayer he missed — should he pray the greeting prayer or not? Answer: He should not pray a Sunnah when he has an obligatory prayer to make up, except the Sunnah of Fajr, which is prayed alongside the obligatory prayer. The greeting is not prayed except at times when it is permissible to pray Sunnah prayers.

Question: What does our master say about one who arrives while the imam is leading the obligatory Fajr prayer and joins it — should he make up the greeting or the Sunnah prayer after it concludes? Answer: If he prays something before the greeting and the time passes, he may make up the Sunnah prayer after the obligatory Fajr prayer, unless the sun has risen, or after midday, or at night.

Question: What does our master say about an imam who prays the congregational prayer in a mosque and then another imam comes — should he lead that same prayer again in congregation in that mosque or not? Answer: Two imams should not lead the same prayer simultaneously in the same gathering, unless the first imam has vacated his place and another has been appointed in his stead.

Question: If the imam leads the people in funeral prayers in the mosque, how should they offer the greeting? Answer: If they entered the mosque at a time when optional prayers are permissible, they pray the greeting first. Whoever prays in open ground and finds nothing to use as a sutrah should draw a line.

<!-- page 125 -->
He should pray and not leave his sandals facing the qiblah to serve as a sutrah. Whoever prays the intercession prayer or the Sunnah of noon in his house and then enters the mosque should begin with the greeting of the mosque if he enters it at noon or when dawn breaks. Whoever builds a small mosque for the people around him — once it is permissible to pray in it — that is valid. If it was built after a grave, it is not permissible and it is disliked for the qiblah of the mosque to face toward a bathroom, graves, or a latrine. Whoever does not know the qiblah direction should investigate and then pray; if he errs and becomes aware of his error while still in the prayer, and the time is short, he should turn toward the correct direction and complete his prayer — if the correct direction lies between east and west, even though he was following the Ka'ba, he is only required to repeat it. If a man is uncertain between two directions and considers the qiblah equally likely to lie in either, he may pray in both directions, as is similarly stated regarding the four sides. Whoever is traveling and the prayer time arrives while he is on a path and finds no water, yet there is a mosque along the way, he should go to the mosque and pray there. If he prays in congregation in the mosque and the imam leaves and the congregation disperses, and a second imam arrives with the congregation, he prays alone. The imamate should not be held in one mosque more than once. Whoever enters the mosque, prays the greeting of the mosque, goes out for a need, and then returns to the mosque should pray the greeting of the mosque again. One should not pray facing camels in the direction of the qiblah, though cows, sheep, and the like are permissible. Whoever prays the intercession prayer in his house and then enters the mosque does not

<!-- page 121 -->


<!-- page 126 -->
It is permissible to greet the mosque until dawn breaks, at which point one begins with the mosque greeting, then prays Fajr. If he enters the mosque, performs the mosque greeting, and the time for the witr prayer has returned, he repeats the witr, since no prayer may intervene between the witr and dawn. Whoever prays the obligatory prayer in the mosque, and another arrives and places his sword facing the qibla — where a barrier prevents him from moving away from the prayer — it is still permissible for him to pray, though praying between two rows is disliked unless no other spot is available. If another place within the mosque is free, even if finding it requires effort, it is permissible to pray there. If land is jointly owned and a mosque is built on it, prayer therein is permissible, just as it is permissible when one owner builds a mosque on his own portion and the other builds one on his. From the book summarizing the work: it is disliked for the qibla of a mosque to face a bathroom, graves, or a crowd. Whoever does not know the direction of the qibla due to an obstruction should investigate, then pray — and if he discovers his error while still in prayer, he should, in a brief movement, turn toward the correct direction and complete his prayer, provided he is between east and west; if the deviation was deliberate, it is permissible to repeat it. If a man is uncertain between two directions and neither dominates his thinking as the qibla, he may pray facing either of the two directions — and the same applies across all four directions. From the book Ta'eefil al-Da'a'im: regarding the saying about weapons, bringing weapons into mosques is not permissible except for the mosque imam on Friday. It is mentioned in the book of clarification that Abu Abd Allah (peace be upon him) disliked a mosque being built inside a house with a closed door. He said in

<!-- page 127 -->
the councils of clarification that prayer is disliked in twelve places: 1 – a cemetery; 2 – a slaughterhouse; 3 – a pigeon loft; 4 – a place of weeding; 5 – camel shelters; 6 – praying facing a sleeper; 7 – praying without a garment; 8 – facing unclean wet earth; 9 – on top of food; 10 – prayer in a place of commerce; 11 – churches; 12 – the houses of the Magi, unless they have been sprinkled with water.

Our master Mawlana and our father Muhammad ibn al-Fahd — may God sanctify him and grant us his intercession and forgiveness — said in his noble epistle, which he titled "The Lover of the Path of Guidance for Converts and the Revealer of Their Hearts in Matters of Jurisprudence": Question Nineteen — I asked, may God Almighty grant you His pleasure: what is the ruling on praying in a domed structure or a mosque in which a grave is located on any of its sides? Answer: With the help of God Almighty, the correct and proper position on this matter is that both the domed structure and the mosque in question — whichever was built first — are permissible places of prayer. If the grave was placed there by the one who built the structure, or if the grave was there before the structure was built, prayer therein is permissible in both its obligatory and voluntary forms; and whichever of the two was built later, the ruling is likewise that prayer in it alongside the grave is permissible. He also said — may God sanctify him — in the aforementioned noble epistle, regarding the matter of greeting the mosque built to seek the pleasure of its owner: Question Twenty-one — I also asked, seeking God Almighty's pleasure, about the prayer of greeting the mosque:

<!-- page 128 -->
Is it only obligatory inside the mosque and not obligatory in its courtyard or immediate surroundings at all? And if he enters the mosque and performs it there, then goes out to the courtyard and returns to the mosque, must he repeat it or not? Answer: With the help of God Almighty, the correct and proper position is that this greeting is obligatory only inside the mosque itself — not in its courtyard or immediate surroundings — though if one performs it in those areas it is commendable, since they are considered part of the mosque and its precincts. If he goes out from the mosque and then returns, as I have explained, he need not repeat it, since he has not yet left its precincts.

It is stated in the book of interpretation of the Shari'ah, narrated from the Prophet — may God bless him and his family — that he said the ḥudūd punishments must not be carried out in mosques; and whoever heard someone singing astray in a mosque, he told them, "I did not find it." He said: The Prophet — peace be upon him and his family — said it is permissible to pray in sheep pens.

The owner of the councils of al-Mustansiriyya — peace be upon its owner — said in the eighth assembly: Listen now to what we are about to tell you: prayer has seven different levels. They are: 1 – The prayer of the worshipper in his house, counting as one prayer; 2 – Prayer in the market mosque, counting as twelve prayers; 3 – The prayer of the worshipper in the mosque of the tribe, counting as twenty-five prayers; 4 – The prayer of the worshipper in the congregational mosque, counting as one hundred prayers; 5 – The prayer of the worshipper in al-Aqsa Mosque; 6 – The prayer of the worshipper in the Mosque of the Messenger of God — peace be upon him — counting as ten thousand prayers;

<!-- page 129 -->
7 – The prayer of the worshipper in the Sacred House of God, counting as one hundred thousand prayers. Each of these levels carries deep meanings and words of wisdom.

Our master Amīr al-Mu'minīn Ali ibn Abi Talib — peace be upon him — said in his four hundred precepts of conduct: Sitting in the mosque from dawn until sunrise is a swifter means of seeking provision than striking the earth in pursuit of it. He said: A Muslim must not spit toward the qibla; if he forgets, let him seek forgiveness. He should not blow toward the place of his prostration, nor into his food, nor into his incense. He said: None of you should pray with a sword directed at the qibla while it is safe to do otherwise. He said: Whoever eats something that harms him should not approach the mosque.

**Mention of the Imamate**

In the first question-and-answer book mentioned, which is arranged in the order of the chapters of the book "The Pillars of Islam," it is asked: What is your view regarding an imam who prays with people in the corner of the qibla while they stand behind him in the public mosque — is the imam's prayer in the arch disliked when his prostration does not occur within it? Answer: There is nothing wrong with praying and prostrating in it. Question: What do you say regarding a traveler who is residing somewhere and praying the four-rak'ah prayer, then prays two rak'ahs with an imam — should the traveler give the salām and depart after the second rak'ah, or should he remain seated until he finishes praying with the imam when the imam gives the salām at the end of the last rak'ah? Answer: It is preferable for him to remain standing until he prays with the imam. Question: What do you say

<!-- page 130 -->
regarding what is established in the Shari'ah about the prohibition of praying behind one who is muqayyad — O our master, what is meant here by the restriction and the one who is restricted? Answer: The muqayyad is one upon whom a qayd has been placed. Question: What do you say about what is stated in Shari'ah — that the muqayyad does not lead the divorced ones in prayer — O our master, does this restriction refer to all types of restraints and bonds placed upon him, or is it specifically those on his legs? Answer: What is meant is restraints and bonds placed on the legs and similar limbs. Question: What do you say about what is stated in Shari'ah — that it is permissible to pray behind a slave if he is a jurist, and that one who is not a jurist may be excused from following him in order to reunite with his family — O our master, what is meant by "those below him in rank" here: people like him who are slaves, or all those who are lower than him in standing? Answer: It includes anyone below him in rank, even if they are free. Question: What do you say about a person who prays with people and, among them are some common people, so he omits the phrase "Rabbanā laka al-ḥamd" out of taqiyya — does this constitute a deficiency in his prayer or not? Answer: There is nothing wrong with taqiyya, but he must not omit it except out of taqiyya; rather, he should recite it as the pure guardians of God did. Question: What do you say about a long, wide ship in which the imam leads people in the Dhuhr prayer in one location, and then another imam leads that same prayer in a different location on the ship — is that permissible? And does the same apply in a large house? Answer: If there is a barrier between the two groups, there is nothing wrong with it. If two imams find themselves in the same place, the later one must be of higher standing; and what is meant by "the same place" here is that

<!-- page 131 -->
the imam leads the noon prayer with people in a house or elsewhere, and then someone who outranks him — from among the people of distinction — leads that same prayer with another group in that same house. There is no objection to that. Question: What do you say about an imam who prays with people on a ship while seated, either because the ship is moving or due to lack of space or some other reason — is it permissible for him to do so or not? Answer: There is nothing wrong with it when there is genuine necessity; but if there is no necessity, it is not permitted. Question: What do you say about an imam who prays with people, and then someone taller arrives, so all the believers move and stand to his right while he stands to their left, and they pray — is their prayer valid? Answer: They may pray to his right or to the north, and there is no difficulty in that; their prayers are valid. Question: What do you say about a man who leads people in the obligatory Maghrib prayer, and the people of al-Ẓāhir also gather with him — how should he recite Bismi Llāhi r-Raḥmāni r-Raḥīm? Is he permitted to recite it aloud? Answer: He must recite Bismi Llāhi r-Raḥmāni r-Raḥīm aloud; however, he may lower it to where only he himself hears it, for the sake of taqiyya. Question: What do you say about a person who leads people in prayer yet cannot pronounce certain letters correctly because he has lost his teeth or does not know the correct articulation points — is it permissible for the prayer to proceed with him leading or not? Answer: If the mispronunciation changes the meaning, it invalidates the prayer. Question: What do you say about a person who leads people in prayer and then needs to relieve himself during the prayer — is it permissible for him to designate someone he deems fit to complete the prayer in his place, even if that person has not been divorced from the prayer? Is it permissible for that person to continue, or is the prayer invalidated? Answer: If it is unconditional, there is nothing wrong with it,

<!-- page 132 -->
and he may offer the leadership to whomever he wishes. Question: What does our master say about a mosque in which the imam leads people in the noon prayer, and after they depart, another man comes and leads a different group in that same prayer — and they were unaware of this at the time, but later found out — must they be instructed to repeat the prayer or not? Answer: The prayer has been performed and need not be repeated, whether they knew of it or not. Let them be informed, and let them be grateful. Question: What do you say about a resident praying with travelers, and a traveler praying with residents? Answer: If the traveler is superior to the residents and he gives the salām after two rak'ahs, then they rise and complete the prayer. If the resident is superior to the travelers, he leads them and they depart after two rak'ahs. Question: Regarding an imam who leads a congregation in prayer, and after finishing recalls that he had an impurity he had known about before the prayer but forgot to wash off — must those who prayed behind him repeat both the obligatory and the voluntary prayers, or only the obligatory? Answer: Those who followed the imam are only required to repeat the obligatory prayers, not the voluntary ones. Additionally: if a eunuch prays with women, it is permissible for him to stand in line with them.

From the book Mukhtasar al-Muṣannaf: If a man begins an obligatory prayer on his own and an imam then arrives and steps forward to lead, it is recommended for the man to bow and prostrate, complete his cycle, count it as voluntary, and then begin the prayer with the imam. If he does not do this and continues praying alone, that is also permissible. If some of the believers stand in front of the imam, the imam's prayer and the prayers of those beside him remain valid, while the prayers of those who stand in front of him are not valid.

<!-- page 133 -->
If he prays with them in Mecca and the rows encircle the Kaaba, no row should be positioned closer to the walls of the Kaaba than the imam's row on any side. If a non-adult holds authority — such as being the governor of the Muslims in his era — it is not permissible for anyone to lead him in prayer, nor is it permissible for other boys to lead the obligatory prayers. One who recites with a melody that distorts the meanings of the Quran may not lead the prayer, nor may a deaf person who cannot hear even if warned.

If the master of a slave comes to the slave's dwelling, the master has a stronger claim to lead the prayer than the slave. It is stated in the book of interpretation of the Shari'ah by our master al-Mu'izz — may God bless him and grant him peace — and narrated from the Prophet — may God bless him and grant him peace — that he once rode a horse, fell from it, and was injured on his right side. His companions came to him and he attended the prayer and led them while seated. They stood, and he gestured to them to sit, so they sat. When he finished his prayer, he said: The imam is to be followed — if he stands, stand; if he recites, listen; if he says "Sami'a Allāhu liman ḥamidah," say "Rabbanā laka l-ḥamd," glorifying God greatly; if he gives the salām, give the salām; and if he prays seated, sit together with him.

The author of the book of footnotes said: Shortening the imamate of a blind person is appropriate when he is compelled to lead and does not take a salary for it. It is stated in the national collection that a blind person is not permitted to lead the prayer unless he performs it without charge and without salary. The Nāṣib oppose this, but our master Qāḍī al-Nu'mān ibn Muhammad — may God bless him — in an epistle responding to questions posed by some judges, was asked about the imamate of a blind person and ruled that there is nothing wrong with him leading, provided he performs it


<!-- page 134 -->
An imam should not take a salary. As stated in the book *Mukhtasar al-Athar*, it is narrated from the People of the House — may Allah's prayers be upon them — that they disapproved of the imamate of the leper, the one afflicted with leprosy, a child born of adultery, one under obligation, the possessed, and the blind, except when necessity compels it or when he takes an imam for a salary. They did not regard the Arab, the one with limited capacity, or the madman as fit to lead. He said: They permitted those in similar situations to lead one another, each leading his like.

Among those who held the same view, it is stated in the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf* that if a man departed the mosque and no one joined him, their prayers are invalid if they come after the imam has already left the mosque with a man who was not with him. It is only permissible for them to wait a short time for the imam to speak before he exits the mosque. The imam must not begin any complete act of prayer before the imam himself begins it. If one of them precedes him in any act through omission, he must return to his position so as to remain a follower.
