It is stated in the Essential Councils that the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him — said: "Whoever raises his head before the imam fears that Allah will disfigure his head into the head of a donkey." It is stated in the book of purity that if there is a barrier — such as a wall — between the imam and the congregation, it is not permissible for them to pray unless they can see his prayer from behind it, whether the wall is short enough for them to see over, or open like the partitions in women's sections of mosques. It is stated in the book *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf* that if a man prays with a group and two men join the prayer with him having missed some of it, and then the imam gives the salaam,

<!-- page 135 -->
they make up what remains of the prayer, one of them completing it in the manner of the imam with the other — otherwise the prayer of the one being led is invalid. It is stated in the book of clarification that the Messenger of Allah — peace and blessings be upon him and his family — combined the noon, afternoon, sunset, and night prayers without travel, illness, or fear. Our master, the author of the Book of Councils al-Mustansiriyya — peace be upon its owner — stated in the tenth council, which concerns the imamate in prayer, that seven conditions distinguish the ranks of imamate. Mawlana Ja'far ibn Muhammad al-Sadiq — may Allah's blessings be upon him — said: The one who leads the people is the eldest among them in terms of emigration if they are otherwise equal. If they are equal in that, then the one with the most Quran memorized leads; if equal in memorization, then the most learned in jurisprudence; if equal in that, then the eldest. The owner of the mosque has the greater right in his own mosque, and the imam — peace be upon him — and whoever establishes it has the greater right over all of them. A woman prays with other women and does not precede them; rather, they stand to her right and to the north. Twelve categories of men may not lead in prayer: 1) the healthy man does not lead the sick; 2) nor does the leper; 3) nor the one with leprosy; 4) nor the insane; 5) nor the one of limited capacity; 6) nor the child born of adultery; 7) the Arab does not lead the Muhajirin; 8) nor does the divorced man lead; 9) nor the one who performs dry ablution lead those who perform water ablution; 10) nor the mute lead the eloquent; 11) nor the resident lead the traveler; 12) nor the one who opposes by way of innovation lead those who mourn in the traditional manner. Each of these, however, may lead others in a similar situation. It is stated in the book of questions — which is from the late Sheikh Shimon ibn Muhammad Ghori

<!-- page 136 -->
al-Hindi — may Allah be pleased with him — and the answers are from our master Badr al-Din Hassan ibn Idris ibn al-Hasan — may Allah be pleased with him — Question: What do you say about two men, one residing in Rajpur and the other in Jalalpur, who meet in Rajpur and do not know who among them is more virtuous? Or what if they are both in Rajpur and then meet in Jalalpur, and the one whose ablution is nullified from Jalalpur leads the one whose ablution is nullified from Rajpur — what is the status of their prayer? Answer: If it is not known who is more virtuous, then the owner of the mosque has the greater right in his mosque.

Question: What do you say about a man whose ablution has been nullified, who stood and prayed alone, and then another man joined him before his bowing or after he completed it, and that man is not known to have led anyone else — is it permissible for the one joining to pray behind this imam, or not? Answer: His prayer is permissible with the imam even if the imam did not intend to lead anyone else.

Question: What do you say about an imam who prays with the congregation on a ship — seated because of the ship's movement or for some other reason — and this is not possible for some of them while others manage it — what is the ruling on their prayer? Answer: It is permissible to pray seated on account of the ship if they are unable to stand. Whoever is able should pray alone while standing.

It is stated in the questions posed by some of the sheikhs of India and the answers given by some of the loyal preachers of Yemen — may Allah exalt them and bless us with their intercession and forgiveness — Question: If a man prays with a woman who is his mahram, where do the man and the woman stand? Answer: If he prays with his mahram, she prays behind him or to his right, slightly behind him. If she is not a mahram, her prayer with him is not permissible unless a third person is present with them.

<!-- page 137 -->
A further question from among the questions of some of the learned of India, and their answers from some of our loyal preachers of Yemen: I asked about an imam who leads the people but does not recite well, introducing alterations and distortions — such as pronouncing "Allah is innocent of the polytheists and His messenger" with a break on the word for blame, or saying "some of them are the guidance of Allah" with an opening on the word for distraction, and the like. Answer: Whoever cannot recite well should not be taken as imam. The obligation falls on him to recite in such a way that he does not substitute a verse of mercy for a verse of punishment, nor substitute falsehood for truth. Such a person should not lead the prayer. Whoever recites well should lead the people if they call upon him to do so and he consents.

It is stated, regarding the legal questions answered by some of the loyal ones — may Allah sanctify their souls — I asked about an imam leading the people who does not know what portion of the prayer he has completed and what remains. He must rely on certainty. If those behind him separate from him, they should return to what they affirmed until the truth becomes clear. If those behind him also did not know, and neither he nor they could determine whether the prayer was more or less complete, the matter is left open.

**Mention of the congregation and its categories**

It is stated in the first-mentioned book of questions and answers — arranged according to the order of chapters in the book *Pillars of Islam* — Question: What do you say about a man who prays alone with his wife — who is his mahram — praying to his right,

<!-- page 138 -->
alone, while there is also a man praying, and in front of him his wife or a mahram prays — is it permissible for him to pray in that position or not? Answer: It is not permissible for a woman to stand before a man while he is praying behind her.

Question: What do you say about a man from the people of the da'wah who prayed the afternoon prayer and then prayed that same prayer again at a time when taqiyya does not apply, and some of the people of the da'wah followed his lead in taqiyya before those not adhering to the doctrine of the People of the House — may Allah's prayers be upon them — intending him as their imam? What is the ruling on their prayer, and is anything required of the imam in that situation? Answer: He does so for the sake of taqiyya, and he need not be concerned about their following him; nothing is required of him in this regard.

Question: What do you say about what is mentioned in the book of purity — that the Messenger of Allah — may Allah bless him and his family — struck a man who prayed behind the rows alone, with a gap beside him, and ordered him to repeat the prayer? And Ja'far ibn Muhammad — peace be upon him — said to him: "Our Lord, is the one who prays in such a situation also commanded to repeat?" Answer: He may be commanded to repeat it, and it is incumbent upon him to do so.

Question: What do you say about a man performing the prayer of glorification or another supererogatory prayer — is it permissible for him to cut it short if he fears missing the congregational prayer? Answer: Praying in congregation is better and more obligatory than other prayers.

Question: What do you say about a man who wishes to pray in congregation and joins one, but fears missing the sunnah prayer — is it permissible for him to cut short the supererogatory prayer? Answer: If he fears missing the obligatory prayer, he should cut it short; otherwise, he should not.

<!-- page 139 -->
Question: What do you say about a man who enters the obligatory prayer and is reciting the Quran when the imam arrives; he moves forward while standing, and when the imam bows, he bows with him without renewing his ihraam — what is the status of his prayer? Answer: He must repeat it.

Question: What do you say about a man who stood and prayed alone, and another man joined him before he bowed or after he had bowed, and he was not known to lead anyone else — is it permissible for the latecomer to pray behind him or not? Answer: His prayer is permissible with the imam even if the imam did not intend to lead anyone else.

If a man prays with a woman who is his mahram, or prays with both a man and a woman who is a mahram, where should the man and woman stand? Answer: If he prays with his mahram, she prays behind him or to his right, slightly behind him. If she is not a mahram, her prayer is only valid if a third person is with them.

Question: Is it permissible for the exalted ones to recite the religious benefits to women — whether related or unrelated — in a single gathering? Answer: If there is a screen between the man and the woman, he should recite across it while she listens without seeing him, and a man should not be alone with a woman unless she has another woman with her or he has another person with him.

Question: If a man joins the imam during the final tashahhud of the obligatory prayer, does he intend to complete it or not? Answer: If he reaches the imam during the final tashahhud, he intends the prayer in a mutually exclusive sense, because the final tashahhud is the last limit of the prayer. He sits and recites as the imam recites, and when the imam gives the salaam, he stands and completes the prayer, thereby catching the congregation.

It is also related: The saying of al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad — may Allah's prayers be upon him — that the rows should

<!-- page 140 -->
be complete and continuous, with only as much space between each two rows as a prostrating body requires. If they do not pray in this manner, their prayer is not valid. The meaning of "the length of a prostrating body" is that the worshipper has enough room to prostrate. If the imam prays in congregation and all the rows are filled, and a man arrives and finds no place in the rows, he prays alone beside the imam and does not enter the imam's row. If a man who is the argument of the imam stands behind the imam and finds no place in the rows, he advances to stand beside him and prays with the imam. This is not permissible for others — this is the explicit text.

It is stated in *Mukhtasar al-Musannaf* regarding the saying: "Whoever prays with a people — they think well of him." He said: This applies when he is unaware of anything bad about them. He said: There are differing views on lining up between the columns. Some disapproved of it while others narrated that there is a dispensation for it. It is stated in the book of clarification that the Messenger of Allah — may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him — combined the noon, afternoon, sunset, and night prayers without travel, illness, or fear.

Our master and our master Ja'far ibn Sayyidina Sulayman — may Allah bless them in the Garden — stated in an answer to questions that were from the ownership of the author of this book — Sayyidina Ali ibn Sulayman — and his servant answered them — may Allah bless her — from the island of his right to the island of Hinda. Among what I asked about — may Allah preserve you — is the question of whether women, if they pray in congregation with men from behind a curtain, must be able to see the men.

<!-- page 141 -->
Does closeness to them suffice, or not? If the place is connected, is there still a need for them to see the men? Answer: They should observe the men before entering ihraam. According to the Dawudi position, the woman who is close to the men prays in a place near them and continues to watch the men during bowing and prostration; otherwise the prayer of all of them is invalid. The meaning is that if the place is connected — for example, a tent or a shed — prayer may be performed in it. Looking at the men before ihraam is permissible, but looking after entering ihraam is not permitted; rather, it is prohibited even outside of prayer, so how much more so during prayer, which is the greatest act of worship. Looking during bowing and prostration, or while standing or sitting after ihraam, invalidates the prayer if it is directed at something — and this is looking at something that, while not itself forbidden, carries a sin in that context. If the place is not connected, the woman close to the men need only glimpse their clothing before ihraam, and this suffices for all of them.

It is stated in the interpretation of the shari'ah: The Prophet — peace be upon him and his family — saw a man praying alone behind the row without aligning with the imam or standing beside him, and he ordered him to repeat the prayer. It is also not permissible for anyone to approach the mihrab except from the foot of the imam when he steps forward. Likewise, none of the respondents may come forward except those introduced by the preacher and his family.

<!-- page 137 -->


<!-- page 142 -->
On the Characteristics and Sunnahs of Prayer

He said in the first-mentioned question-and-answer book, which is arranged in the order of the chapters of the book *Pillars of Islam*: Question — What do you say concerning what is stated in the book of purity, that Ibn al-Kowa opposed the Commander of the Believers, peace be upon him, repeatedly by raising his voice in prayer and then reciting, "So be patient, for the promise of Allah is true" — the verse refers to our master — was it 'Ali, peace be upon him, who was meant to begin the prayer with the surah of which this verse is a part, or not? Answer: No; rather, he recited this verse in order to silence and rebuke him. The question then follows: What do you say concerning what is mentioned in the books of the Shari'ah regarding the length of the joint, the middle, and the shortness thereof? As for our master, there is no fixed limit in the madhhab of the Ahl al-Bayt, the prayers of Allah be upon them. Answer: The longest are from the joints to the argument, the middle are from the argument to the uncle — and they are wondering — and the shortest are those who ask among the people. And further: Whoever does not pray without excuse or compelling reason is in violation of the covenant, and whoever accuses a believer — the accused swears by Allah and the accuser does not believe him — has broken the covenant; and whoever commits adultery or kills a believer has turned renegade from the covenant; and whoever insults the caller, and whoever — when the caller commands the believer to strive with himself and his wealth — refuses without excuse, has broken the covenant; and whoever forms the intention to pray but then abandons his intention before

<!-- page 143 -->
entering into it — whether through distraction, forgetfulness, or any other cause — and then prays afterward, that prayer is not valid from him. It is not permissible for a man to recite to himself unless he does so with his own tongue. Whoever witnesses the first tashahhud has not thereby validated the second, nor does the second validate the first. He said in a book that is a summary of the classification: Whoever forms the intention to pray but then abandons his intention before entering into it — through preoccupation, forgetfulness, or any other cause — that prayer is not valid from him thereafter. From him: The worshipper should follow after the opening takbir of ihram, the imam being in the prayer space — and this holds whether the imam has preceded him by something — and should begin recitation only after proceeding to the mosque. He said in summary: He opens the prayer with a takbir in which he raises his hands, stretches out his palms, and gathers his fingers. Regarding the lowest and least of the prayers, they are: 1 – Opening with the takbir; 2 – Standing for those who are able; 3 – Bowing; 4 – Prostration; 5 – Sitting for the witnessing. Whoever performs these five limits and forgets or neglects anything else, having prayed and knowing its limits — if he deliberately omits something obligatory or from the Sunnah, there is no prayer for him, and he must repeat what he left out of the prayer in this manner; and the ignorant person must learn. He said in al-Akhbar: A woman should place her hands above her knees on her thighs and should not lower them too far, so that her thighs rise. He said in a book of interpretation: The props — and he says from behind the imam, when the imam says "Allah heard those who praised Him," they say quietly, "Our Lord, praise be to You" — except for the one who leads on behalf of the imam when many people are praying behind him.

<!-- page 144 -->
Those among them who hear him speak aloud about it will do so openly, and those who do not hear will not speak aloud in praise of him. He said in a book that is a summary on practice: Whoever witnesses the first tashahhud has not thereby validated the second, and the second is credited against the first. He said in the book of interpretation of the pillars: Evidently, when the worshipper sits in prayer, he bends his left leg and raises his right leg. He said in the brief speech on jurisprudence — whose questions come from the noble Sheikh Hasan ibn Mawla Adam al-Hindi, and his letters to the masters of Yemen, may Allah sanctify all their souls — that the prayer of a woman who is not veiled, and her position, is a reward; and likewise for the man, except that the Sunnah is to take the initiative to do so. Our master and Mawlana said — Ja'far ibn Sulayman, may Allah sanctify him and grant us his intercession and forgiveness — in his noble letter, which he tagged with the sword as the finest in refuting innovations, advising and admonishing one who had fallen into heedlessness, one of those who slept much from the people of the island of Yemen after he had settled therein and taken comfort. Chapter: This is also what he has taught.

Many of you have seen men and women who are committed to the mountain of loyalty and who hold fast to the rope of obedience to Allah Almighty from among those obligated to pray — yet they do not prostrate their foreheads to Allah, Glorified be He, with a single prayer rak'ah. This is a degradation from prayer, and from the rak'ahs surrounding it, and it is among the gravest of consequences. I hear it from all of them by reputation, or it has struck the ears of those upon whom the words of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, have fallen: "The distinction between a slave and disbelief is the abandonment of prayer," and the saying of the Prince of

<!-- page 145 -->
the Believers, peace be upon him: "There is no share in Islam for one who has abandoned prayer." If that is the case, then likewise there is no share in Islam for one who does not pray. The one who does not pray — the distinction between a slave and disbelief is prayer — so the one who does not pray among those who claim faith is not counted among the number of Muslims, and is no more than a nail-clipping of Islam. Allah Almighty has said in the verses of His Book: "And whoever seeks a religion other than Islam, it will not be accepted from him, and he will be among the losers in the Hereafter." Those who abandon prayer — as we have observed among the people — fall into two categories: the first are people of a tribe who have turned away from the affairs of religion; and the second are people who occupy themselves with their own desires and with animal concerns, most of them as though they had emerged from the eye. Some of them are people grown coarse and thick — especially those among them who are women — who neither care to be lean nor feel any shame before Allah Almighty, nor toward worshipping Him. Whoever does that has destroyed himself and wronged his Creator, who has no need of his worship — for its benefit returns to the worshipper himself and to those like him in the world. The Lord did not create them and make them His servants in worship except to benefit them thereby, as Allah Almighty said: "And the jinn and mankind were not created except to worship Him." There is no doubt that whoever has entered the path spoken of — Allah Almighty having said in the verses of His Clear Book: "Indeed, those who are too arrogant to worship Me will enter Hell, disgraced" — is in truth already dead. For when a slave prostrates to his Lord, the Almighty,

<!-- page 146 -->
in his prayer, and instead calls out to Satan — woe to him — obeying Satan and worshipping the disbeliever, even though he himself does not pray the prayer of Islam nor worship his Lord with it, whose worship of Him is acceptable in his religion — he worships what he has received from his forefathers. And there is no disbeliever in the lands of India who is too arrogant to worship Allah Almighty with this arrogance and who does not manifest this trait — male and female, all of them — except those among them who fall into the two sections mentioned and incline toward them, as they do from misguidance and inclination. The two aforementioned conditions exist in the doctrines of Islam just as they do in the doctrines of all religions. But this does not serve well those who have attached themselves to the guardianship of the saints of Allah Almighty with its firm mountain, boasting of their affairs to the people of faith and trust, and claiming the guardianship of anyone — without that attachment being accompanied by prayer. Whoever does so is like one who calls himself in the hereafter to doom and woe and the opposites of the guiding call. Even if some of them are created thus from among their men and their women, they are estranged from what they were created for, and they are not successful in their affairs. But you, in your affairs — praise be to Allah Almighty — you are successful. And whoever recognizes the invitation of the guardian of Allah, peace be upon him, and believes in heaven, hell, punishment, judgment, the balance, the path, the gathering, and the resurrection, his sound mind compels him to adhere to the worship of his Lord Almighty through prayer — which is the pillar of religion and the strength of Islam — and to comply with it in accordance with the command, and to heed the warning and the punishment of his Lord. Let such a person reform himself by this means,

<!-- page 147 -->
as do the reformers and those who fear Allah — for those are the successful ones. He said in a book of interpretation of the Shari'ah: The obligatory duties of prayer are seven: 1 – The opening takbir; 2 – Recitation; 3 – Bowing; 4 – Prostration; 5 – Praise; 6 – Greeting; 7 – Submission and surrender. He said: When you stand, it is not permissible for you to shift your foot forward or back — it must remain planted — and you may not speak after the opening has been enlarged until the submission, nor move from your place. He said in it: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "I was commanded to prostrate on seven instruments." He said: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "Do not recite while bowing, do not move the pebbles during prayer, do not turn your attention to the fox, do not fall like the monkey, and do not peck like the crow." He said in it: He separates the two rak'ahs with a sitting. He said: Do not pray the obligatory prayer if permission has been given, and do not pray when the prayer is already being performed. He said: The Dhuhr prayer is four rak'ahs. He said: It was narrated from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family, that he said: "Do not, when bowing, knock like the donkey, nor like one skewering meat," and he commanded that the head should not drop below the alignment of the back. He said in it: The head and back are parallel and aligned in prayer. He said: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family — whoever prays much at night, his face is brightened during the day. The author of the hidden message, which encompasses the foundations of the religion of Allah, said: al-Musnah — who is the noble Da'i Zayd ibn Muhammad ibn al-Fadl, may Allah, Exalted be He, sanctify him, provide for his intercession, and forgive him: The obligatory duties of prayer are seven, namely: 1 – Intention;

<!-- page 148 -->
2 – The opening takbir; 3 – Standing; 4 – Recitation; 5 – Bowing; 6 – Prostration; 7 – Submission. In it he said: "And I have made every day five prayers." In it he said: Prayer has four limits, namely: 1 – Place; 2 – Time; 3 – Purity; 4 – Intention. In it he said: The rak'ahs of the five prayers are seventeen. He said: Prayer is of two kinds — obligatory and Sunnah. He said in it: Hence Muhammad, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "Whoever abandons my Sunnah is cursed" — that is, he has left a guardian who is far from the mercy of Allah. He said: The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and his family, said: "There is no prayer for one who does not pay zakat." He said in it: The movements of prayer, such as bowing and prostrating, are submission and reverence. Then he said: Therefore, the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, said: Whoever does not find that his prayer restrains him from immorality and vice has only increased in distance from Allah Almighty. That is why the nation of Shu'ayb said to him: "O Shu'ayb, does your prayer command you to leave what our fathers worshipped?" — then he said that prayer whose outward form does not restrain and does not command is not prayer. Our master, the owner of the Mustansiriyyah councils, said — peace be upon its owner — in the sixth council: You have heard what we have recited to you from the divisions of the obligatory duties of purity and its Sunnahs, corresponding in number to the chapters of the witnessing and its letters — for purity is one of the rights of the witnessing, and its rights are likewise prayer. So listen now to what we read to you of its meaning: Prayer has seven obligatory duties and twelve Sunnah prayers. Of the obligatory, they are:

<!-- page 149 -->
1 – The prayer of residence, which is seventeen rak'ahs every day and night; 2 – The prayer of travel, which is eleven rak'ahs in the day and night; 3 – The Friday prayer, which is not performed until one meets with the imam four times; the prayer of fear — part of it is performed with the imam and the rest alone; 4 – The prayer completing the obligatory circumambulation of Hajj; 5 – The prayer of the obligatory circumambulation of 'Umrah; 6 – The prayer of the obligatory tawaf of 'Umrah; 7 – The funeral prayer, which is obligatory upon those Muslims who are aware of it to perform, such that if one of them performs it, the rest become volunteers. The Sunnah prayers are: 1 – The prayer of Eid al-Fitr; 2 – The prayer of Eid al-Nahr; 3 – The prayer of the solar eclipse; 4 – The prayer of the lunar eclipse; 5 – The prayer of rain; 6 – The prayer of the earthquake; 7 – The prayer for the verses that occur; 8 – The prayer of intercession and witr; 9 – The prayer of frequency — these are two rak'ahs performed sitting; 10 – The prayer of the two rak'ahs of Fajr, which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and his family, made up after the sun had risen and he made up the obligatory prayers after them; 11 – Twenty-eight rak'ahs every day and night, including the prayer of the Awwabeen, which is six rak'ahs after the setting of the sun; including eight rak'ahs between Dhuhr and 'Asr; including ten rak'ahs between Maghrib and 'Isha'; including four rak'ahs after the last 'Isha', which is the night prayer; 12 – The prayer of greeting the mosque upon entering it. Then he said, may Allah be pleased with him, in the seventh council of it: So listen now to what we read to you — that in each of the five prayers there are seven obligatory duties.


<!-- page 150 -->
The seven obligatory duties in each of the five prayers are: 1 – Intention; 2 – Facing the Qibla; 3 – Takbīr al-iḥrām; 4 – Reciting the Opening of the Book; 5 – Bowing; 6 – Prostration; 7 – Sitting for the salutation. The twelve sunnahs are: 1 – The call to prayer; 2 – The iqāma; 3 – Saying "I have turned my face to the One who created the heavens and the earth," in its complete form; 4 – Seeking refuge from the accursed devil; 5 – Reciting a sūrah after the Opening of the Book in the first two rak'ahs of each obligatory prayer; 6 – Raising the hands when bowing and when descending into the bowing posture; 7 – The state of bowing; 8 – The imam saying "Allah has heard those who praise Him," and "Our Lord, praise be to You"; 9 – Praise during prostration; 10 – The first sitting and the first tashahhud; 11 – The second tashahhud; 12 – Qunut in every prayer that has no first sitting, since such a prayer has two sessions for the witnessing and therefore includes no Qunut. Mawlānā Amīr al-Mu'minīn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his Four Hundred Etiquettes that the believer should not clasp his hands together while standing in prayer, as that is the practice of the People of the Book. He said: if one of you is injured during prayer, let him cover the wound or conceal it in his garment until he has finished. He said: excessive inattention breaks the prayer, and whoever falls into it must begin again — with the adhān, the iqāma, and the takbīr. He said: give each sūrah its due of bowing and prostration when you pray. He said: sit between the two prostrations

<!-- page 151 -->
so that your limbs may settle before you rise, for that is what has been done. He said: the one praying behind the imam should not prostrate before the imam prostrates. He said: if you pray alone, let yourself hear your own recitation, praise, and takbīr; and if you move from one prayer, do so on your right side.

A legal question concerning the limits of the island of India was put to Muḥammad ʿAlī ibn Ṣāliḥ al-Badrī, may God preserve him, and was answered by our master Hibat Allāh — may God bless him. The second question addresses what our master — may God bless him and grant him support — rules regarding a person who inadvertently recites Sūrat al-Falaq and Sūrat al-Nās during the obligatory prayer: is the prayer valid, or must it be repeated? And if it is permissible, what is the ruling on one who recites Sūrat al-Nās in the first rak'ah — must he repeat it in the second, or may he recite what comes before it, and if not, must he repeat the prayer? And if it is not permissible, what of one who recites Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ in the first rak'ah — must he repeat it in the second, or should he recite the sūrah that precedes it?

Answer: From God alone we seek success and reward, and from His guardian and his father we receive the clouds of immortality. There is no text from the guardians of God explicitly prohibiting the recitation of the two refuge sūrahs in the obligatory prayer. If the worshipper recites them with full presence, his prayer is complete. If he recites Sūrat al-Nās in the first rak'ah, he should repeat it in the second. Whoever recites Sūrat al-Ikhlāṣ in the first should recite al-Falaq in the second. One must not deliberately recite what is obligatory with the knowledge that it should be replaced by what is recommended.

<!-- page 152 -->
**Mention of supplication after the prayer**

It is stated in the first question-and-answer book — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — that the question was: what do you say regarding what is mentioned in the books of the guiding call, that supplication is not recited after the afternoon prayer? Does this refer to the supplication of drawing near, or to all supplications? Answer: It refers to drawing near and other acts, and no one is obligated to commit a sin through supplication.

Question: Should the worshipper supplicate after the obligatory ʿAṣr prayer or not, and should he wipe his face at the place of prostration during every prayer or not? Answer: It is not permitted until after ʿAṣr and Fajr. Our loyal ones — may God sanctify their souls — have prescribed wiping after ʿAṣr and Fajr in particular, and this is what they received from the pure Imams, may God's blessings be upon them. It has also been mentioned that a person's lifespan is not prolonged by a supplication made after the ʿAṣr prayer, yet if he prolongs it, he bears no sin for that. It is said in the book of the interpretation of the Sharīʿa: if the prayer is during the day, the recitation is silent; if it is at night, in the depth of darkness, the voice is raised; and at dawn, one announces his prayer like glass catching the light. Our master Mawlānā, the owner of the Mustanṣiriyya Councils — may God's peace rest upon its owner — said in the Eleventh Council: having completed our discussion of the jurisprudence of prayer to the extent explained therein — where the outward has its exposition and elaborations, and the inward has its signs and intimations — we follow it with

<!-- page 153 -->
what you will now read: the mention of supplication, which is the culmination of prayer, and what it contains.

According to what we have set forth, we have explained in the remainder to those who are prepared to receive it the following: 1 – the supplication of appeal; 2 – the supplication of seeking refuge; 3 – the supplication of al-istikāna; 4 – the supplication of weeping; 5 – the supplication of wetting; 6 – the supplication of al-ibtihāl; 7 – the supplication of al-taʿaffur. In the supplication of appeal, the supplicant spreads his hands with the inside of his palms facing his face and calls for help against what he fears; he then turns his palms so their backs face his face. In the supplication of seeking refuge, he turns his palms outward. In the supplication of al-istikāna, he clasps his shoulders with his hands, and this is done only in solitude. The supplication with the rosary is one in which the finger counts the beads, and supplication by moving the finger is done by moving it. In the supplication of raising, he stretches his palms outward and extends his hands upward toward the sky. In the supplication of al-taʿaffur, he supplicates while pressing his cheeks to the ground. For each of these seven supplications there is a mention in the Qurʾān, which we will address when we return to explaining what we have read, because everything we have mentioned consists of roots — like the roots of a tree waiting to branch out and bear fruit — and the twelve are the principles and foundations of supplication.

This is because the one who wishes his supplication to be answered should: 1 – precede it with repentance from what God has forbidden; 2 – give charity; 3 – fast; 4 – pray, for God Almighty says: good words ascend to Him, and righteous deeds lift them higher; 5 – perfect his intention in supplication, accept it, and gather his heart; 6 – begin by glorifying God Almighty and praising Him as He deserves; 7 – invoke blessings upon the Prophet, peace be upon him.

<!-- page 154 -->
8 – Draw near to God Almighty through the imams of the religion, naming them one by one until he reaches the imam of his own time. 9 – Beseech him and them before God in answering his supplication. 10 – Then ask for the good of this world and the Hereafter. 11 – Seek refuge from the evil of this world and the Hereafter. 12 – Then call upon God for whatever he wishes and wills.

Mawlānā Amīr al-Mu'minīn ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his Four Hundred Etiquettes: remember God Almighty and He will accept your supplication; remember Him and He will preserve you and care for you. He also said: be prompt in supplication before calamity descends, for the gates of heaven are opened at six times: 1 – at the descent of rain; 2 – when the army advances; 3 – at the call to prayer; 4 – when the Qurʾān is recited; 5 – at the setting of the sun; 6 – at the rising of dawn. He said: supplication responds to a decree that has already been concluded, so call upon God and hasten. He said: ward off affliction through supplication, and do so before calamity descends — for He who splits the grain and stirs the breeze, affliction reaches the believer faster than a torrent from the mountaintop to the valley below. He said: the one who does not supplicate is like the thrower who aims without an arrow. He said: whoever recites "Say: He is God, the One" ten times until the sun rises, and "Indeed, We sent it down on the Night of Power" ten times, no sin will be recorded against him even if Iblīs exerts his utmost effort.

<!-- page 155 -->
**Mentioning words and deeds in prayer**

It is stated in the first question-and-answer book — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — that the question was: what do you say about a man who prays behind an imam who is intoxicated, and the man signals to him with a bow or some other gesture without completing the prayer properly — does anything fall upon him or not? Answer: the prayer is invalidated if such gesturing is done deliberately and without excuse, whether directed toward the imam or accompanied by praise.

Question: What do you say about a man who bows excessively in his prayer out of habit — does anything fall upon him for that? Answer: if he does so deliberately, he must repeat the prayer.

Question: What do you say about a woman praying in congregation or alone who carries her infant with her during prayer to prevent him from crying — is that permissible? Answer: it is not permissible for her to engage in prayer in such a manner.

Question: What do you say about a person who prays while experiencing some internal discomfort — must his prayer be deficient on account of that? Answer: if it is something unavoidable, nothing falls upon him.

What do you say about a man who prays with his hair gathered inside his turban, and he draws it from the back of his neck to the area of his forehead above his temple without inserting it in the middle of his head — is that permissible? Answer: there is nothing wrong with that; the forbidden practice

<!-- page 156 -->
is drawing the hair back to its origin, which means gathering it to the middle of the head. As for one who prays while something is inside him that resembles a lobe or a dirham or the like — if it does not prevent the proper formation of the letters, his prayer is complete. And if he holds something in his hand that does not prevent him from fulfilling the conditions of his prayer, nothing falls upon him; but if he is free of such encumbrances, that is preferable.

It is stated in a classified book that one who eats or drinks during prayer — whether out of forgetfulness or deliberately — has interrupted his prayer. It is also stated in the book of the interpretation of the Sharīʿa that recitation comes before bowing. He said: do not raise your voice above the voice of the imam. He said: recitation in the noon and afternoon prayers should not be audible, for these are daytime prayers. He said: the night prayer is recited aloud. He said: the Prophet, peace be upon him, forbade wiping over the feet, and he left aside the public utterance of the basmala. He said: once the worshipper rises, he does not speak until he offers the salutation.

Our master Jaʿfar ibn Manṣūr al-Yaman — may God sanctify him — said in the book of the subconscious, on the meaning of prayer: prayer has doors, a few of which must be perfected for the prayer to be complete, and these are: 1 – Wuḍūʾ; 2 – Opening; 3 – Adhān; 4 – Iqāma; 5 – Orientation; 6 – Recitation; 7 – Bowing; 8 – Praise; 9 – Praise; 10 – Prostration; 11 – Tashahhud; 12 – Taslīm; 13 – Duʿāʾ al-Qunut; 14 – Duʿāʾ al-Witr. These are fourteen doors that the worshipper must observe; they are the pillars of prayer, and without them his prayer is not complete.

<!-- page 157 -->
**Mentioning dress in prayer and what the worshipper prostrates upon**

It is stated in the first question-and-answer book — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — that the question was: what do you say about a prayer hall where the rug and other furnishings are decorated with engravings facing the qibla or in another direction — is it permissible for the worshipper to pray on them or not, and does this apply specifically to the place of prostration or more broadly? Answer: if any such decoration distracts the worshipper, his prayer is not permissible in that space; if it does not distract him, the prayer is permissible — except that one should not pray in the direction of images.

Question: What do you say about a man who prays in a garment that a woman is permitted to wear — is his prayer permissible? And what of a woman who prays in pure silk or a very red garment, whether dyed with saffron or not — is her prayer permissible? Answer: this is a matter of etiquette; wearing such garments does not invalidate the prayer, whether the wearer is a man or a woman — except that pure silk is disliked for men.

Question: What do you say about what is mentioned in the books of the Sharīʿa — that if any part of a woman's ʿawra becomes exposed during prayer, her prayer is invalidated whether she knew of it or not? And since it is not the custom of the women of the people of the daʿwa in India to wear full covering, and their legs are sometimes exposed during prayer once or twice, how is their prayer? Answer: it is obligatory to wear a covering, for all women


<!-- page 158 -->
If any part of her 'awrah becomes exposed, she may not pray, and I am aware of this ruling. Or perhaps you are unfamiliar with what this question actually concerns: namely, the person swimming in water, the person in fear, and the traveler.

When a traveler carries weapons that prevent him from praying properly — such as feathers in arrows, daggers, and pure silk garments — must he pray without repeating the prayer, or not? The answer is that a prayer need not be repeated except when clothing is pure, and there is nothing impure on the worshipper that he has not removed.

Question: What do you say about a man who prays while wearing gold or silver weights that have been forged, and similar items — is his prayer valid? Answer: His prayer is complete and valid.

Question: It is stated in the summary of the Musannaf that it is permissible to pray on a carpet on which figures are depicted, unless the figure is in the place of prostration, where it would distract from prayer. Is it permissible to pray on such a carpet? And what of wearing garments on which figures are depicted — is that permissible for prayer? Answer: A rug or garment bearing figures — if the worshipper can see them, it is not permissible to pray on or in it; if he cannot see them, it is permissible.

Question: If the worshipper prays on stone, milk, or something similar, is that permissible? Answer: It is not permissible. Only the obligatory prayer is binding upon him; the nafl and sunnah prayers are not.

Question: What do you say about a man who has rings on his hand or foot for adornment rather than for any practical benefit — is that permissible? Answer: Men should not wear rings for adornment; women are permitted to do so, though leaving it is better.

<!-- page 159 -->
For men, there is no valid reason to do so, and whoever does it is not sinful. On another point: it is permissible to pray in the clothing of Muslim boys and Muslim slaves unless those garments show impurity or one does not pray in them. Whoever prays and then, upon finishing, notices impurity on his garment — and knew of it beforehand — his prayer is invalidated and he must repeat it, whether he was making up a missed prayer or not. A person should not pray in a state of distress, nor in darkness, nor in a garment that distresses him; if he finds no alternative, he prays as he is.

Whoever prays while wearing only a small cloth on his shoulders — meaning he has only that as a garment — it is not permissible for him to lead others in prayer; if he cannot find anyone else, his own prayer in that state is permissible. It is stated in the summary of the Musannaf: if a man prays while his obligatory covering is exposed and he is unaware of this until he finishes and then learns of it, he must repeat the prayer. If a free woman or an umm al-walad prays with her 'awrah exposed, whether knowingly or not, their prayers are void.

It is stated by our master Ja'far ibn Mansur al-Yaman — may Allah sanctify him — in his book on obligatory laws and the limits of religion: it is permissible for a man to uncover his head in public and to go out to the congregation and pray with his head uncovered. If a man prays while wearing something resembling an earring, a dirham, or something similar, and it does not prevent him from completing the motions of prayer, his prayer is valid. Even if he holds something in his hand that constrains him but does not prevent him from fulfilling the requirements of prayer, there is nothing wrong with that; if he is free of it altogether, that is preferable.

From the same source: a man who has nothing but an impure garment and cannot obtain another, nor water to purify it, should pray in it and then repeat the prayer when he finds another garment or water to purify it with. And if he has

<!-- page 160 -->
two garments and one of them is impure but he does not know which one in particular, he must pray twice — once in each garment — intending the obligatory prayer with each. It is permissible to pray on a carpet on which figures are placed, unless the place of prostration is occupied by one, since that would distract from prayer. Plaster and the like: when burned with the dead, it becomes counted as part of the mosque and the house, and prostration on it is permissible, since fire has purified it.

It is stated in the book of interpretation of the pillars, in his discussion of the saying: there is nothing wrong with praying in a single dense shirt — meaning the worshipper's shirt — and the point here is that if he bows, his side becomes apparent. From the same source, on the saying "like the wings of the hook," it refers to a garment wrapped around the back with the limbs resting on the shoulders, when no other option is available.

From the same source, in the saying forbidding the wearing of the garment known as ishtimal al-samma': this is when a man wraps himself in a single garment, placing its middle over his head or shoulders and holding one end in his left hand against his body, while the other end is turned from his right side to behind him, and he gathers it with his right hand so that it becomes like a sewn garment with both hands underneath it. If he prays in this manner, he is unable to pray properly — he cannot place his hands on the ground as required, cannot bow or prostrate correctly, and cannot complete the raising of his hands. Such a prayer is therefore incomplete and does not earn him reward.

It is stated in the summary of the Musannaf: an umm al-walad, a correspondence slave woman, a concubine, and a slave girl who has not yet reached menstruation — there is nothing wrong with

<!-- page 161 -->
their praying without a face-covering. Those among them who are disobedient and do not know this must repeat every prayer

performed without a face-covering, from the time of that lapse. It is also stated in the book of questions and answers — questions posed by the venerable Sheikh Yusuf ibn Ismail ibn Ibrahim al-Samati al-Hindi, and answered by our master Idris ibn al-Hasan, may Allah sanctify him:

Question: Is it permissible to pray on stone and plaster? Answer: The Prophet, peace be upon him, said: "The earth has been made for me a mosque and a means of purification." Anything that does not qualify as earth in the complete sense cannot be prayed on.

It is also stated in the section on Shari'ah issues and their answers — a collection of one hundred questions and three issues prepared by some of the virtuous sheikhs of India in correspondence with some of the preachers of Yemen, may Allah sanctify their souls:

Issue Seventeen: Regarding the free woman who prays while wearing a net over her clothing — does the net serve in place of the robe, or not? Answer: It is not mentioned that she is permitted to pray in a shield or veil unless they are thick. As for the net, it does not suffice as covering.

The fourth issue: whether the milk, fat, and eggs of camels, cows, sheep, and chickens that come into contact with the worshipper's clothing or body render them impure. Answer: A small amount is permissible.

It is stated in the book of interpretation of the Shari'ah: there is no valid prayer except on the ground or what grows from it, such as grass. He said: the Prophet, peace be upon him, said: "Three things of your world have been made dear to me" — and he placed women and goodness among them — "and the coolness of my eye has been made in prayer." He also said: the Prophet, peace be upon him, said: every prayer in which the Fatiha is not recited is incomplete and deficient.

<!-- page 162 -->
He said: there is no prayer in camel pens, and there is nothing wrong with praying in sheep pens after they have been swept and sprinkled with water. Mawlana Amir al-Mu'minin 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred maxims: none of you should stand before Allah, the Almighty, in a state of distress. He also said: a man should not pray in a shirt alone without a waist-wrapper. It is not permissible to pray in a garment worn loosely around the neck, nor in a garment worn immodestly. He said: a man should not prostrate on a picture, nor on a carpet on which he stands facing one. He also said: a man should not tie a dirham bearing a picture into his garment while praying; however, if the dirham is in a money-pouch or garment and is pure, that is permissible. A man should not prostrate on a heap of wheat, barley, anything edible, or on bread.

**On the Friday Prayer**

It is stated in the aforementioned book of questions and answers — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam:

Question: What do you say about what is stated in the book of purity — that if two congregations are separated by three miles, the Friday prayer may be held in each of them, whether in one city or two cities; but if they are less than three miles apart in a single city, the Friday prayer is held only in one of them? If the distance between them is less than three miles, should Friday prayer be held in both or not? Answer: The Friday prayer should not be held in both if the distance between them is less than

<!-- page 163 -->
three miles, whether they are in one city or two. The next question concerns the statement of our master al-Mu'ayyad, may Allah sanctify him, regarding the Friday sermon: whoever deliberately abandons it or treats it with contempt without valid excuse, whether the imam is just or unjust, shall not be gathered by God. The answer is: it is not permissible to pray behind the people of injustice except out of taqiyya — as stated in the Shari'ah from Mawlana al-Baqir Muhammad ibn 'Ali, may Allah's blessings be upon him — who said: do not make an imam of a Nasibi or a Haruri in prayer; instead, stand at the pillars of the mosque and recite to yourself as though you were praying alone.

It is mentioned in the exegesis of the narrations that al-Husayn ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, placed the agent of Mu'awiya before him in prayer for his brother al-Hasan ibn 'Ali, peace be upon him, and said: were it not for the sunna, I would have placed you ahead — meaning the sunna of the Ahl al-Bayt of praying behind them in taqiyya. As some of the Imams, peace be upon them, said: taqiyya is my religion and the religion of my fathers. What is meant by "the unjust" in this saying is: whoever among the preachers has openly displayed injustice and transgression — it is obligatory to pray behind him. If the people observe this, they should refer the matter to the guardian and await his response. This is the meaning of his saying: O righteous people, investigate and do not become doubtful after certainty, for you have taken refuge in the strong fortress of God.

It is stated in the summary of the Musannaf: the Friday prayer behind the Imam of the Muslims is better than praying behind his deputies. If the Imam is delayed due to some matter of Muslim welfare, his deputies

<!-- page 164 -->
pray on his behalf in the nearby mosques; the prayer held in his mosque — the one he designated for prayer before all others — takes precedence. It is also stated there: the call to prayer that is obligatory for whoever must observe the Friday prayer is the adhan given in the presence of the preacher when he sits in the position from which he delivers the sermon. If the two officers on Friday accept the pledge of allegiance at the appointed time, their transaction is not thereby cancelled. Out of thirst, the imam on the pulpit may drink.

It is also stated: if the imam delivers the Friday sermon and then descends and performs ablution, he may, if he wishes, repeat the sermon, or if he wishes, simply proceed to pray — and both are rewarded. If he becomes occupied while sitting because of something that distresses him and he dislikes leaving the sitting between the two sermons, and if he does so without completing it, he need not repeat it; but if he does complete the sitting and then does it again, he must repeat it.

If the imam delivers the Friday sermon and then the people leave him with only one man remaining, that man prays four rak'ahs. If the imam addresses the people on Friday and prays two rak'ahs with them and then they all leave — and he remains alone to pray two rak'ahs of Friday prayer — if he opens the prayer and they depart before he bows, he prays four rak'ahs of noon prayer. If the imam prays Friday prayer with the people and upon finishing the second rak'ah rises and then sits back down, he should say the testimony and perform the two prostrations of forgetfulness, and so forth.

Whoever among the people of Friday falls asleep while waiting for the prayer and the prayer is held without him — because his state of purity does not allow him to join the imam in time — must repeat it four times. It is stated in the book of narrations: he leans on his sword; if he does not draw it, he leans on a staff. It is stated in the book of interpretation of the Shari'ah: the sermon comes before the prayer, and the prayer after the sermon consists of two rak'ahs; he separates

<!-- page 165 -->
the sermon into two parts with a sitting in between. Reciting it aloud distinguishes it from the noon prayer on other days. The noon prayer extends over seven hours. He mentioned the pause after the sermon. He also stated: the sunna of the respondents in the council of the da'i is like the sunna of the congregation on Friday or the congregation behind the imam.

When the preacher on Friday mounts the elevated pulpit and greets the people, silence becomes obligatory — no one may speak or act without listening to him and attending to him. He also said: it is not permissible for him to appoint a nurse unless he designates and selects one for that purpose. When the imam recites, listening is the sole obligation; turning away is not permissible. Nor is it permissible for the respondent to prompt the preacher or put questions to him regarding all kinds of knowledge, for he knows how to raise his children.

Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad ibn al-Fadl, may Allah honor him, said in his hidden epistle: the Friday prayer points to Muhammad, peace be upon him and his family, who is the gate of Paradise. It has two invitations — an outward invitation to the exoteric dimension of his Shari'ah, and an inward invitation to the esoteric interpretation of his law. He then said: since prayer on other days consists of four rak'ahs, Friday prayer was made two rak'ahs, with the sermon replacing the other two rak'ahs — consisting of praise, exhortation, supplication, and a statement from the Lord of Manifestation, which is nothing but a warning. The sitting between the two sermons is the separation between the upper light and the lower light. The one who is reminded of this stands and says peace upon the two lights. He then said: do not address the preacher with the authorized call to prayer, so that the muezzin who stands before the pulpit may give the adhan after


<!-- page 166 -->
After the Imam ascended the pulpit, it was said that the call to prayer is given only in the mosque in order to inform the public, and that whoever was in a mosque other than that mosque on that day had erred. If he did so deliberately, he was to be penalized and commanded to perform the Friday ritual washing. Then it is said: "Know the virtue of Friday until God reunites you on Saturday, so that you may rest and remain — or you will be in hardship, if Allah wills." Mawlana Amir al-Mu'minin Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, stated in his four hundred teachings: whoever has a need to petition God should do so in three particular hours — first, on Friday at the hour when the sun begins to set; second, when the wind blows and the gates of heaven open and mercy descends; and third, in the final hour of the night at the break of dawn. At that last hour, two angels call out: "Is there anyone who repents? Is there anyone who seeks forgiveness and is forgiven? Is there anyone who asks for something?" So answer the call of God. He also said that in the Friday prayer, before the bowing of the second rak'ah, the recitation on Friday should be Surah al-Jumu'ah after the Opening in the first rak'ah, and the Opening followed by Surah al-Munafiqun in the second. He said: In all matters of the religious law, there is no other case quite like the following — if a preacher was delivering the Friday sermon and a man entered through the door of the mosque, thereby disrupting the sermon of that preacher and the prayer of those praying with him, what is the ruling? The answer is that the preacher owns that man who entered through the mosque door, and the man became his follower; it is therefore not permissible for the man to come before his master or to pray without him.

<!-- page 167 -->
**Mention of the Two Eid Prayers**

In the first question-and-answer book mentioned, arranged in the order of the chapters of the Book of the Pillars of Islam, the following question is posed: What do you say regarding the statement in religious law that the traveler is not obligated to attend the mosque except in Egypt — our Lord? Does "Tashriq" here refer to the Eid prayer or not? And if someone forgets one or more of the Eid takbirs, what should he do? The answer given is that it is stated in the book on what is rewarded with respect to camels, cows, and the like, that Tashriq takes place only in Egypt — that is, in the mosque of a city — because the two Eid prayers are performed there at sunrise, and such a worshipper is described as radiant. It is also mentioned in the abridgment of the chapter that whoever forgets some of the takbirs should complete them before bowing; if he has passed the point of bowing and is certain he has omitted them, he performs the two prostrations of omission. Question: What do you say about the takbir from the day of Arafah to the last day of Tashriq — it is twenty-three takbirs, yet in our country they add one takbir after the Eid prayer, making it twenty-four. What is the meaning of this? The answer is that the takbir in those days is twenty-three, corresponding in a manner to the five pronouncements of the first intentions aligned with the five prayers, with mention of the spiritual ten hudud, the ten corporeal, and the male of the presiding speaker and his guardian. As for what has passed, take what you have received and be grateful. As for those who pray

<!-- page 168 -->
the Eid prayer while still owing a makeup of the Fajr prayer — the Eid prayer is valid and need not be repeated. The same applies to the funeral prayer. From our master al-Hasan ibn Idris, may God sanctify his soul: whoever prays the Eid prayer before distributing the Zakat al-Fitr need not repeat the prayer, and may give the Zakat al-Fitr afterward. The ritual bath for the two Eids is a Sunnah, and its earliest time is the break of dawn; its latest time is when the Eid prayer ends. Whoever prays the Eid prayer before the end of its time due to a valid excuse, that is permissible for him. It is stated in the abridgment of the Musannaf that the ritual bath for the two Eids is an annual Sunnah, with its time beginning at dawn and ending at the time when the prayer lapses. It is also said in the transmitted reports: "And the qunut is: O God, forgive me, have mercy on me, and pardon me in religion and in life — You are over all things Most Powerful." He said: If the people of the outlying valleys come, together with those travelers who choose to attend, witnessing the Eid is good and meritorious for them, and it is binding upon those who are inhabitants of a city within two leagues or less to witness the prayer. He also said that its congregation consists of five, one of whom is the Imam — as with the Friday prayer — and that it is obligatory in the presence of the custodians, just as Friday is obligatory. Whoever misses it while being a resident may follow it. The two sermons are also prayed in four, and whoever misses a rak'ah of the Eid prayer and joins the second rak'ah with the Imam adds another rak'ah with the additional takbirs. The Sunnah regarding the takbir is that it begins after the completion of the fast, on the night of Eid al-Fitr and the day of al-Fitr, and continues until the prayer ends. Whoever prays the Eid prayer raises his hands with each takbir, and whoever forgets some of the takbirs completes them before bowing. If he has passed the point of bowing and is certain he has omitted them, he performs

<!-- page 169 -->
the two prostrations of omission. It is stated in the book of the Musannaf that the Eid prayer is not performed in Mina. Likewise, our master Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad, may God sanctify his soul, stated in his interpretation of the Pillars — as verified by Sayyiduna Idris ibn Hassan, may God be pleased with his holiness — that the Eid prayer is not performed there in congregation, but is rather prayed individually, each person on his own. In the first question-and-answer book mentioned, it is stated that sexual intercourse with one's wife is permissible on the night of Eid al-Fitr and before the Eid prayer. Our master Ja'far ibn Mansur of Yemen, may God sanctify him, said in the Book of Suckling that it is permissible to fast on the day following Eid al-Fitr, but that it is not permissible to fast after Eid al-Adha in the same way that fasting is permissible after Eid al-Fitr. Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad ibn al-Fadl, may God sanctify him, said in the hidden message that the Eid al-Fitr prayer is described by the term "fitr" in the language of appearance and emergence — for it is said that the earth "fatarat," meaning it cracked and what was within it appeared and came forth; and that the tree "fatarat," meaning its concealed fruit emerged at noon; and that the camel's tusk "fatara," meaning it emerged and appeared. In all these usages, the common thread is that the day is one of the appearance of what was concealed. Breaking the fast is then called "fitr" because fasting is silence and secrecy. God Almighty said in the story of Mary: "I have vowed to the Most Merciful a fast, so I shall not speak to anyone today" — meaning: I will not reveal anything of what I carry within me. Thus "fitr" signifies the appearance of interior realities and hidden knowledge. His saying "and when the sky was split open" similarly means it cracked and what was within it came forth.

<!-- page 170 -->
Another testimony is the new mushroom, and "the fitr of God" means the beginning of His creation of them in one day. Eid al-Fitr is thus the day of the revelation of what had been hidden. He then said: Whoever fasts the days of the month of Ramadan attains al-Fitr, the feast, and divine pleasure; and whoever does not know the meaning of fasting and its proper limits gains nothing from God — as God Almighty said: a Nasibah prays over a blazing fire. The Eid prayer has no call to prayer and no iqama. He then said that the khutbah in Eid al-Fitr comes after the prayer, and that the recitation in it is performed aloud. He said that the Eid al-Adha prayer is held on the day of al-Adha, which is the day of the Hajj. He said that the beginning of the sermon is a matter of dispute, and that the opening of the sermons is proof that the matter appears differently from what was concealed in the depths — for every sermon begins with praise except on the day of sacrifice, which begins with four takbirs.

**Mention of the Prayer of Omission**

In the first question-and-answer book mentioned, arranged in the order of the chapters of the Book of the Pillars of Islam, the following question is posed: What do you say if someone makes room for another to pray — standing in for someone who does not know all the places requiring the prostration of omission in prayer — must that person prostrate the two prostrations of omission after completing every obligatory act in which something appears uncertain to him, or not? The answer is: if it is established that he has indeed committed an omission, he prostrates for it; otherwise he does not. Question: What do you say regarding the statement in the Book of Purity that whoever errs

<!-- page 171 -->
by not pronouncing the opening takbir, or by leaving it deliberately or out of ignorance, must repeat the prayer? O our master, if a man forgets to open the prayer after every two rak'ahs in the four- and six-rak'ah prayers, does that invalidate his prayer or not? The answer is: if he did not sit after each pair of rak'ahs, his prayer is invalidated. Question: What do you say regarding the statement in the Book of Purity that whoever inadvertently adds to his prayer — even without sitting for the tashahhud — must repeat the prayer? And if he adds an extra bowing, he repeats the prayer; but if he adds an extra prostration, he completes the prayer and performs the two prostrations of omission. What does this mean? The answer is: if he adds a bowing and has not sat for the tashahhud, he must repeat the prayer. But if he has sat for the tashahhud and completed two rak'ahs — whether with an additional bowing or prostration — he has done so in order. Question: What do you say about a man who is praying behind an Imam and neglects one of the pillars of the prayer while following the Imam — how is his prayer? The answer is: the Imam bears the responsibility for the omission, except in the case of the bowing and the opening takbir, and the first thing is that he should not neglect those. Question: What do you say regarding the statement in religious law that whoever forgets a prostration should prostrate after delivering the salaam when he remembers — O our master, how does he prostrate after having left the prayer? The answer is: he prostrates if he remembers before delivering the salaam; if he has already left the prayer, he has no recourse. Question: What does our master say about a man who prayed the obligatory prayer and recited it in reverse order, from bottom to top — must he perform the two prostrations of omission or not? The answer is: there is nothing wrong with that, and it is not obligatory upon him to perform

<!-- page 172 -->
the two prostrations of omission. Question: A man prayed some of the obligatory Zuhr prayer and sat down and recited the first tashahhud, then prayed two more rak'ahs and recited the tashahhud again, then recalled that he had prayed only three rak'ahs — what should he do? The answer is: he prays the rak'ah he missed after the salaam, reciting in it the Opening and a surah because it is one of the first two rak'ahs; he then recites the tashahhud, delivers the salaam, and performs the two prostrations of omission. Question: Whoever neglects the first tashahhud in the obligatory prayer but recites the final tashahhud and then remembers before delivering the salaam — must he perform the prostration of omission or not? The answer is: there is no explicit text on this, and it has not been stipulated that the worshipper must prostrate. Question: Regarding the man who repeats a single surah in the obligatory prayer after the Opening — must he perform the prostration of omission or not? The answer is: this too has not been stipulated. Question: Regarding a man who omits one obligation — should he prostrate two prostrations of omission, or if he omits three obligations, does he prostrate separately for each omission with two prostrations, or just two prostrations shared among all of them? The answer is: the prostration of omission is not multiplied. Question: Regarding a man who prays behind an Imam and makes it clear that he intends in his prayer to be a follower — if the Imam delivers the salaam of the dhikr yet he did not intend the prayer, is his prayer valid or not? The answer is: if the Imam intends the prayer on his behalf, then it is so; if he does not, the prayer lapses. Question: What is transmitted from Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon them, that whoever forgets the recitation in some of the prayers and recites it inadequately in what remains —

<!-- page 173 -->
if he completes the recitation and finishes the bowing, prostration, and takbir, must he repeat the prayer? The answer is: this presents no difficulty. Question: If he forgets to recite some of the Quran or all of it, must he perform the two prostrations of omission or not? The answer is: the two prostrations of omission are required, and he performs them. Question: What do you say about a man who has preceded the Imam in some part of the prayer — must that person who preceded perform the two prostrations of omission that the Imam himself had to perform before the latecomer arrived? The answer is: whoever enters the prayer prostrates with the Imam for the Imam's omission, even if the only part he caught with the Imam before the salaam was the opening takbir. Question: In the case of the one who has preceded the Imam in prayer and then prays some prayers with the Imam, then makes an error in what remains of it — must he prostrate for the omission or not? The answer is: if he omits something while praying on his own, he prostrates. Question: It is stated in the text that deeds are accepted only with intentions — so if a person neglects to form the intention when purifying himself, or when beginning his prayer, or when fasting, or when performing any of the duties of God, and then contracts the intention verbally at the moment he resolves in his heart to act — is his action valid or not? The answer is: the intention resides in the heart, and what is said with the tongue is merely a verbal expression of it, as mentioned in the Pillars; if he has made the intention in his heart, then he has fulfilled it. He said in the councils of instruction that doubt in prayer is rooted in the prostration of omission, which causes bewilderment — a word that applies to many of the agreed cases that people think resemble an omission but in fact do not, because the one who commits an omission is certain of what he has omitted, while the doubter is not certain of what he doubts. He said in the transmitted reports, when it was said that he should prostrate —


<!-- page 174 -->
Regarding the prostration of omission: it is performed after the salām. After it, one recites a brief testimony: "In the name of Allah, and Allah is great, and I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, and may Allah bless Muḥammad and his family." It is also said: Whoever experiences doubt during prayer has not thereby omitted any obligatory act, whether he is leading prayer or praying a supererogatory prayer. If he has already stood up for what was written and then realizes he has been excused and believes it to be a voluntary prayer, he should treat it as such. It is recorded that this does not harm him through his negligence, but if he rises to perform a voluntary prayer, it counts as voluntary. It is also said: There is no doubt concerning whether he prayed Ẓuhr or ʿAṣr if what remains of ʿAṣr time is sufficient for him to pray, but if the sun sets and he then doubts, there is no need to make it up. It is said: If he doubts about the Fajr prayer, he should pray it; and if he doubts about Maghrib, he should pray it. It is said: Whoever repeats the same prayer without cause does not need to perform the prostration of omission. It is mentioned in the book Mukhtaṣar al-Muṣannaf that if a man prays and then rises at the fifth position, having already witnessed the fourth, he should salute while standing and depart. If he did not witness it, he should sit, give the testimony, and then prostrate the two prostrations of omission. This applies when it is remembered that he rose to a fifth rakʿah after having already bowed, having witnessed the fourth — in that case his prayer is sound, and the additional rakʿah becomes a voluntary addition for him. Even if he did not witness the fourth and did not sit the length of the testimony, he should repeat the prayer. Whoever forgets a prayer and does not know whether he missed two, three, or four prayers should follow the imam in praying aloud what is prayed silently, for his prayer is complete. If he does so deliberately, he must perform the prostration of omission, even if he is not the
170.
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 175 -->
imam but is praying on his own. If he prays aloud in what is prayed silently out of uncertainty, there is no obligation upon him. Wherever the matter of omission arises in the Ḥaḍrat prayer, its ruling applies equally to travel. It is mentioned in the questions addressed to certain scholars of the islands of al-Hind and in their letters to some preachers of the island of Yemen — may Allah sanctify their souls — specifically the twenty-sixth issue, concerning a man who neglects the obligatory recitation after the first rakʿah and recites a sūrah from the beginning in the second rakʿah without obligation in the obligatory prayer: does he have to perform the two prostrations of omission or not? The answer is that this is neither stipulated nor deliberate. It is also mentioned in the first issue: deeds are only accepted with intention. If a person neglects, when purifying himself, or in prayer, fasting, or any obligatory act of Allah, to form the intention with the tongue while exerting effort to hold it in the heart — is his action valid or not? And if the tongue-utterance of intention is omitted, is the action still valid or not? The answer is: the intention resides in the heart, and what is uttered on the tongue is merely a statement, as mentioned in the Pillars. If the intention is contracted in the heart, it has been fulfilled. It is mentioned in the book on the interpretation of the Sharīʿah that whoever is obligated to pray or has forgotten it should pray it when he remembers it. Our master, the owner of the Mustanṣiriyya councils — may God's peace rest upon its owner — said in the ninth council: Listen now to what we recite, so that you understand the meaning of doubt and omission in prayer. The places of omission in prayer are seven, for six of which the prostration of omission is appropriate — those being the two prostrations
p. 171.
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 176 -->
They are called as such because they force the devil's nose down when he causes forgetfulness to occur. The cases of omission are as follows: 1 — Whoever neglects to prostrate and remembers it should perform the two prostrations of omission. 2 — Whoever forgets to sit for the first tashahhud and stands for the third rakʿah: if he had not yet knelt, he sits, gives the testimony, and when he salutes, he performs the prostrations of omission. 3 — Even if the omission exceeds the first tashahhud to the point where it accumulates, he performs the prostration and completes the prayer in the most complete manner, and upon him are the two prostrations of omission. 4 — Whoever neglects the second tashahhud before the salutation is required to perform the two prostrations of omission. 5 — Whoever, through inattention, prays only two rakʿahs and then prays two more after them, must perform the two prostrations of omission. 6 — Whoever, through inattention, rises from the final tashahhud after completing it to add a fifth rakʿah must perform the two prostrations of omission. 7 — Whoever is following an imam bears no responsibility for omission; the imam bears that. These are the seven places of omission for which the two prostrations are valid. As for doubt in prayer, it is a matter that many who agree on it think resembles omission, but it is not so, because the one who omits is certain of what he has neglected, whereas the one who doubts is not certain of what he doubts. Doubt arises across twelve situations, in some of which there is a prostration of omission and in others there is none. The cases requiring prostration of omission are: 1 — If the worshipper doubts whether he has bowed while still in prayer, he should bow, and the prostration of omission is upon him. 2 — If he doubts and does not know whether he has prayed one rakʿah or two, he acts on the lesser certainty. 3 — Similarly, if he doubts whether he has prayed two rakʿahs or three, he acts on
p. 172
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 177 -->
certainty. 4 — If he doubts whether he has prayed three rakʿahs or four, he sits and performs the two prostrations of omission. 5 — If he doubts whether he has prayed two or four rakʿahs, he salutes, then prays two rakʿahs standing, and performs the two prostrations of omission. As for the cases in which there is nothing required: 6 — If the worshipper doubts whether he made the opening takbīr after having already bowed, he continues his prayer and nothing is required of him. 7 — If he doubts whether he recited after bowing. 8 — If he doubts whether he bowed after prostrating. 9 — If he doubts about the first prostration after the second. 10 — Similarly, if he doubts about prostrating after the tashahhud. 11 — If he doubts about something after the salutation. None of these cases require anything. Mawlānā Amīr al-Muʾminīn Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred manners: Whoever is upon certainty and is then visited by doubt should proceed upon his certainty, for doubt does not displace certainty nor diminish it. It is mentioned in the questions addressed to certain scholars of India — may Allah be pleased with them — and in the letters to our loyalists, the preachers of Yemen — may Allah sanctify them and grant us their intercession and forgive them: Question: What do you say about a man who deliberately expresses his intention verbally? Is his prayer valid or not? Answer: If the imam deliberately does so, his prayer is void; if he does not say it deliberately, it stands. It is mentioned in the book of questions: Whoever doubts in his prayer and does not know what he has prayed should act on certainty.
173
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 178 -->
