He performs the two prostrations of omission after the salutation. If he is certain of nothing and does not know how many rakʿahs he has missed, he should restart the prayer from the beginning, and the same applies to the imam.

**Mention of What Interrupts Prayer**

It is mentioned in the first question-and-answer book — arranged according to the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — that whoever is praying in a state of purity and impurity then reaches his garment: if water is nearby he washes it; otherwise his prayer is invalidated, so he washes his garment and then prays. Whoever encounters this before the tashahhud must repeat the prayer. If it occurs after he says, "Peace be upon you, O Prophet, and the mercy and blessings of Allah, peace be upon us and upon the righteous servants of Allah," his prayer is permissible and he does not repeat it. It is mentioned in the book of purity that whoever is afflicted with a nosebleed or vomiting while praying: if he is able to wash the blood of the nosebleed and stop it, and rinse away the vomit, without turning his face away from the qibla, he does so and continues building on his prayer. If he turns away from the qibla, he repeats the prayer from the beginning. It is mentioned in the reports: if one perceives a smell or hears the sound of passing wind, or another person hears it from him — meaning the event of ritual impurity — it is mentioned in the book on the interpretation of prayer that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said: "Three things interrupt the prayer: a woman, a donkey, and a black dog." The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, was standing in prayer, and a dog passed before him, then a donkey, then a woman passed by while he was praying.
p. 174.
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 179 -->
He said, "I saw what you saw, yet nothing interrupts the prayer of a believer." Do what you are able. Mawlānā Amīr al-Muʾminīn Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred manners: Smiling does not interrupt prayer, but laughter does. It is mentioned in the book of questions — from the late Sheikh Shīmūn ibn Muḥammad Ghūrī al-Hindī — and the answer is from our master Badr al-Dīn Ḥasan ibn Idrīs, may Allah sanctify them both and grant us their intercession and forgive them. Question: What do you say about a man who enters into the obligatory prayer with the opening takbīr and begins reciting, and then the imam arrives and the man steps forward to stand, and when the imam bows he bows with him without specifying a new opening takbīr — what is the state of his prayer? Answer: He must repeat it.

**Mention of the Prayer of One Who Has Been Preceded in Some Rakʿahs**

It is mentioned in the first question-and-answer book — arranged according to the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — Question: What do you say about a worshipper who joins the imam during the final tashahhud of one of the five prayers, intending to follow the imam in that prayer? He sits with the imam during his sitting, and when the imam salutes and completes his prayer, the latecomer rises and completes his missed rakʿahs. Has the latecomer attained the congregational prayer, and is his prayer valid or not? Answer: If he joins during the final tashahhud, the tashahhud is
175
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 180 -->
the concluding limit of prayer. He intends to pray as a follower, sits and prays the supplication, and when the imam salutes, he rises and completes his prayer, having thereby attained the congregational prayer. It is mentioned in a book: In the summary of the classification, if a person joins the imam while he is bowing, he says the opening takbīr and then the takbīr of bowing, and that rakʿah is counted for him. If he fears missing it, he is rewarded with one takbīr for both the opening and bowing. Whoever joins the imam having been preceded should enter with him in whatever state he is in. What he builds upon is counted for him; otherwise he is a volunteer, and he prays what he manages to catch and makes up what he missed. It is mentioned in the reports regarding the imam: if something happens before him, and he does not know what the imam missed before him, he continues upon his certainty until he is informed by someone behind him. He then returns to the correct position. Our master and Mawlānā Muḥammad ibn Fahd — may Allah sanctify him — said in his honorable epistle, bearing the title From Him: With Love for the Path of Guidance for the Converted — a question I also asked of Allah Almighty, seeking His pleasure: concerning the latecomer in prayer who is unable to form the intention by uttering it with his tongue and enters with the imam in whatever state he is in — is any of his prayer, including its rakʿahs, valid? The answer: You too must pray in this, with the help of Allah Almighty, for it is true and correct, as it has come down in the books of the saints of Allah, peace be upon them, that if the latecomer joins the imam during any one of the rakʿahs of his prayer, having not yet raised his head from bowing in it, he says a single takbīr and enters with him in his prayer. He thereby attains that rakʿah with him, and with it he has formed the intention in his heart to do so.
176
Scanned with CamScanner

<!-- page 181 -->
**Mention of the Age at Which Boys Are Commanded to Pray**

Mawlānā Amīr al-Muʾminīn Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib said in his four hundred manners: Teach your children what Allah will benefit you with, and command your children to pray and accompany them in it when they reach the age of eight years. Our master the great preacher Ibrāhīm ibn Ḥusayn al-Ḥāmidī — may Allah sanctify his soul — said in the book The Phrase of Good News: The Commander of the Believers said, may Allah's prayers be upon him: the boy is formed over seven years, reaches fourteen, and completes his length, breadth, and depth at twenty-one years, and completes his mind at twenty-eight years. It is mentioned briefly: separate them in their beds at this stage, as a precaution for them when they are close to one another and close to puberty and the stirring of desire and intercourse, lest they become involved in that. If they are close to one another and there is concern, they should be disciplined accordingly.

**Mention of the Traveller's Prayer**

It is mentioned in the first question-and-answer book — arranged according to the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — Question: What do you say about a man who has two houses, one in Aḥmadābād and the other in Naharwāla, and he lives some days in Aḥmadābād and some days in Naharwāla. Is it permissible for his prayer to be completed until
177
Scanned with CamScanner


<!-- page 182 -->
Should he enter the borders of the country where he is staying? Answer: If he stays five prayer times with his wife or with his mahram relatives, he should complete his prayer and fast.

Question: What do you say about what is mentioned in the Book of Purity — that one whose journey was merely a leisurely outing should not shorten the prayer? What is meant by a "picnic journey"? Answer: If a man owns a great deal of land and enjoys walking through it for pleasure, that does not justify shortening the prayer.

Question: What do you say about a man who prays the Sunnah and nafl prayers aboard a ship as it rotates — must he turn with it or not? Answer: He should establish the direction of prayer at the moment he enters into it. If the ship turns on its own, there is nothing upon him. If he is able to turn with it and reorient himself, that is better. If he is in an obligatory prayer, he should do whatever he is able.

Question: What do you say about a slave who is authorized to trade — whether or not on his master's behalf — during the month of Ramadan or at another time: should he shorten the prayer and break his fast or not? Answer: Yes, he shortens his prayer and breaks his fast.

Question: What do you say about the divorced women in India? If someone travels with them — for trade or otherwise — in the month of Ramadan or at another time, should he pray and fast as a resident or as a traveler? The same question applies if someone travels with a non-Muslim. Answer: If their intention was to travel with him, they pray and fast in full. If they set out for a different purpose, their ruling follows that of travelers.

Question: What do you say regarding the three categories that Allah has forbidden in the Qur'an — the ten degrees of kinship in marriage — with respect to those one travels with and those with whom one prays

<!-- page 183 -->
and fasts — are these mahram relatives? Answer: Anyone who is a close relative through the womb is one for whom the traveler prays and fasts in full, standing for the five prayers wherever he happens to be. This also applies to a man's wife; it does not apply, however, to the wife's relatives or to those related through nursing.

Question: Someone who travels from Nahrwala to Ahmedabad and meets a relative along the way outside the city — should he pray in full or not? Answer: He should not pray in full until after he has entered the borders of the city where he has a relative. As for traveling from his home to stay in a city or in the home of relatives, or stopping for a day or more — he should not shorten the prayer for as long as he remains there. If he departs and then returns, or stops for a day or more, he again does not shorten the prayer.

The owner of a boat and merchants all pray in full if the journey is no more than from Sofa to Aden, and nothing else applies to them. Nor should one be compelled to repeat the prayer after reaching his family.

Separately: Whoever travels and, when the prayer time arrives, finds the sky heavily overcast and cannot determine the qibla — he should pray facing four directions, one in each direction for each obligatory prayer. Allah Almighty excuses His servant when He afflicts him with hardship. Even if he later prays again when the sun appears and the qibla becomes clear, and the time for prayer has not yet expired, he should return to perform the prayer. If a traveler descends in a place and intends to stay, then two or more days pass, he begins that stay anew, and it is not obligatory for a woman traveling to repeat it. Whoever enters Makkah should pray in full and not shorten, regardless of whether he stops for an hour or a day — for the sake of Allah — whether or not he is engaged in worship there. Our master said: Isma'il ibn Sayyidina Hiba said that prayer is performed only in the sanctuary of the House of God.

<!-- page 184 -->
Makkah is a place where prayer is not shortened. Question: What do you say about the wandering sheikhs — such as Sheikh Abdullah ibn Nuh — who travel among villages for the sake of the believing community, where some villages are close together and others are far apart, sometimes more than a prayer's distance: should he shorten his prayer and break his fast or not? Answer: His case is like that of a prince who goes out to govern his emirate — he is not required to shorten the prayer or break the fast. This is the answer of our master Idris, may God sanctify his soul.

Question: About a traveler who reaches a town just before sunset — should he remain with the people until the day after sunrise, and if so, is that counted as a day and a night or not? Answer: If he intends to stay until he prays on the second day, he should pray and fast in full.

Question: The relatives who complete the prayer and fasting for the traveler — are they the ones whom Allah Almighty mentioned in the Qur'an when He said: "Forbidden to you are your mothers, your daughters, your sisters, your paternal aunts, your maternal aunts, your brother's daughters, and your sister's daughters" — and what corresponds to these verses for men? And do the mothers of wives, stepmothers, and relatives through nursing complete the prayer and fasting for the traveler or not? Answer: The relatives — both male and female — who complete the prayer and fasting are those connected through the womb. The mothers of wives and those related through the male line do not.

Separately: A man who enters the gate of a city or village returning from travel during the month of Ramadan — if the sun has not yet set before he enters his house — his prayers and fasting are performed in full. Whoever travels and reaches the village where his brother-in-law resides, or

<!-- page 185 -->
his sister-in-law — he shortens the prayer. But if a woman comes to her father's house yet does not actually live there, having stayed as a resident or in the home of a mahram relative, and she is a minor who has not yet reached adulthood — or a child yet unborn — the prayer is performed for her in full.

Whoever travels fewer than two postal stages and intends to stay, and the prayer time comes upon him on the way, he prays in full and does not shorten. And whoever travels and reaches a postal station intending to return, then returns to his family, and the prayer time comes upon him on the way — he prays in full and does not shorten.

Question: As is stated in the shari'ah, a man coming from a journey enters the road and prays in full with us — our master — and Kanpur, Sarnak Pur, and the other outlying districts of Ahmedabad: are all of these collectively considered one jurisdiction permitting a homeward-bound traveler to pray in full? Answer: A traveler returning home may not pray in full until he enters his own house. However, if he enters one of the city gates but has not yet reached his house, and fears missing the prayer, he may pray shortened. And if he leaves his house while still in the city, without having passed through any of its gates, and the prayer time comes upon him while he is still within the city, he prays in full, as stated and established in the concise book concerning the effects of the classification of our master al-Nu'man, may God sanctify his soul, which states that the traveler should break his fast and shorten his prayer from the moment he leaves his house and begins to travel, until he returns and enters his house — if his travel is blameless. However, if along the way he encounters a mahram relative whose home is not at a full travel distance from his own country, and he resides in that country — meaning the one who is a mahram to that traveler — then his prayer is completed in full.

Question: If a man intends to stay in a house for ten days and then needs to leave after four or five days have passed —

<!-- page 186 -->
a need having arisen that he could not put off — what is his status regarding prayer and fasting? Answer: As long as he still holds in his heart the intention to stay, he should pray and fast in full. And when does he intend to travel and leave the place of his stay? When he is on his way to travel, he shortens his prayer once he has resolved to do so and committed to it.

Question: If a traveler leaves a house where he had been staying for ten days and moves to another house nearby — only a mile away — does he need to form a new intention to stay for ten full days, or does the original intention for ten days carry over? Answer: As long as he intends to return to the place where he had originally intended to stay, he prays and fasts in full, even if he left it with the intention of staying elsewhere. Moving to the other place does not nullify anything — he need only renew his intention to stay there for ten days.

Question: If a traveler has family in a village and enters the far side of the village or a house in it, but has not yet reached his family and relatives — should he pray in full or shortened? Answer: The prayer is not performed in full until the mahram agrees to host him, if he is residing with a mahram. This holds for five prayer times, unless he intends to stay for ten days. So when he arrives in the village with the intention of staying, he prays and fasts in full.

These three answers are from our master Idris ibn Hassan, may Allah sanctify him. He also said in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf: If the traveler prays in full — whether forgetfully or deliberately — and there is still time, he must repeat the prayer, even if the time has passed. Every prayer or prostration that is obligatory upon a person while he is

<!-- page 187 -->
on the ground — it does not count for him if he performs it as a rider. And every prayer or prostration that is obligatory upon him while he is riding — if he performs it on the ground, it is valid and accepted for him. As for the Sacred Mosque, there is no shortening in it, whether one is in a state of spiritual engagement or simply present there.

He also said in the Mukhtasar al-Athar: The scholars said that the traveler should pray the Sunnah and nafl prayers. If he is walking during the day and the time for prayer comes while he is walking, he prays the obligatory prayer as two rak'ahs and defers the Sunnah until he dismounts at night, making it up with the night prayer.

From the same source: A mail is four leagues; a farsakh is three miles; and a mil is three thousand cubits — that is, one thousand moderate paces measured by one leg, from where the right foot is raised to where it is placed down. Even if the destination he intends to reach is only one mail away and there are no people or shops there, and he intends to return within ten days — he shortens and breaks his fast until he departs. The traveler breaks his fast and shortens the prayer from the moment he leaves his house and begins to travel, until he returns and enters his house — if his travel is blameless. However, if he encounters a mahram relative at a distance from his country that does not reach the full travel distance, while that relative resides in that country — meaning the one who is a mahram to that traveler — then his prayer is completed in full.

He also said in the Mukhtasar al-Athar that praying on the road is forbidden. He said in the commentary on the Pillars that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, used to pray on his way to Medina, inclining his head for prostration, with his mount behind him. He also said in the questions addressed to some of the scholars of the borders of

<!-- page 188 -->
al-Hind and in letters to some of the preachers of the peninsula of Yemen — may God sanctify their souls, all of them:

The Thirty-Fifth Issue: Regarding a traveler who arrives to find the resident imam in prayer, joins for two rak'ahs and then says salaam — is the imam's prayer fixed in this case or not? Answer: It is stipulated that if I wish to leave after two rak'ahs, I may depart with two rak'ahs. If I wait for the imam to say salaam, there is no harm in that.

The question was also asked: What do you say about an imam who leads people in prayer aboard a ship — sitting because the ship's movement makes it impossible otherwise — while some of the congregation are also unable to pray standing, yet others are able? Answer: It is permissible given the circumstances of the ship, if they are not able to manage otherwise. If he is rushed, he should pray alone.

The question was also asked: What do you say about the Qur'an and other books — should they be carried during travel and in settled life or not? The common people narrate from the Prophet, peace be upon him, that he said: "Do not carry the Qur'an" — what is the ruling? Answer: The Qur'an should be placed on the head out of honor and reverence for it, not otherwise.

Our master and Mawlana Ja'far ibn our master Sulayman — may Allah Most High sanctify them in the chambers of the heavens — said in answer to questions submitted to him by Sayyidina Ali ibn Sulayman Qas, the author of this book, and his servant who answered them — from the island of Yemen to the island of India — among the things I asked and stated: What do you say about a minor who prays behind a resident imam, who sits after completing two rak'ahs while the imam rises to continue the prayer, and there is no one behind the imam except the minor — should the imam call out the takbir for the remaining prayer or not? The answer is: There is no obligation upon the imam to call out the takbir aloud. He fears doing so, but his voice serves the purpose only for one who can be trusted to follow him in bowing, prostrating, and standing. When that trust is absent, calling out aloud serves no purpose.

<!-- page 189 -->
So he understood that. Our master Ja'far ibn Mansur of Yemen, may God sanctify him, said in the Book of Sub-Nursing on the Sunnah of shortening: the three prayers that are shortened are the first — meaning Zuhr — and Asr and Isha, each of which is obligatory to pray as four rak'ahs. The praise is ten rak'ahs, and shortening reduces each four-rak'ah prayer to two, making six rak'ahs shortened in total.

He also said in the questions of Sheikh al-Hasan ibn Nuh to our master Idris ibn Hassan, may God sanctify their souls, and in his answers to him:

The Eleventh Issue: Should a man travel with his son's wife or with his wife's mother without a barrier between them, speak to them, bury one of them, and travel with her? And what is the status of nursing relatives in this regard? Answer: It is not necessary to impose restrictions if there is no one else from her womb available. Allah knows best. Yet the first and most important course is to be cautious about these matters for the reasons mentioned — on account of the saying of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and his noble family, that one should leave what is permissible in order to be safe from falling into what is not. If one of their relatives is present — even if not a womb relative — that relative makes all of the above permissible. Allah knows best what is right.

Our master Muhammad ibn Fahd, may God sanctify his soul, said in his treatise entitled "The Love of the Path of Guidance for Those Who Have Converted": Question 17 — I asked my brother about the traveler who travels the distance of a mile and stays there for eight days, returning on the ninth day:


<!-- page 190 -->
The question is whether he shortens his prayer or completes it, given what is stated in the books of the purified Sharīʿah — may God's peace be upon its founder and his family and his prayers — that if a traveler covers a postal distance in the round trip, the shortening of prayer applies, since the going and returning together constitute two legs. It is also stated that the maximum permitted length of stay is ten days. The answer is as follows:

With the help of God Almighty, what is stipulated in the books of the guardians of God — peace be upon them — is that the stay referred to independently is ten days, neither less nor more. The first case applies to one who has traveled a distance of one mile and intends to return after a few days: if he intends to stay for ten days, the prayer is completed. If it appears that he will return before ten days have elapsed, he has returned and is not required to fulfill the full ten-day stay as stipulated in the books of the saints of God — peace be upon them — and this does not invalidate his completing the prayers mentioned above.

I asked about the prayer of a traveler praying alongside residents, and it was said that this is permissible if the imam is one of the virtuous and he prays two rakʿahs, whereupon those behind him stand and complete their prayers; and this ruling is specific to people of the higher ranks, not others.

I asked about the prayer of the one who completes his prayer — the *mutamim* — and it was said that this is permissible for people of the higher ranks, as was narrated from our master and Mawlānā Dāʿī al-Zamān Jaʿfar ibn our master Sulaymān, may God sanctify his soul, when he was in the Yemeni island and descended from

<!-- page 191 -->
a noble castle to the land of Ḥarāz. May the Highest sanctify him — he performed the noon prayer of one of his days in a state of purity like everyone else, but he did not agree with that, because the Highest — may God sanctify him — was the one who investigated this and other matters, and was not prepared to investigate purity alongside the purity of others. When he completed tayammum in a way no one noticed, he prayed with the people in congregation. He then said to some of those close to his invitation to clarify the matter: this is how the dispensation arose for praying the prayer of the one who performs ablution, when it is obligatory for some important reason that serves the good of the call, its tranquility, and the establishment of its order — just as there is a dispensation for the traveler to pray with the residents.

It is said in the book *Anas al-Munibeh* regarding Qaḍāʾ — meaning the Commander of the Believers, may God's peace be upon him — that there are six categories of people who do not shorten their prayers or fasting: 1) the collectors who are engaged in their rounds of collection; 2) the merchant who travels in his trade from one market to another; 3) the prince who is within his emirate; 4) the man who goes out in pursuit of hunting; 5) the shepherd who seeks pastures and grazing grounds; 6) the warrior who cuts off the road.

It is said in the book of the question of Sheikh Shamūn ibn Muḥammad Ghūrī al-Hindī, and the answer is from our master Badr al-Dīn Ḥasan ibn Idrīs — may God be pleased with him — who said: You raise the question of certain sheikhs regarding a woman who is a close relative traveling with someone, and whether one who does not shorten the prayer has all five prayers established through his presence. His answer was that whoever is one of the guardians by kinship, then the traveler prays

<!-- page 192 -->
and fasts by virtue of that person's presence, and has the five prayers. The relationship with a wife is established, whereas it is not valid through the relation of a woman by kinship alone or through the relation of breastfeeding. So clarify for us, O Mawlānā, the details of who travels with one of them, touches him by hand or otherwise, and who does not shorten his prayer — in detail. He said: A man who is married to his wife — in this matter the footing of those who err has been removed. Among you, who is saved? The best of you are those who reflect on matters of religion and contemplate them. Answer: It is only applicable when one of his maḥrams is present in a place, and the traveler has all five prayer times established through that presence. The same applies to his wife if she is staying with him through all five prayer times.

It is said in the questions addressed to certain sheikhs of India, with written replies from some of our loyal preachers of Yemen — may God sanctify them: Question: What do you say — does a resident pray with travelers, and does a traveler pray with residents? Answer: If the traveler is the imam of the residents and prays two rakʿahs, they stand and complete the prayer. Likewise, if the resident is the imam of the travelers and they depart after two rakʿahs.

**On the Prayer of the Sick**

It is said in the first-mentioned book of questions and answers, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*: Question: What do you say about a sick person who is unable to pray standing, or to stand while bowing and prostrating — how should he proceed?

<!-- page 193 -->
Answer: He should pray standing, or standing with bowing and prostrating, according to whatever God has excused the servant from in his condition. It is said in the book on purity: if he is unable to do so, he nods with his eyesight; and if he cannot do that either, he nods with his heart. It is said in the abridged book of *Āthār*: if a sick person is unable to pray beyond the obligatory prayer, he may make up — if he wishes — what he missed of the Sunnah and voluntary prayers once he recovers; that is a virtue and a reward, but it is not obligatory to make them up as it is obligatory to make up the obligatory prayers. It is said in the abridged classified book: whoever takes something to cure himself and thereby loses his mind must make up whatever prayers he missed while in that state — whether his action was within the character permitted by God or was disobedient.

**On the Eclipse Prayer**

It is said in the first-mentioned book of questions and answers, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*: Question: Regarding the eclipse that occurred in the year eight hundred and thirty-seven, in the month of Dhū al-Qaʿdah — it began on Monday, two hours after sunrise, and a solar eclipse occurred at midday. It is stated in the letters of the Brethren of Purity, in a treatise on astronomy, that a solar eclipse only occurs at the end of the month. Yet this one, and the cause of

<!-- page 194 -->
it, had occurred many times before this year while we were fasting in the first days of Ramaḍān and the eclipse happened — so the believers were confused as to how to understand this. Answer: It has been narrated from Mawlānā the author of the letters — may God's peace be upon him — a statement on this, and it has been explained by the pure Imams and those who follow their guidance. They said that a lunar eclipse occurs on the night of the fifteenth, and on the morning of the tenth, while a solar eclipse may occur at the beginning of the month or at the end of the month — the matter is in the hands of God, and there is no power except in God, the Most High, the Great.

Whoever joins the imam at the third rakʿah follows him through his first prayer, bows with the imam when he bows, and prostrates when he prostrates. When the imam rises from prostration, he stands with him and prays the five rakʿahs following him, prostrating with him. When the imam makes the final testimony, the latecomer sits and waits. When the imam says the salutation, the latecomer stands and completes the remaining rakʿahs, reciting the qunut, bowing, prostrating, making the final testimony, and saying the salutation — and he does not count the first two prostrations that he performed with the imam, just as one who catches up with the imam during the prostration of the final obligatory prayers does not count them.

For whoever misses the eclipse prayer with the imam and joins at the beginning of the imam's prayer before the first two prostrations: he prays what remains with the imam. When the imam prostrates in the first two prostrations, he prostrates with him, since it is mentioned that the one who joins at the beginning but is unable follows along. If the imam prays the five rakʿahs with him, and he prostrates and stands with the imam, and when the imam prostrates he prostrates with him, and when the imam sits the latecomer sits. When the imam says the salutation, the latecomer prays what remains of the rakʿahs,

<!-- page 195 -->
prostrates, makes the final testimony, and says the salutation — and the first two prostrations in which he prostrated with the imam are not counted, just as is the case for one who has already prayed and joins during the prostration of the obligatory prayers.

It is said in the book on purity: do not pray the eclipse prayer until the eclipse is actually seen; it is not prayed on the word of an astrologer or anyone else. If the eclipse is obscured by clouds or something else so that it cannot be seen with the naked eye, the prayer is not performed.

**On the Prayer of Fear**

It is said in the first-mentioned book of questions and answers, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*: Question: What do you say — it has come in the book on purity that when the situation involves danger, conflict, and combat, they perform their prayers with takbīr, enlarging the place of each takbīr. O our master, do they shorten the takbīr as they shorten the prayer of fear? Answer: They shorten the takbīr just as shortening is obligatory in it. It is said in the book *Mukhtaṣar al-Muṣannaf*: if the imam prays the prayer of fear with three people as his guard, and their number falls below three, it is not recommended for them to pray the prayer of fear, so that the group does not fall to fewer than three. If the imam combines the eclipse prayer and the funeral prayer with the prayer of fear, he prays the funeral prayer and then prays the eclipse prayer and the prayer of fear.

<!-- page 196 -->
**On the Istisqāʾ Prayer**

It is said in the book *al-Iqtiṣār*, *al-Akhbār*, and *al-Muntakhabah*: he faced the people and said one hundred takbīrs, then he turned to his right and said one hundred praises, then he turned to his left and said one hundred expressions of joy — raising his voice throughout all of this. He then faced the people and praised God with one hundred praises and glorified Him. It is said in the book on purity: he also delivered two sermons, as is done for the two feasts, then descended and departed. He dismisses them, and he performs the prayer in a bow — in Arabic, if he finds the Arabic — as the Messenger of God, peace and blessings be upon him, did. If they go out on a day other than Monday, that is also acceptable. It is said in the abridgement of *al-Muṣannaf*: whoever delivers the sermon before the prayer should pray and then deliver the khuṭbah.

**On the Prayers of al-Witr and Fajr and the Qunūt**

It is said in the first-mentioned book of questions and answers, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *Pillars of Islam*: Question: About a man who forgets the qunūt? Answer: In Witr he must repeat it, and in Fajr he performs the prostration of omission.

Question: What do you say about a man who prays Witr and forgets the qunūt, then remembers after he has bowed, prostrated, or sat — should he repeat the Witr or perform the prostration of omission? Answer: The qunūt is a supplication that must not be deliberately abandoned. Whoever forgets it is not required to repeat the prayer, and there is no prostration of omission in the Sunnah prayers. Whoever deliberately omits it in Witr repeats it.

<!-- page 197 -->
The minimum qunūt is three tasbīḥs or three takbīrs. Question: When should the qunūt be recited in Witr? Answer: It should be recited after the worshipper says, "Our Lord, to You belongs all praise" — and whoever achieves that has fulfilled it.

Whoever prays the *shafāʿah* prayer — two rakʿahs — and then engages in something until dawn arrives should repeat the shafāʿah as a make-up prayer. Even if he prayed three rakʿahs and remained sitting until dawn breaks, he does not repeat the three prayers but remains sitting with the intention of making up the prayers.

Question: Is it permissible for a worshipper to pray from the first rakʿah of Witr, speaking or connecting the iḥrām to the iḥrām? It has been said that the prayer of one who is seated is half the prayer of one who is standing. It has also been said that the prayer is begun by the takbīr and concluded by the salutation — so which of these does he act upon? Answer: Our master the Judge al-Nuʿmān — the highest of the world, may God sanctify him — mentioned in *al-Mukhtār*:

The thread of prayer may be broken by the salutation
Without any actions or words.
If one must speak, there is no harm in that, unless the speech is prolonged.

Question: Is it permissible to perform the shafāʿah prayer and Witr during the first dawn — what the Arabs of the desert call *al-kādhib* — before the second Fajr has set in, or not? And is it permissible to pray the two rakʿahs of the Sunnah of Fajr? Answer: The shafāʿah prayer and Witr are performed unless the true dawn has already set in, which would preclude them.

Whoever prays with the imam at Fajr and bows before the imam, then raises his head and bows again with the imam in bowing — his prayer is valid, for his prayer is like that of one who prays alone. If the imam prays alone, he repeats the prayer. Whoever prays Witr prayer and forgets


<!-- page 198 -->
Al-Qunoot offered the two prostrations of forgetfulness after the salutation. Question: If a person had prayed the prayer of intercession and then entered the mosque and prayed the greeting of the mosque during the time of intercession, must he repeat the intercession or not? Answer: He must repeat it, because he does not have the right to pray after it until he has prayed Fajr. When dawn comes he prays the greeting, then he prays Fajr. It is stated in the landmarks of the religion that Witr prayer is obligatory and it is not permitted to abandon it, even though it is a Sunnah, and it is among the appendages of the obligatory prayers. It is stated in the book of purity that one should recite in the first rak'ah the opening of the book and a surah — "Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of the daybreak" — and in the second the opening of the book and a surah — "Say: I seek refuge in the Lord of mankind" — then testify and give salutation. These two rak'ahs are part of Witr. One sits in them as mentioned, spreads the palms upon the thighs with the fingertips resting on the knees, and reads. When the reading is finished, one bows and places the palms of the hands on the knees. Likewise when standing; and when raising the head from bowing, one places the right leg behind, lifts the feet, prostrates, then sits in testimony as one sits in prayer. Our master Muhammad ibn Fahd — may God honor him — in his honorable message, transmitted from him out of love for the path of guidance for those who seek it, question twenty-two: I also asked about a person who prays the prayer of intercession and Witr, then sits for as long as he is able, then prays Fajr as an obligatory prayer, and it later becomes clear to him that his dawn had come before it — what is his condition regarding his prayer? In this case his prayer of intercession and his Witr stand confirmed, and he is required to repeat

the answer is also upon you.

194

<!-- page 199 -->
He should repeat it as a Sunnah and make it obligatory — except that if dawn is near him, the Sunnah prayer is also confirmed for him, because it is mentioned in the books of the saints of God, peace be upon them, that it is permissible to pray it before Fajr on account of the noble meaning that permits this. The Commander of the Believers, 'Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, stated in his four hundred teachings: "Place your trust in God when you bow down at Fajr after you have finished it, for in it desires are granted." Our master Sharaf al-Din Ja'far ibn Sayyidina Sulayman — may God bless them in the chambers of Paradise — stated in his book entitled "Elegant Psychological Gardens," which contains letters of evidence drawn from the answers of our master Dawud ibn Ajab to his son, the religious Sulayman ibn Hasan, in what was chosen from his sayings: As for what you asked regarding one who must make up Witr — is it permissible for him to pray voluntarily or not, and upon whom has the Sunnah been made obligatory to make up? Answer: It is permissible to do so, and the first priority is to offer the Witr as a make-up for what was missed.

**Remembrance of Sunnah and Nafl Prayers**

It is stated in the first mentioned book of questions and answers — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book "Pillars of Islam" — Question: What do you say regarding the opening prayer, the prayer of praise, and the prayer of Tahajjud? Has anything been transmitted specifying what should be recited in them

<!-- page 200 -->
as fixed recitations, and what surahs should be read in them? Answer: Nothing fixed has been transmitted for them; they are like Sunnah and nafl prayers in which one reads whatever one wishes. Question: What do you say regarding Witr prayer and Eid prayer — is it permissible for one who has not memorized the specified recitations to recite something else from the Quran? Answer: If he has not memorized those, he should recite as much as he is able and should not omit the prayer. Question: What do you say regarding the supplication of Tahajjud — is it permissible for one who has not memorized it to intend to perform it voluntarily? Answer: He intends to pray Tahajjud and prays with the supplication if he has memorized it, or he prays as much as he is able and intends Tahajjud, which is twelve rak'ahs, and it has two times: he may pray it between Maghrib and 'Isha'. Question: What do you say regarding Tarawih prayer — if the believers pray with the imam for taqiyyah purposes with the intention of voluntary prayer, and the imam recites the Quran from beginning to end, which is not the practice in the obligatory prayers, nor in Witr, nor in the Sunnah of Fajr, nor in the greetings? Answer: There is nothing wrong with taqiyyah, but they do not complete it. Question: It is mentioned in the book of evidence and statement that the worshipper prays ten rak'ahs for Dhuhr, eight for 'Asr, five for Maghrib, and ten for 'Isha'. Answer: The prevailing view is based on the pillars, which is the gathering of rulings; even if a person omits something from the Sunnah for taqiyyah, he makes it up. Question: It says in the prayer of praise that when one rises from the second prostration to perform ten glorifications of "My Master" — how does one perform them: sitting upon the feet or standing? Answer: If one wishes to pray

<!-- page 201 -->
the prayer of praise, one sits in the position of alertness after raising the head from the second prostration and glorifies ten times, then rises for the second rak'ah. After the second rak'ah one sits in the position of alertness after the second prostration and glorifies ten times, then sits in testimony and gives salutation. One follows the same procedure in the last two rak'ahs. However, the proper practice for one performing the prayer of praise is to sit upright after raising the head from the second prostration in each rak'ah, glorify ten times, then rise for the second — or testify after the glorification following the second prostration while remaining seated in the glorification posture.

The question concerns what is mentioned in the prayer of al-Kifayah: that in the first rak'ah one should recite the opening of the book, al-Ikhlas, al-Kafirun, and the verse of the Throne once each; in the second, twice each; in the third, three times each; and in the fourth, four times each. Our master — does one repeat al-Fatihah in every rak'ah or once only in each rak'ah, and what is preferred? Answer: One recites al-Fatihah once in each rak'ah, and God knows best. It is stated in the summary of effects: "Whoever has not memorized the Quran, let him recite what is available to him." God Almighty said: "So recite what is available of the Quran." Whoever wishes may pass the whole night with one surah, repeating it, or with part of it, or with two or more surahs. It is stated in the interpretation of the Pillars regarding the prayer of the Messenger of God, may God's peace and blessings be upon him: "I left him while he was directing his face toward Medina, and it was the day of his prostrating with his head," and the kiss was behind him. It is stated in the brief speech in the jurisprudence — whose questions are from

<!-- page 202 -->
Sheikh Hasan ibn al-Mawla Adam al-Hindi and whose responses are from the masters of Yemen, may God sanctify the souls of them all — that whoever prays between the obligation of the latter prayer and its negation, whatever he wishes of nafl prayer, does not intend it as a make-up, for it is called the night prayer. Our master and Mawlana Ja'far ibn Sayyidina Sulayman — the highest of their saints in Paradise — wrote in a letter of his to the servant who is the author of this book, his slave from the island of Yemen to the island of Hind, concerning the matter of Umm Dawud prayer: the information is for all the limited and the limitless. If asked about it so that matters become clear, the healer answered him regarding the means and paths he mentioned in connection with the prayer of Umm Dawud, the opening supplication, and that Qasim ibn Feer — the caller of the paralyzed band al-Akmahiyyah al-Dawudiyyah — commanded his companions to pray it, to fast, and to supplicate in the month of Rabi' al-Awwal of the year 1042 AH, when the fifteenth of it fell on a Friday while they were in prison, and likewise in the month of Ramadan of that year. You asked about that, its permissibility, and its practice. I answered the one who asked you about this that what Mawlana al-Sadiq — peace be upon him — wrote concerning the prayer of Umm Dawud is mentioned only for the month of Rajab, and he commanded that one purify oneself for it. When I asked him about it — whether it may be called upon with supplication outside the month of Rajab — he said: "On the day of 'Arafah, and if it falls on a Friday, his companion will not be emptied until he has been forgiven." This is the case here, since there is no apparent symbol that indicates otherwise, and there is no stipulation in that or in the command of Umm Dawud other than seeking forgiveness for the month of

<!-- page 203 -->
Rajab — for the aforementioned opening supplication is sufficient and persuasive. And in the text, after that, the day of 'Arafah that is not a Friday — if it were a Friday it would be as he mentioned — that too is sufficient and persuasive, meaning it applies only to that day or the mentioned day. Accordingly, one should not offer it except in accordance with what is stipulated; if they have it, let them follow it; otherwise let them do a little, for as the Prophet, peace and blessings of God be upon him, said: a little done consistently in accordance with the Sunnah is better than a great deal of innovation. I mentioned that on the day of 'Arafah one may call upon that supplication or pray within it, since the origin of this is one of the conditions of the aforementioned supplication, for he said: "So where are you in relation to the opening supplication?" And when I asked him about it, he introduced it before that prayer, then before the aforementioned supplication — if you pray within it, then her prayer rests upon that; and if you pray within it, you must pray for it on account of His bounty and generosity. God knows best and is wiser regarding His guardians. We seek to know, and God is the guide to what is right and the guide to the best of means. It is stated in the questions addressed to some of the Sheikhs of India — may God be pleased with them — and the answers given by some of our loyalists, the preachers of Yemen, may God sanctify them — Question: Do the believers not fast the Sunnah fasts as they pray the Sunnah prayers? Answer: It is permissible for them to break the Sunnah fast without making it up, but a Sunnah prayer that is missed must be made up, as the Imams — peace be upon them — and their people did. They have their reward from God if they fast. Our master and Mawlana, the gift of God,

<!-- page 204 -->
ibn Sayyidina Ibrahim, in answering questions that were presented to him by the most humble of his servants, Salih ibn Ahmad, who wrote to him from the island of Yemen to the island of Hind in the year 1135 AH: You asked about what is mentioned in the books — that the prophets, such as Jesus and others, and the Imams, such as Mawlana 'Ali, Mawlana Zayn al-'Abidin, and others among the Imams, may God's prayers be upon them all — that at the time of a great calamity they would call for water, perform ablution, and pray two rak'ahs, and in some cases four rak'ahs, for the relief of distress. It is not clear at what time they prayed: did they pray at any and all times of hardship, calamity, and disaster, or is it permissible at certain times according to Sunnah and nafl prayer? May the master of the land kindly answer this. Answer: With the help of God Most High, from Him and to Him in His land, may God's peace be upon him. The testimony of Mawlay Badr al-Din — the inspiration — makes clear that the prayers of the prophets and the prayers of the Imams, may God's prayers and peace be upon them all, extending to those who follow them — their prayers in matters of great events, calamities, and disasters that befall them are distinguished, praise be to God, and from their gratitude for what He has imparted to them of the secret of things and their knowledge that these things only occur to the extent that is possible. You mentioned that their prayers, may God's prayers be upon them, did not specify at what times they would pray; rather, they mentioned it in a general statement. Yes, it is permissible to pray Sunnah and nafl prayers at all times when such prayers are permitted, and the prohibition applies only to the

<!-- page 205 -->
known prohibited times, which are not hidden to the sound mind. The picture is illuminated for their slaves and Shi'a: if they wish to pray that mentioned prayer and thereby repel all calamities and satisfy all evils and defects, let them be guided by their guidance, follow their Sunnah, obey them, be supported by their intercession, be sustained by their secret and their blessings. He is my guardian in that, and He is capable of it; His secret is with Him.

**Remembrance of the Prostration of the Quran**

It is stated in the first mentioned book of questions and answers — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book "Pillars of Islam" — Question: What do you say about one who recites a supplication in which there is a verse of prostration — is he required to prostrate or not? Answer: If it is a firm recitation, he must prostrate. Question: What do you say about a teacher of boys who is teaching them the Quran and passes by the places of prostration while instructing them — must he prostrate every time he and those who hear him pass by one, or not? Answer: I prefer that he prostrate, and I also prefer that he leave it. Others say: what a man says in the prostration of the Quran is what is said during prostration. It is stated in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf: if the reciter recites a prostration from among the prayers, he prostrates in his position; and if he recites the prostration a second time, neither of them is required to prostrate again as long as he remains in his position. As for the remaining prostrations, whoever wishes to prostrate may do so, and whoever does not wish to prostrate need not.


<!-- page 206 -->
Prostration is praiseworthy, and the better course is to prostrate. Mawlānā, the Commander of the Faithful, ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred points of conduct: "Prolong the prostration, for nothing is harder on Satan than to see the son of Adam prostrating — Satan was commanded to prostrate and refused, while the son of Adam was commanded to prostrate and obeyed, and was saved."

**The Book of Funerals: Visiting the Sick, Clinics, and the Dying**

In the book *Tafsīr al-Arkān* it is stated that the dying state is the presence of death and the imminence of transition from this world to the hereafter. In the book of questions and answers compiled by the late Sheikh Yūsuf ibn Ismāʿīl al-Samātī al-Hindī, with responses from our master Idrīs ibn Ḥasan, may Allah be pleased with him, the following question is posed: What is your ruling on people who move from place to place out of fear of epidemic and plague, thinking they can escape it — is this permissible or not? Answer: They may go wherever they wish, for the matter is in the hands of Allah Almighty. Our master and Mawlānā Jaʿfar ibn Sayyidinā Sulaymān — may Allah sanctify him and grant us his intercession and forgiveness — addressed this in his noble epistle, which he titled *The Sharpest Sword in Nullifying Innovations*. He wrote it to advise his people and warn those who were heedless among the people of the island of Yemen, after he had settled and lived there. Chapter: The following has also been narrated.

<!-- page 207 -->
Let none of you be remiss in visiting the sick, given the reward that has been established for it in the book *Arkān al-Islām*, where it is recorded — may Allah sanctify his soul — a narration from our master the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, that he heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, say: "No Muslim slave visits the sick without seventy thousand angels praying over him from the hour he sets out — if it is daytime, until the sun sets; if it is at night, until dawn." He also narrated — may Allah sanctify his soul — and announced his command regarding the Commander of the Faithful, peace be upon him, that he said: "The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, forbade eating the food of the sick man in his home, for Allah will thereby nullify his reward." In another narration: "Allah will nullify his reward and even set it against him." The reason is that eating and drinking in the sick man's home harms him and his family, given the difficulty of his situation. He may be weak and become distressed on account of it, as may his family. Whoever is knowledgeable of this must make it known to those who are not, for not everyone is a scholar — yet among the scholars is the one who warns about it. The one who has been warned is excused, and truth has come while falsehood has perished. Mawlānā the Commander of the Faithful ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, also said in his four hundred points of conduct: "Let none of you sleep on his back or face down. If you see someone sleeping face down, wake him. And a woman should not sleep lying on her back."

He said: "I ask Allah for well-being from the severity of calamity, for the severity of calamity is a trial for one's religion."

<!-- page 208 -->
**Mention of the Command to Remember Death and Related Matters**

In the aforementioned book of questions and answers, arranged according to the order of the chapters of *Arkān al-Islām*, the following is recorded. Question: What do you say about people's lifespans — are they fixed with Allah Almighty, neither increasing nor decreasing? And if so, what is the meaning of the statements found in books that lifespans increase and decrease? Answer: There are deferred lifespans that neither increase nor decrease. But the meaning of increase and decrease is sound in the sense that what decreases from the past portion increases in what remains of the future. Question: What is your understanding of the words of the Prophet, may peace be upon him, regarding the wretched and dissolute person who is overtaken by the Hour while still alive and not yet dead — does this mean he is more severely punished than those who have already died, and that in the state of his sensory, animal life he has not yet made the transition to the spiritual life? Or is it something else? Answer: If the Hour comes upon him while he is still in the animal state, that is the case. It is also stated in the questions of Sheikh Ḥasan ibn Nūḥ to our master Ḥusayn ibn Idrīs, may Allah sanctify his soul, and in his answers to them — Issue Ten: regarding a woman who dies pregnant with a fully formed, living child in her womb — should the fetus be left inside her or removed? Answer: If a woman is available to extract it, it is obligatory to do so if it is possible, or if the fetus is six months or older and shows signs of life. The mother should not be buried until the child's death is confirmed. And Allah knows best. Mawlānā the Commander of the Faithful

<!-- page 209 -->
ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred points of conduct: "Hold fast to what Allah Almighty has commanded you. Let none of you be present at something he loves unless the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, would also attend it — otherwise it holds no good. Stay, and good news will come to you." He swore by Allah to make this clear: "Remember death often on the day you emerge from your graves and on the day of your resurrection before Allah — this will make your calamities easier to bear." He also said: "Fever is the herald of death. Allah imprisons in the earth whomever He wills among His servants, and it wipes away sins as thistles are removed from camel fur." He further said: "None of our Shia will leave behind something we have forbidden, and then die, without being struck by a calamity that purges his sins — whether in his wealth, his child, or even in his own person — so that he meets our love with none of his sins remaining upon him. He is pressed at the moment of death, and his sins are purged. The dead among our Shia is a martyr — one who believed in our cause, loved us, and allied with us."

**Mention of Condolences and Patience, What is Permitted by Way of Weeping, What is Forbidden by Way of Wailing, and Related Matters**

It is stated in the book *al-Mukhtaṣar al-Muṣannaf*: no hair is to be shaved over the deceased, nor is a nail to be cut for him. It is also stated in the aforementioned book of questions and answers, arranged according to the order of the chapters of *Arkān al-Islām*. Question: What do you say about the practice of some people who place certain weapons on the deceased when

<!-- page 210 -->
he is at home, while others consider it obligatory to sit in attendance — what is the ruling on this? Answer: Such a practice has no reliable basis. Our master and Mawlānā Jaʿfar ibn Sayyidinā Sulaymān — may Allah sanctify their souls in the chambers of the heavens — addressed this in his noble epistle titled *The Sharpest Sword in Nullifying Innovations*, in which he advised his people and warned those who were heedless and deeply negligent among the people of the island of Yemen, after he settled and lived there. Chapter: The following is also among what he made known to his community.

Among you there are those who have taken up the practice of wailing for the dead, with all the innovations that accompany it. Groups of women gather from their various places, mourning the dead through their nights and successive days, as though it were a scripture from your Lord, exalted be He — accompanied by singing and its consequences of amusement and play beyond the bounds of permissibility, even in the days of weddings. The distinction between what is forbidden and what is lawful has become so blurred that what is unlawful is not made permissible even if your adversaries from among the people of this noble school of thought have far more right to such restraint than you. So do not exhaust yourselves in ways that only stand against you before Allah, exalted be He, and before His guardians, peace be upon them. May Allah bless you in your time — this fleeting, perishing world — for you are the cream and the essence of all creation, so how is it that you are not deterred by your own failings, and do not guard yourselves against falling into ruin? May Allah protect you from this henceforth, and from any return to it, if you are believers.

Some of you have read in the book *Arkān al-Islām* the words of its author,

<!-- page 211 -->
may Allah sanctify his soul, in his narration — which he cited with intent and deliberation — from Jaʿfar ibn Muḥammad, may Allah's blessings be upon him, who instructed at the time of his death that no one should wail and no garment should be torn. No woman should tear her garment in grief — for every such tear is a crack in Hell that only grows wider. It is also narrated in it from the most exalted Allah — may He be glorified — who sanctified him in the gardens of paradise. And from ʿAlī, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he wrote to Rifāʿah ibn Shaddād, his judge over al-Ahwāz: "Forbid wailing over the dead in whatever land you govern." His saying also carries clear meaning. It is narrated from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, that he said: "Two voices are cursed and hateful to Allah: a voice at the time of calamity — meaning wailing — and a voice at the time of blessing — meaning singing." Whoever wails over the dead thereby inflicts on the deceased the causes of the torment by which his soul is afflicted, as is established from the Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family.

This is also what is mentioned in the Holy Book referred to, in the account of ʿAbdullāh ibn — who was ill and it was hoped he would recover — when he was found in severe pain, unconscious and unresponsive, while the women around him were wailing. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, called to him three times and received no answer, so he prayed for him as he prayed to his Lord Almighty, then turned away from him. ʿAbdullāh then felt a lightness within himself and regained consciousness. He told the Prophet, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, what he had experienced, and the Prophet told him of it. He stood up and

<!-- page 212 -->
said to him: "O ʿAbdullāh, what you saw is what happened — you witnessed something wondrous." ʿAbdullāh said: "O Messenger of Allah, I saw an angel of the kings holding an iron club ignited with fire. Every time a woman wailed — 'O my grief, O my courage!' — he would raise it toward me. The Prophet said, 'You were brought to it.' And I would say, 'No — but Allah restrained her after her outburst.' Then when she screamed, 'O my consolation, O my strength!' he raised it again. He said to me, 'Is she not your pride?' I said, 'No, but Allah,' and He restrained her after her outburst." The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, then believed ʿAbdullāh, and said: "What is the matter with your deceased, that they afflict one another? I greet you with something beyond this — yet something far less than this is sufficient for your lifetime. Perhaps the world has been explained to you and its rites and landmarks made clear, so that you follow a course toward your end in a way that is not within your control and not encompassing you." What is concealed from someone by his guardian — through dwelling in his home over his family — is a breach of the covenant of Allah that he has imitated, reverting from his knowledge to his ignorance. The aforementioned Holy Book has clarified what is permissible and what is not, in the noble sayings it contains, in which it says — for those who follow it there is attainment and success. And ʿAlī, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: "The Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, wept when some of his children died. He was told: 'O Messenger of Allah, you wept, yet you forbade us from weeping?' He said: 'I did not forbid you from weeping — I forbade you from wailing and lamenting. But this is tenderness and mercy that Allah, Mighty and Exalted, places in the hearts of whomever He wills among His creation. And Allah has mercy on whomever He wills — He has mercy on the most merciful of His servants.'" He, peace be upon him, also said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, spoke regarding weeping at calamity and said: "The soul is grieved and the eye is tearful,

<!-- page 213 -->
and the parting is near. Say only what pleases Allah, and do not say what constitutes abandonment — for that will be your deed, hidden from you." The loud voice and the cursed voice at the time of blessing — which is singing — have also been explained in the aforementioned Holy Book: what is permitted at banquets and weddings, to distinguish between what is forbidden and what is lawful in terms of what is permissible and what is not. This is the position of the author, on which his pen has dried — may Allah sanctify his soul. He states, narrating from the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him: "He heard music being played and asked, 'What is this?' They said, 'O Messenger of Allah, so-and-so has gotten married.' He said, 'He has completed his religion. This marriage is not like secret liaisons — it is not a secret union until smoke is seen or the sound of a tambourine is heard.' He said: 'The distinction between marriage and illicit union is the beating of the tambourine.'" This is what the author prefers for those who believe in it, whether in one matter or another. Whoever goes beyond it to other things — he counts all such matters against them. In this is a reminder for whoever has a heart, or lends an ear while being present of mind. Whoever does good, it is for himself; and whoever offends, it is against himself — and your Lord is not unjust to His servants. Mawlānā the Commander of the Faithful ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, also said: "Command your family to say good things over your dead." Fāṭimah bint Muḥammad, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, was prevented by her father, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him, from wailing. He summoned the daughters of Hāshim and said: "More supplication and less lamenting." He also said: "Whoever strikes his thigh at the time of calamity has forfeited his reward."


<!-- page 214 -->
On the washing of the dead and related matters

The following is drawn from the first question-and-answer book mentioned earlier, which is arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*. Question: What is said about a man who washes the dead — should he perform ablution three times with three washings, or not? Answer: Yes, he should perform ablution three times with three washings, and at the final washing he should divide them, as stated in the Book of Reports. Additionally: if a person is injured and has not been moved from his place, yet regains consciousness for an hour, two hours, or a day, and then dies, he must still be washed, because he was not removed from his place. Question: Concerning a deceased person who dies while traveling and there is no water available, or a man who dies among women with no woman present, or a woman who dies with neither a husband nor another woman to wash her — should such deceased persons be left without washing, and be given dry ablution with dust instead, or not? Answer: It is mentioned that if a person in a state of major ritual impurity (*janabah*) and a deceased person are in the same situation and there is only enough water for one of them, the living person washes and the deceased is handled accordingly. Additionally: if a man dies and the washer who washes him thereby causes himself harm, he should perform dry ablution (*tayammum*) and then wash the deceased — and this is permissible if no one else is available. If the living person is in greater need of washing than the deceased, it is permissible. It is stated in the summary of the classification that the first among those present with the deceased has the right to wash him — even if that person is himself in a state of major ritual impurity. If the deceased is found without any trace of washing, mummification, or shroud, it is stated briefly

<!-- page 215 -->
that in the cases of drowning, burning, and dismemberment — if a part is found from a person known to have perished — it is treated accordingly. Whatever falls away from the deceased is washed and prepared along with the rest, even if it is a wound or a part of one: each wound is washed in its place, the part is shrouded, and it is buried. It is also stated in the summary of the classification that one does not shave the hair of the deceased or cut the nails.

Our master and Mawlana Ja'far ibn Sayyidina Sulayman — may God sanctify them in the chambers of the heavens — answered questions posed by the author of this book. He replied from the island of Yemen to the island of Hind regarding what was asked. Among the questions: What do you say about a woman who dies and is washed using the *zar* method — where the tip of a cloth is passed behind her over the pudendum and between her thighs until the other end emerges behind her, forming a bundle — a practice used by unbelievers and certain Muslims alike, since the Dawoodi community uses it and regards those who do not as in error? Should this method be used when washing her, or not? And likewise, should it be used in her shroud? For the Dawoodi community insists she should be treated this way in her shroud. They also use the *sarah* when washing the deceased, covering her with it — so is it permissible to do only that, or may she be washed without a cover while a man is present? Answer: Nothing is mentioned in the Book of Pillars except that a cloth is placed over the deceased when washed,

<!-- page 216 -->
covering from the navel to the knees, with the water passing beneath, and the washer wrapping his hand in a cloth and reaching beneath the covering to wash the pudendum and whatever other private parts are not beneath the prohibition. Working according to what I have described is therefore contrary to the method you mentioned. Regarding the shroud, it is mentioned in the book *The Pillars of Islam* on the authority of Ja'far ibn Muhammad, peace be upon him, that the shroud consists of three garments: a shirt that is neither buttoned nor closed, a wrap, and a covering cloth — so take note of that.

As for the pleasant practice in washing the deceased — particularly with regard to sanctity — the most important thing is to do it only in a garment. And regarding what was asked: What do you say about the meaning of the words of our master Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad — may God sanctify him and grant us his intercession and blessing — in his book on purity, at the end of the section on washing the dead, where he mentions the woman being *'ajar*? What does *'ajar* mean? The answer is that the well-known *'ajrah* in Arab usage refers to a gathered shirt, where the portion from the navel downward is collected and secured, with the clothing and its fullness tucked into the *zar* fastening. This is the distinction between men and women, just as the face-veil (*niqab*) marks the distinction between them — so take note of that.

Mawlana the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, said in his *Four Hundred Counsels*: if one touches the body of the deceased after it has cooled, ghusl is required. He also said: whoever washes a deceased person and is then clothed, he must wash after the shrouding, and if he touches the deceased afterward, he must perform ghusl. It is stated in the explanation of what was said in the question-and-answer book mentioned earlier, beginning with the following. Question: What is meant by the words of the Commander of the Faithful,

<!-- page 217 -->
peace be upon him — "whoever touches the body of the deceased after it has cooled must wash himself" — what does "after it has cooled" mean? Answer: It means after the deceased has been washed. Whoever washes the deceased should do so according to the Sunnah, because the body of the deceased has become impure — and so if a living person comes into contact with it, he becomes impure through its moisture, and this is the meaning of the obligation of ghusl being established by contact.

It is stated in a book of questions from some of the scholars of India, with answers from some of our preachers in the Yemeni peninsula — may God sanctify them and grant us their intercession and blessing. Among the questions asked: a woman who gives birth to two conjoined children — if one of them dies, what is to be done? God Almighty creates what He wills. If their adhesion can be separated without harm or fear for either of them, then it should be separated. If separation carries a high degree of risk, and if their life and death are one — that is, they cannot survive independently — then God knows best and does what He wills. If one of them dies without the other, the dead one is separated from what connects it to the living one, insofar as the living one does not feel his parts being severed. If this is possible, the dead one is buried and the other left, even if pain is felt from the body of the other — since their life is one and their death is one. And God knows best.

On mummification and the shroud

The following is from the first question-and-answer book mentioned, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*. Question: What is said

<!-- page 218 -->
in the Book of Reports, that the first claim on the estate of the deceased is the shroud and its preparation, then the debt, then the bequest — if the debt exceeds what the deceased left behind, does the preparation of the shroud still take precedence, however great the cost? Answer: It is obligatory to take out the shroud and its preparation first, regardless of the amount. Additionally: the embalming preparation should be placed on the sites of prostration of the deceased — the forehead, the nose, the knees, between the big toe and the next, and on the navel, armpits, chest, mouth, eyes, ears, hands, palms, and knees. A small amount of oil and camphor mixed with a little water should be used. On the third night, water is poured on the grave. If the deceased is from among the people of rank, the Quran is recited over his grave for seven days; over the graves of others, for three days. This is what our followers did — may God extend their lives. It is narrated on the authority of Sayyiduna Hasan ibn Idris — may God grant him His grace — that he would recite the Quran for the people of rank seven times, and for others three times, whether in the mosque, at the grave, at home, or elsewhere.

It is stated in the summary of the effects that what is good to use in embalming does not include musk or saffron for men, but these may be used in the embalming of women. It is also stated in the book of purity that embalming camphor is placed on the forehead, nose, inside the hands, the knees, and the backs of the feet — these being the sites of prayer prostration. Cotton is placed on the face, under the armpits, between the knees, the legs, and the feet, when available. It is stated in the interpretation of the pillars that the practice regarding incensing the dead is that

<!-- page 219 -->
the deceased is not personally incensed, nor is his funeral procession accompanied by incense. Rather, his shroud is perfumed with incense, as is the place where he is washed and shrouded — nothing beyond that.

Mawlana the Commander of the Believers, Ali ibn Abi Talib, peace be upon him, said in his *Four Hundred Counsels*: "Do not fear your dead, and do not touch them with anything fragrant except camphor, for the dead is like one in a state of *ihram*." It is also stated in a book on legal issues that have all been addressed: another issue — what is said about a man who died while in a state of *ihram* for *hajj*, and who was not covered on his head or face and had no embalming applied to him — how is he washed and shrouded? Answer: In this case, since the man died as a pilgrim in *ihram*, his head and face are not covered, and nothing fragrant is applied to him, because that remains forbidden for the span of those days.

On the funeral procession

The following is from the first question-and-answer book mentioned, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*. Question: What is said in the Shari'ah regarding the command to walk behind the funeral procession — is it blameworthy to walk in front of it or not? Answer: It is mentioned in the Book of Reports that walking in front of a *nasib*'s funeral procession is disliked, because of the angels of wrath that accompany it. As for a believer's funeral, it does not matter where one walks, since the angels of mercy surround it; walking behind it is nonetheless better. It is also stated in the Reports that the call to prayer (*adhan*)

<!-- page 220 -->
at funerals is disliked, though notifying people of the death without making the call is permitted. It is stated in the interpretation of the pillars that it is obligatory for one not to remain seated when a funeral procession passes — except for one who intends to follow it — and not to sit until the deceased has been placed at the edge of the grave. From the summary of the monuments: when the procession reaches the graveyard, the bier is set down crosswise, and the deceased is laid on his back so that his right side faces the qibla and his left faces away, in the direction a worshipper would stand.

From the interpretation of the pillars: it is narrated that the Messenger of God, peace and blessings be upon him, saw women following a funeral procession. He stood and asked them whether they would pray over the deceased — they said no. He asked whether they would carry it — they said no. He asked whether they would find it with the one who would protect it — they said no. He said: "Then return, bearing a burden of sin and no reward."

It is stated in the summary of the effects that it is narrated from the Imams of the family of the Messenger of God, peace and blessings be upon him, that they ordered the deceased, after being shrouded, to be laid on the bier and carried on the shoulders of the men if they are present. If this is not possible, the body may be placed on an animal or whatever is available, with the shroud covered from above by a garment if one is found. A coffin is placed over the bier of the deceased — which serves as a canopy — covered with a garment, and the body is secured if there is fear it may fall from the bier.

On the funeral prayer

The following is from the first question-and-answer book mentioned, arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book *The Pillars of Islam*. Question: What is said about a man

<!-- page 221 -->
who dies in a place where there are no men present, only women — is it permissible for the women to pray the funeral prayer, or may men pray over him alone and then bury him? Answer: A man's prayer is more in accordance with the madhhab of the Ahl al-Bayt, may God bless them. Question: Regarding the funeral prayer for the deceased — is it permissible to pray over him after he has been carried from the place where he was prepared, or must one wait until he reaches the graveyard? Answer: He may be prayed over wherever one wishes — in the graveyard, in his home, or elsewhere. Whoever wishes may recite supplications between the takbeers of the funeral prayer, and whoever wishes may leave that to the imam. Funeral prayers are permissible even at times that are generally disliked for prayer, and also during an eclipse.

It is stated in the councils of the statement that the funeral prayer is obligatory for those Muslims who know about it, until at least one among them has performed it — at which point it becomes a voluntary act for the rest. It is stated in the Reports that narrators transmitted from the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, that it is recommended to pray the funeral prayer during the regular prayer times, unless one fears missing an obligatory prayer — in which case one begins with the obligatory prayer first. It is stated in the interpretation of the pillars that the same applies if one is not the imam of his time; and that whoever oversees or is delegated authority over the affairs of the Muslims has a greater right to lead the funeral prayer. If a group of those close to the deceased attend by order of the imam, or by the imam himself, this raises their standing. If only one of them attends, the guardian of the deceased — whether appointed by the imam or acting in one of the general affairs of the Muslims — has the greater right to lead the prayer. Whoever is established to lead people in prayer, if he attends the funeral, has the greater right to lead it.


<!-- page 222 -->
If none of the guardians attend, then they take precedence in praying over the deceased. It is stated in the summary of antiquities that prayer is also offered over the martyr, over those who committed major sins, and over those killed by hadd or retaliation.
