It is stated in a brief book that the reciter lowers his voice in supplication during the day and raises it if he prays over the deceased at night. Ali prayed over himself, and from him: his shroud knot is tied, then loosened, his right hand brought out from beneath his side, and his cheek pressed to the earth. If a woman dies and the child in her womb has reached six months, it should be delivered if possible. Our master and Mawlānā Ja'far ibn Sulaymān — may God sanctify them in the chambers of Paradise — addresses, in his answers to questions he was asked, the author of this book and his servant, responding from one island to another.

Hinda: Concerning what was asked — the question being: what do you say about attending the funeral prayer of a deceased person when the imam came forward to lead the prayer, and one is content to follow his recitation rather than recite independently — should the one following also recite if the prayer takes place during the day? The answer: In that case, it is stated in the book of Sharī'ah questions and answers that whoever wishes may recite the supplication between the takbīrs of the funeral prayer, and whoever wishes to leave it and be content with the imam's recitation may do so. Know this.

Dhikr al-Dafn wa'l-Qubūr

He said in the aforementioned book of questions and answers — which is arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book "The Pillars of Islam" — Question: What do you say concerning what is established in the Sharī'ah, namely that whatever falls from a person while he is still alive should be buried in the earth?

<!-- page 223 -->
Our master, should a person bury such a thing wherever he wishes, or only in the cemetery? And if he loses it and does not bury it, does he incur sin or not? Answer: He may bury it wherever he wishes, and there is nothing wrong with him if he loses it. Question: What do you say about a man whose dead were buried in his land by others without his permission, and when he learned of this he wished to have them removed — is that permissible? Answer: It is not permissible for him to remove those buried in his land without his consent, for he has nothing but the value of the grave plots, and it is not permissible to exhume them. Question: What do you say about what is established in the Sharī'ah concerning the command to bury the dead in the places where they die and the prohibition on transporting them — our master, if the place of burial is far away, how does this work? Answer: The deceased is buried in the place where he dies, in the graveyard of the people of that locality, and is not taken to another country. If he dies in the wilderness, there is nothing wrong with carrying him to the nearest burial place. Question: What do you say about a woman who dies and there are no men who are her close relatives in the city who are lawfully permitted to look upon her — who should lower her into the grave? Answer: If a woman dies and there is no male relative whose gaze is lawful upon her, then those closest to her among the people shall bury her; if that is not possible, the injustice of necessity applies. Question: What do you say concerning what appears in the books of the Calm Call — may God's peace be upon its owner — regarding placing milk on the deceased, or wood, or whatever they wished, and then placing dust over it? And what do you say, our master, about placing wages, stones, and other existing materials upon the grave? As for the people of the apparent path,

<!-- page 224 -->
they dislike the reward, so how may Allah redeem you? Answer: It is permissible to place the reward and other existing materials upon the grave — rely on this and pay no attention to anything else, except what is required for taqiyyah. Mawlānā Amīr al-Mu'minīn Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred manners: Attend to the graves that you need — their inhabitants visit them and seek provision there; they will be glad of your visit. A man should seek what he needs at the grave of his father and mother after he has prayed for them.

Book of Zakāt

Mentioning the intentions in giving zakāt and related matters

Our master Ja'far ibn Manṣūr al-Yaman — may God sanctify him and grant us his intercession — said in the book of sub-nursing, citing the saying of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family: "No charity is permissible for me, nor for my family." He said: As for one who is able to provide for those of my household, the call after this is what he must do for them outwardly, in that he has abundant wealth and pays from it what he will beyond instinct, from among obligatory rights and voluntary acts. He said: Whoever fulfills the khums is spared from paying the tithe, except for voluntary giving; but whoever pays the tithe alone is not spared from paying the fifth that he must fulfill, because the tithe does not encompass the khums as the khums encompasses the tithe. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "There is no prayer for one who does not pay zakāt."

<!-- page 225 -->
Our master Zayd ibn Muḥammad, may Allah be pleased with him, said in the hidden message: Allah Almighty has made zakāt obligatory on ten things — 1. gold, 2. silver, 3. camels, 4. cows, 5. sheep, 6. dates, 7. raisins, 8. wheat, 9. barley, 10. grains. Mawlānā Amīr al-Mu'minīn Alī ibn Abī Ṭālib, peace be upon him, said in his four hundred manners: Cure your people through charity, and fortify your wealth with zakāt. Our master and Mawlānā, the owner of the Mustanṣiriyya councils — may God's peace be upon its owner — said in the twelfth council: You have heard what was recited to you above in jurisprudence concerning guardianship, purity, and prayer — what constitutes the sincerity of testimony, which is the foundation of religion and worship and the first cause of mercy and happiness. Now hear what we recite to you: the mention of zakāt, which is one of its rights as established above. Its sincerity lies in knowing its noble boundaries, fulfilling its rights, and performing the seven obligatory duties that are the pillars of Islam as the above system has shown. In zakāt likewise there are seven obligatory duties and twelve years, like the number of chapters of testimony and its letters. Zakāt must be brought to the Imam and taken from seven things: 1. gold, 2. silver, 3. camels, 4. cows, 5. sheep, 6. crops, 7. minerals. Disbursements fall under eight names divided into seven categories: 1. the poor and the needy — zakāt is given to them; 2. those who work in its collection —

<!-- page 226 -->
they are given the wages of collection; 3. those whose hearts are to be reconciled — they are paid from it for protection; 4. for the freeing of those in bondage; 5. those burdened with debts incurred in settling the affairs of the community; 6. in the way of Allah, meaning jihād, which is a collective obligation; 7. the wayfarer who is cut off and stranded.

As for its two sunnahs: 1. it should not be taken until after the completion of the year; 2. the niṣāb of gold is twenty dinars; 3. the niṣāb of silver is two hundred dirhams; 4. the niṣāb of camels is five head; 5. the niṣāb of cows is thirty head; 6. the niṣāb of sheep is forty head; 8. zakāt is taken from cattle of middling quality — neither the finest nor the lowest; 9. a joint must not be added to it, nor must the community be divided; 10. the stud animal must not be taken from them; 11. and charity is given by the owner of crops that have not reached the niṣāb — which is five awsāq, measured with the squeeze and the fist — at the time of his harvest, for Allah says: ﴿وَآتُوا حَقَّهُ يَوْمَ حَصَادِهِ﴾. There is no zakāt on houses, jewelry, and whatever is for use; and each of these words carries many meanings and effects of wisdom.

Mentioning the hardening and the making of zakāt

He said in the aforementioned book of questions and answers — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book "The Pillars of Islam" — Question: What do you say concerning what is established in the Sharī'ah, namely that it is forbidden for a person to conceal his zakāt from his imam? Our master,

<!-- page 227 -->
what is meant — is it that he withholds zakāt or something else, and how does he conceal zakāt from his imam if he is supposed to hand it to him openly? Answer: This hadith refers to one who pays some of the zakāt while withholding the rest — he has abandoned an obligatory duty. Question: What do you say about what is mentioned in the book of Pillars, that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "We are the people of the House — no charity is permissible for us" — our master, does this refer specifically to the Imams, or to all his descendants through Fāṭima, peace be upon her? Answer: What is meant by this is the Imams, peace be upon them. Question: What is stated in the book Pillars of Islam, that charity does not resolve the five for one of the men who bore the beauty — our master, what is the meaning of "the sling" here? Answer: It is either a punishment from an unjust authority or blood money for a competent woman. Question: From the book of Pillars, concerning the covenant written by Amīr al-Mu'minīn for Ḥanaf ibn Sālim — who was one of the people of obedience to us, between the island and Kufa and the land of Damascus — he claimed that his charity was being directed to the agents of Damascus while he was in our possession; it is forbidden for our horses and men to intercept it. Is it permissible for him to do this, given that if the truth is as he claims, he has no right to go down to our country and give the zakāt of his wealth to our enemy? What is the ruling on this, and is it obligatory for the Sultan to forbid the land of India with his forces and men? Does he owe zakāt or not? Answer: The covenant written by the Commander of the Believers, peace be upon him, for al-Muhanaf ibn Sulaym — and his saying that unless his horses and men are not in his possession he should not be asked —

<!-- page 228 -->
means that zakāt is not required from those who are not Shī'ites who have been seized by the enemy. The successive transmissions from the Ahl al-Bayt, peace be upon them, mentioned in the book of Pillars and elsewhere state that the one who handed over zakāt to one whom Allah did not command him to hand it to — it is permissible for him to reclaim it by any means until he hands it over to the guardian or to whoever receives it by his command. He said in the Mustanṣiriyya councils: likewise, the one who performs the outward prayer while holding inward conviction, yet does not spend of what Allah has provided for him as he has been obligated — giving zakāt on his wealth, one-fifth of his gains — has bought himself, yet failed to pay off the debt he owed. He is one of those whom Allah described in the verse: ﴿وَمِمَّا رَزَقْنَاهُمْ يُنفِقُونَ﴾. And the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him and his family, said: "There is no prayer for one who does not pay zakāt," and he said: "The one who withholds zakāt is in the Fire."

Mentioning Zakāt on Gold, Silver, and Jewels

He said in the aforementioned book of questions and answers — arranged according to the order of the chapters of the book Pillars of Islam — Question: What do you say about a man who died and appointed two guardians, and one of them, or another of his close ones, feared that the other would neglect what was due from the deceased's estate in the way of khums, zakāt, Ḥajj, and other obligations — so he paid it secretly without informing the other, out of fear? Is there anything wrong with that? And what should be done if the deceased left nothing, yet the state of kinship was such, and it was known that he had been withholding zakāt, had not paid one-fifth of all his wealth, and had not performed Ḥajj? Answer: If the guardian discharged what he did

<!-- page 229 -->
on behalf of the deceased, there is nothing wrong with it, because zakāt is a debt that must be settled first, then Ḥajj — and if the saints are not settled, then nothing else clears his obligation, whether secretly or publicly. Question: What do you say about a man who does not pay zakāt on his wealth or deliver it to the one who is commanded to receive it — is it permissible to eat food in his house? Answer: The answer to that question is that there is nothing wrong with it. Question: What do you say about a man who possessed wealth and did not pay the fifth, and then died — should everything left, including the house, servants, and common goods, be set aside? Answer: Yes, the khums must be taken out of his entire estate. Question: What do you say about a man who had reached the niṣāb but did not pay zakāt, and then owned nothing at death except the goods and property he left behind — and it was observed that the estate had reached the niṣāb — should zakāt be paid on it or not? The same question applies to Ḥajj and any other obligations on his wealth. Answer: If his remaining estate reaches the niṣāb of what was subject to sale, trade goods, and commerce, it is obligatory to pay zakāt on it, and the Ḥajj obligation is to be discharged after zakāt. Question: What do you say about a man who died, and when his entire estate was examined it was found to be sufficient for Ḥajj and other obligations such as expiation, al-Shurwa, and khums, yet the deceased had little during his lifetime beyond the house and furnishings — should Ḥajj and all other obligations be discharged from it, with the heirs receiving what remains? Answer: If he leaves wealth, the Ḥajj obligation takes precedence over inheritance, because it is a debt, and debt comes before inheritance; and the other obligatory duties are likewise obligatory if something remains in the estate, even if nothing is left for the heirs. Question:


<!-- page 230 -->
The collector of the obligatory levy takes ten dirhams from polytheists for every two dirhams, and takes five dirhams from Muslims for every dirham received and forgives the remainder. The owner of the fardah for some Muslims is one or two dirhams. May the owner of the cloth that the owner of the fardah did not forgive — to one who has forgiven him — write it in his name on the condition that what remains is divided equally between them: is it permissible to take that or not? Answer: What the partners have agreed upon and consented to is permissible. Question: A man died and left two hundred dinars, or more or less, and he was obliged to pay zakat, Hajj, khums, al-Sharwa, and expiation — with the capital in hand, which of these claims takes precedence? Answer: He begins with zakat, because that is a debt owed from his wealth; then Hajj, then khums, then expiation, then al-Sharwa. Question: A man died and left money and an estate, and he made no bequest — does khums come out of the capital, along with the purchase obligation and expiation? Answer: Khums is obligatory and must be taken from the capital. If the heirs are pious, it is their duty to discharge it on behalf of the deceased. If they refrain, there is no compulsion in religion. On another matter: whoever pays zakat two years in advance, the second year's obligation is waived. Question: Regarding a man who has two hundred mithqals and pays zakat at the beginning of the hawl, then leaves one hundred at home — in which he neither sells, buys, nor trades — and the remaining portion is invested: when the second hawl arrives, must he pay zakat on two hundred or on one hundred? And also, if he buys a commodity on the condition

<!-- page 231 -->
that it is to be held and he does not sell it and its price falls, must he pay zakat on it or not? Answer: Zakat remains obligatory on what is liable as long as it remains in the owner's possession — whether he trades with it or holds it — until what he owns falls below the nisab. Question: It is stated in al-Masa'il al-Sharwa' that the nisab is one hundred and nineteen dirhams, and that for those of modest means it is one dinar from our master, while for those of fewer means there is nothing — what is the weight of a dinar and how much does a dirham weigh? Answer: Our master said: the threshold for the people of means in wealth on which zakat is due after the hawl is either one hundred and ninety-five dirhams of silver — each dirham being fourteen carats of silver and five carats — or twenty gold dinars. On this reckoning, the weight of one nisab of silver is eighteen ounces by Arab weights, ninety-five and a quarter, which is a quarter of a debt and a quarter. Look at how much this silver is worth in the common currency of the day: whoever owns this amount or more is counted among those of means. As for gold, each dinar is twenty-four carats, and on this reckoning the weight of twenty dinars is three ounces of gold by Arab weights — that is sixteen portions of a religious debt and a half. On gold, however, there is a difference of opinion, because the price of gold and silver varies at all times according to people's demand for one over the other — it may be that the price of

<!-- page 232 -->
twenty dinars is more than two hundred dirhams, or the reverse. When that is the case, the believer in matters of religion may be assessed against the price of the two currencies mentioned — gold or silver — whichever is the less costly. Question: What do you say about a man who has one-fifth of one hundred tanks, took out five of them in the first year, and was left with four hundred tanks? He traded with them and gained three hundred tanks in profit, and household expenses consumed two hundred tanks, leaving him one hundred tanks of the principal. Does khums come out of three hundred tanks or one hundred? Answer: If he has already paid khums on the capital, then khums is not due on the profit except on what remains in his hands. If he pays khums on everything, that is a virtue and not an obligation. This is the answer of our master Idris — may God sanctify his soul. He said in the summary of the classification: zakat is obligatory when wealth reaches the nisab on which it is due, is free of encumbrances, and a full hawl has passed over it. He also said: if a deposit is altered by the depositor, who introduces into it something that removes it from the ruling of a deposit or consumes it and makes it as if it were money of his own, then zakat is obligatory on him for all of that. If a hawl passes over the estate of a deceased person after his death and his heirs have not yet divided it, zakat is not due on the principal of the whole estate, but is obligatory on the principal of each heir's share: whoever's share reaches the nisab must pay zakat, and whoever's share does not reach the nisab — or who has no other wealth — zakat is not due from him on that share. If a man has wealth and a hawl passes over it while he is prevented from accessing it, and his wealth is more than what he owes, then if he sells it voluntarily he is obliged to pay zakat on the excess over his debt.

<!-- page 233 -->
For trade goods acquired for trade with other goods also bought for trade, the second item's hawl is counted from the time of his ownership of the first — whether he was fair in that or not, he pays zakat on it. He said: the same applies if he purchased trade goods with cash on which zakat was payable, according to what the cash had established; if the hawl arrives while the goods are in his hands, he pays zakat on his money. He also said, citing transmitted reports: it is narrated that whoever acquires wealth that is good for him and then finds it should pay zakat on it. He also said: an apostate receives zakat but does not owe zakat on what he held during his apostasy. He said further that there is no zakat on an orphan's wealth or on the wealth of someone who lacks legal capacity — meaning there is no zakat on what such a person owns and keeps for their own use; but what is held for trade purposes is subject to zakat. He said in the summary of the classification: from extracted metals, one-fifth is due, and the remainder belongs to the finder; one-fifth is due on ore; and dinars found along routes of Islam are governed by the ruling of found property. Some of the guardians of God — peace be upon them — said that a person of means is one who can afford one hundred and nineteen mithqals of gold, and whoever cannot afford that, then one hundred and nineteen dirhams of silver. He said in questions posed to our master Sulayman ibn Hasan in his early period, with answers from our master Dawud ibn Ajab — may God sanctify their souls — and it was asked: if a person has sown five ounces of his wealth, is he obliged to give one-fifth of it or not? Answer: Yes — may God bless you — one-fifth of the seeds is obligatory.

<!-- page 234 -->
The clay, as Allah Almighty said: ⟪ar:وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّمَا غَنِمْتُم مِّن شَيْءٍ فَأَنَّ لِلَّهِ خُمُسَهُ⟫ — "Know that whatever you take as booty, a fifth of it belongs to Allah." These seeds fall within the category of "thing," as our master Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad — may God sanctify his soul — mentioned and explained in the Book of Himma. He also said in the Book of Interpretation of Sharia: Imam al-Mu'izz li-Din Allah — peace be upon him — was asked about zakat. He said it consists of five dirhams, and the poor are of five types, each category receiving a dirham, for zakat is a consolation; all people and all creation are like one soul, and when one limb of the body suffers, all the other limbs attend to it in order to relieve its pain. If God afflicts any of the children of the human race, it is the duty of the rest of humanity to cure him, each according to what he is able. A man was mentioned, and it was said that there is nothing wrong with him. He — peace be upon him — said: if no defect had touched him, death would not have found him. Our master, the late preacher Ja'far ibn our master Mansur Yemen, said in the first book of Breastfeeding the Subconscious: the five prayers are known at five times, and zakat likewise has five forms. 1 — One-fifth of all wealth earned through trade, sales, and gains. 2 — Zakat on wealth after a hawl has passed over it — one-fifth of forty dinars — which is obligatory for the Hajj and the khums for the Imam. 3 — Zakat on crops, which is one-tenth. 4 — Zakat on livestock: from forty heads, one head. 5 — Zakat of the soul, which is what the believer performs for himself to expiate his sins, to free his neck, and from his nature — fasting after these four obligatory zakats. Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad said in

<!-- page 235 -->
the hidden message: from a holding of gold, twenty mithqals obliges half a mithqal. He then said: whoever owns two hundred dirhams owes five dirhams. The two hundred are two covenants. He said therein: there is no zakat on one who does not own twenty mithqals, nor on one who does not own two hundred dirhams. He said therein: whoever owns two hundred dirhams, it is forbidden for him to receive zakat or charity. He said therein: the owner of gold does not owe zakat on silver unless it is permissible to combine them.

Mentioning Zakat on Livestock

He said in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf: there is no zakat on livestock until they have grazed freely; if they graze in some of the hawl and are fed in other parts of it, the majority of rulings hold that whoever has five camels owes nothing on them in the first part of the hawl — one sheep is due — and nothing more in the second part, because in the second a sheep becomes due as our religion stipulates. The same applies to camels: if a camel owner owes a camel and he has a sheep taken from his own flock, which is acceptable as a sacrifice, that is also permissible. Buffaloes and cows in zakat are treated the same as sheep and goats. He said in the interpretation of these pillars, specifically that some charity is obligatory in zakat on livestock; if a given level yields nothing, then nothing is due on it. Ja'far ibn Mansur Yemen said in the Book of Sub-Suckling at the beginning: the zakat of livestock is from forty

<!-- page 236 -->
heads — one head from among them. He said: our master Zayd ibn Muhammad said in the hidden message: camels are slaughtered after slaughter. He said: there is no charity on one who does not have five camels. He said therein: once five camels are completed and owned, one sheep of the flock is obligatory on him for every five up to twenty, and four are paid. When he reaches twenty-five, he must give a daughter of labor. He said therein: the daughter of labor is the one whose mother bore a child in her womb. He said therein: above ten, when he reaches thirty-five, a daughter of Labun is due. He said therein: at forty-five, a right of the stallion is due. He said therein: then with every additional five above ten, and when one is added to sixty, a trunk is due in it. He said therein: above the trunk, something is taken in charity. He said therein: in sixty, one of the camels is a stallion that resembles a stallion and can bear what it can bear. He said therein: if the number reaches one hundred and twenty camels, two rights are due, because one hundred and twenty is two times sixty. He said therein: if the number of cows reaches thirty, they are sold. He said: if it reaches forty, then an old cow is due. He said therein: in sixty, three annals are due. He said: in one hundred and twenty, in every forty an old cow, and in every thirty a sold one. He said therein: when forty sheep are reached, one sheep is charity. He said therein: at one hundred and twenty, one sheep is due. He said therein: if it reaches two hundred sheep,

<!-- page 237 -->
then two sheep are due. He then said: for every hundred sheep, one sheep is due. He said: nothing is taken in charity that causes shame. He said: no male sheep is to be taken in charity unless the believer wishes it.

Mentioning Zakat on Grains, Fruits, and Plants

He said in the first mentioned book of questions and answers, which is arranged according to the order of the chapters of the Book of the Pillars of Islam. Question: The nisab of gold is twenty dinars and of silver is one hundred dirhams — how does this work for the nisab of grains? Answer: It is stated in the Pillars that zakat is paid from everything the earth produces, and that is the position of our senior loyalists, so it is one's duty to follow their example. They took one-tenth of what was little or much, and that is a virtue. It is stated in the Sharia that the nisab for grains is five awsaq and sixty sa's. He said in al-Akhbar: and a sow was not sold — meaning grain on which zakat is due; what is sold before it is contracted, zakat is not due on it. The same was narrated regarding fruits. They said the owner of the land does not deduct what he spent on it. In a book that is a summary of the work, it is obligatory on the young and the old, the rich and the poor, and those who have a debt. From the Book of Akhbar: they narrated that tithe land is all land of the Arabs on which Muslims did not settle through conquest or passage, and all land of the mountains whose people extracted and that was not overflowed by great rivers.


<!-- page 238 -->
This is the land subject to the tithe, and they narrated that the land subject to the kharaj is the land of Sudan and the mountain territories that the Muslims conquered by force of cavalry and infantry. The people at war surrendered their lands, making them subject to the tithe. They further narrated that if a Muslim purchases a piece of land from a dhimmi and takes possession of it, its legal ruling does not change by virtue of its transfer to the Muslim — the land remains subject to the kharaj as it was. Conversely, if a dhimmi purchases land from a Muslim and takes possession of it, the tithe becomes obligatory upon him when produce is extracted, because what is obligatory is determined by what the earth yields, not by who owns it. In the section on the landmarks of religion, it is stated that zakat is obligatory on all grains that resemble wheat and dates, and that the nisab is not reached by anything less than five awsaq. In the section on the chosen view, it is stated that the nisab of grain, as mentioned, is five awsaq, and that one wasq equals sixty sa's. In the section on al-Akhbar: if a field was irrigated partly by one water source and partly by another, the ruling follows the majority source. If it was moderate — equally irrigated by both — then three-quarters of a tenth is taken. And it is stated: no zakat applies to a combination of grains and fruits where neither component reaches half of the nisab, since neither type incurs zakat until it reaches half of what makes zakat obligatory. In the summary of effects, it is stated that the tithe becomes obligatory on grain when it forms kernels, and should be taken when the grain's soundness becomes apparent and beneficial to its owners. In the book on breastfeeding, it is stated: as for the tenth, it is an established ruling that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, implemented — it is collected from the faithful and from those whose wealth is visible. And it is stated: likewise, whoever pays a tenth of his apparent gain has truly discharged an apparent obligation.

<!-- page 239 -->
If he retains only nine-tenths, he has done what he was commanded, even if his authority did not see any request upon him. Whoever exploits his gain and retains more than nine-tenths has not rightly fulfilled the tithe he was commanded and ordered to pay, and has not chosen to obey his authority in accordance with the truth. Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad stated in the hidden message that these five categories are dates, raisins, wheat, barley, and grain — if they reach five awsaq, then half an awsaq of charity is due. He said: "The tenth is obligatory on that which the heavens water and the rivers water. As for what is irrigated by means of channels and buckets, half the tenth is due." And he stated in it: Allah has imposed obligations concerning the five shares of war spoils. Allah Almighty said: ⟪ar:وَاعْلَمُوا أَنَّمَا غَنِمْتُم مِّن شَيْءٍ فَأَنَّ لِلَّهِ خُمُسَهُ وَلِلرَّسُولِ وَلِذِي الْقُرْبَىٰ وَالْيَتَامَىٰ وَالْمَسَاكِينِ وَابْنِ السَّبِيلِ⟫ — meaning: one fifth goes to five categories to whom the khums is distributed. He further stated that the khums is also taken from minerals and treasures, and from war spoils. And from Ja'far ibn Muhammad al-Sadiq, may Allah's prayers be upon him, it is transmitted that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him and his family, when spoils came to him, would take his share and then divide the remainder among the relatives, orphans, the poor, and the wayfarer.

Mention of Zakat al-Fitr

He stated in the first book of questions and answers, which is arranged according to the order of the chapters of the Book of the Pillars of Islam: Question — the common practice among Indian scholars is that they combine four types of rice, corn, barley, and salt, and take their monetary value.

<!-- page 240 -->
In the Shari'ah, however, zakat al-fitr is a sa' of dates, raisins, barley, or wheat. Is it sufficient for one giving this to give a sa' of barley? Answer: The majority view, as mentioned in the Pillars, is that it is a sa' of barley, wheat, dates, or raisins — and the guiding principle is that it should be from the best food one eats and the best food one stores. As for combining different grains, whatever one ate during the past month of Ramadan is divided proportionally, and whoever ate nothing other than barley will have fulfilled it with barley. Question: What do you say about a man who wishes to give his zakat al-fitr before the beginning of Ramadan, fearing that something might prevent him from delivering it at the proper time — such as distance or travel? The noble Shari'ah holds that obligatory duties are performed only at their appointed time and are to be made up only after they are missed. Should he receive reward for this early payment or not? Answer: It is mentioned in the Book of Pillars that it is permissible to accept zakat from those obligated to pay it in advance, if there is a need for that — and such a person is blessed with the answer. As for one who does it out of ignorance, is it deemed permissible for him? Answer: If he submits his zakat al-fitr to one who is entitled to receive it, then it is permissible for him to do so, because the prohibition in this matter is that a person should not break his fast before submitting his zakat al-fitr — and there is no delay in that. The obligation is to deliver it. He stated in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf: if a dhimmi submits his payment before the dawn of the day of al-Fitr, he has discharged his zakat al-fitr; if he submits it after dawn, he has not.

<!-- page 241 -->
He stated in the transmitted reports: they narrated that zakat al-fitr is paid on behalf of servants. He stated in the landmarks of religion: if a newborn is born before the break of dawn on the day of al-Fitr, the obligation applies to him. He stated in the Book of Pillars that it was also said that it is to be paid on behalf of the foetus before he is born. He stated in the chosen view: one sa', or half a sa' of wheat. He stated in the landmarks of religion that the weight of a sa' is four and a half pounds according to the Makkan measure, and that one pound according to the Madinan measure equals five pounds less one-third, while its weight in Baghdadi measure is nine pounds and a fraction. Our master and Mawlana, the holder of the Mustansiriyya councils — may God's peace rest upon its author — stated in the thirteenth majlis: having mentioned zakat that is obligatory on the owners of wealth, we follow this with a mention of the zakat that is obligatory on every person individually, given as charity by those who have little, which is zakat al-fitr. It has seven obligatory elements and twelve recommended ones. The obligatory elements are: 1 — that a man pays it for himself; 2 — for his older children; 3 — for his younger children; 4 — for those among them who are free; 5 — for those who are enslaved; 6 — for the males among them; and 7 — for the females. The recommended practice is that it be given from the staple food one eats — being a sa' of barley, a sa' of wheat, a sa' of dates, or a sa' of raisins — and he is rewarded with half a sa' of wheat. It should not be given from anything other than these except in a sa'-for-sa' measure. Four provisions apply: it should be paid to the Imam, peace be upon him, or to one appointed by the Imam; it should be taken out before breaking the fast; it is to be spent from the moment it is taken out; and one should not

<!-- page 242 -->
give its equivalent in paper or gold. If a woman has no husband present, she pays it for herself and for those who depend on her. He stated in the book on supplementary nursing topics: as for the saying of the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, that natural disposition does not permit me or my family to receive it — our master Zayd ibn Muhammad stated in the hidden message, may God sanctify his soul: sadaqat al-fitr is obligatory upon the Messenger of Allah, peace be upon him and his family, and upon all others. One must discharge his zakat al-fitr on behalf of males and females, young and old, even a child born on that very day; and it should be discharged on behalf of the foetus in his mother's womb. He made it obligatory at the rate of one sa' per head of wheat, dates, raisins, barley, or other grains. He further stated: sadaqat al-fitr is called the charity of the head. And he stated: it is to be paid before the prayer — if it is given before the prayer, it is zakat al-fitr; if it is given after the prayer, it is merely voluntary charity. He stated: if a newborn is born after the Fitr prayer, zakat al-fitr is to be discharged for him before the next Fitr. He stated in the book of the Keys of Grace, attributed to our master Judge al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad, may God sanctify him: if the zakat al-fitr is paid before sunrise, it is zakat al-fitr; if it is paid after sunrise, it is charity, according to what has been transmitted. And the meaning of natural disposition here is to discharge one's duty to God — whether that consists of obedience or sacrifice.

He then mentioned various rulings on zakat. He stated in a book containing all the difficult legal questions: Another matter — What do you say

<!-- page 243 -->
about a man for whom zakat has been given? Answer: In this case, it is a man who possesses a thousand dinars, and he also owes a thousand dinars in debt — therefore he owes no zakat on it. He stated: there is another issue in it — What do you say about a man who is obligated to pay zakat on his wealth, but who is himself wealthy? Answer: In that case, it refers to a man living among polytheists who could not find a Muslim upon whom to spend his zakat and who is most deserving of it.

The Book of Lent

Mention of the Obligation of Fasting in the Month of Ramadan

He stated in the book Mukhtasar al-Musannaf: fasting is a shield from the Fire — a shield of the tongue, the ears, the eyes, and all other senses. It is not simply a matter of abstaining from food and drink; for every sense that is restrained, there is a reward, and whoever upholds that is truly fasting. Whoever abandons any of it diminishes the virtue of his fast proportionally to what he has left, and fasting the month of Ramadan is obligatory upon every adult and sound Muslim, male and female. He also stated: there is no fasting for one who is a hypocrite and disobeys. Clarification: what has been transmitted concerning fasting upon sighting the crescent moon, and what is mentioned in this chapter and in the two chapters before it, pertains to those who are distant from the Imam and do not know the determination of the crescents by our master and Mawlana. Ja'far ibn Sayyidina Sulayman stated — may God bless him — in his noble epistle, entitled the Sharpest Sword in Nullifying Innovations, that he urged

<!-- page 244 -->
those who were awake to wake others and warned those of the people of Yemen who had fallen into deep slumber — who had gone along with it and been complacent. Chapter: This too is something that most of you have heard and known: that the holy month of Ramadan is not honoured with the full honour that God Almighty and His Messenger, peace be upon him, have prescribed for it, because it is the month of the great and generous God. The proper reverence is to precede the word "month" as Allah Almighty said: "The month of Ramadan" — yet you address it by calling it "Ramadan" alone, which is not appropriate at all and does not lead to what is right. A person does not address one he holds dear except with a word of honour placed before his name, as is well known among you — who know his glory and how to draw near. As for what has been stated: "And to Allah all things return." Moreover, it has been narrated from our master al-Qadi al-Nu'man ibn Muhammad, may God sanctify him, in the book Mukhtasar al-Athar al-Sharifa, that our master the Imam al-Baqir Abu Ja'far Muhammad ibn Ali, peace be upon him, said: "Do not say 'Ramadan,' but say as Allah Almighty said: 'the month of Ramadan.'" Whoever has heard the word of Allah Almighty — and His permission is in that saying — how can it be permissible for him to disobey and turn away from it? As for the people of indifference and those who, after standing on this matter, belittle the command of Allah Almighty and His decree — and the said Imam al-Baqir, peace be upon him, has made this serious, as stated in this noble book, on the grounds that it falls under the censure contained in the words of Allah Almighty in the verses of His Holy Book — the place where the Almighty says: ⟪ar:يَا أَيُّهَا الَّذِينَ آمَنُوا لِمَ تَقُولُونَ مَا لَا تَفْعَلُونَ⟫

<!-- page 245 -->
⟪ar:كَبُرَ مَقْتًا عِندَ اللَّهِ أَن تَقُولُوا مَا لَا تَفْعَلُونَ⟫

On the Shari'ah ruling that fasting becomes obligatory for a boy once he can distinguish right from left:

He stated in the fourteenth majlis of the Mustansiriyya councils, may God's peace be upon its author: let us recite for you what pertains to the meaning of fasting and its division into categories. It divides into sections, seven of which are obligatory, twelve of which are recommended or delegated. The obligatory ones are: 1 — Fasting the month of Ramadan every year. 2 — Fasting two consecutive months for one who deliberately breaks his fast on a single day and cannot afford an expiation. 3 — Fasting two consecutive months for one who kills a believer by mistake, if he cannot arrange for manumission — and likewise in expiation, until resurrection. 4 — Fasting two months in expiation for zihar, for one who cannot free a slave. 5 — Fasting in expiation for perjury, for one who can find neither food nor clothing — fasting three days. 6 — Fasting in lieu of the gift offering for tamattu' hajj when one cannot find a sacrifice — ten days. 7 — Fasting for a vow. As for the recommended and mourning fasts: 1 — Fasting of i'tikaf. 2 — Fasting of Sha'ban. 3 — Fasting every Thursday on the first day of each month. 4 — Fasting the middle Wednesday. 5 — Fasting the last Thursday. 6 — Fasting the three white days. 7 — The fast of Dawud, peace be upon him, which is fasting one day and breaking the fast the next — and this is the most rigorous fast. 8 — Fasting on Friday, making it continuous with Thursday or Saturday, without singling out Friday for fasting alone. 9 — Fasting the day of Arafah for those who are not performing the pilgrimage. 10 — Fasting for a boy when he reaches the age of seven.


<!-- page 246 -->
The eleventh recommended fast is the expiation fast for hunting, which varies according to what Allah has ruled in that matter. The twelfth is fasting to compensate for shaving one's head due to harm. Our master Zaid bin Muhammad — may God sanctify his soul — stated in his hidden message that after prayer and zakat, it is obligatory upon worshippers to fast the month of Ramadan. He commanded that one month out of the twelve be fasted, saying: the month of Ramadan is thirty days. And Mawlana Amir al-Mu'minin Ali ibn Abi Talib — may Allah's peace be upon him — stated in his four hundred manners: fasting three days of each month, along with the fast of Sha'ban, dispels the obsessions of the chest and brings tranquility to the heart.

**On the sighting of the crescent moon — what has been said about it and its established course**

In the Book of Clarification, the discussion of fasting and the sighting of the crescent moon, along with what is mentioned in this chapter and the two chapters before it, is directed to those who are absent from the Imam's presence — for it is he who knows the appointed times of the crescent moons. Our master and Mawlana Suleiman bin Hassan — may God sanctify him in the chambers of the Gardens of Eden — addressed this in his first honored message, which bears the tag of the Ijdiyyah, with the Ahmadiyyah secrets and the Asjadiyyah jewels drawn from the benefits of miracles. After discussing what is primary, preferable, and its restatement, he explained: when the moon has traveled twenty-eight degrees after separating from the sun, it needs to traverse the first two stations again in order to catch up with the sun, because during that period the sun has

<!-- page 247 -->
advanced two full stations and a step into the third — so that the moon meets the sun at thirty degrees. The astronomers therefore count this as one month.

Our master and Mawlana the long-serving preacher who supports the religion of God — may He be exalted above all — addressed this in the forty-second council of the first hundred of his honored supporting councils, which bears the tag of wisdom in response to those who base fasting on the sighting of the crescent moon. These claimants assert that the month of Ramadan is sometimes complete and sometimes short, and that fasting depends on the crescent sighting. They cite as evidence the words of the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him — "Fast upon seeing it and break your fast upon seeing it; if it is obscured from you, then complete thirty days."

This claim is flawed in several respects, which we shall set out, along with proof that the month of Ramadan is never deficient and never varies in any way. We begin by responding to those who cite the report "Fast upon seeing it and break your fast upon seeing it." We say to them: we acknowledge that this report indicates that the Messenger of Allah — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family — when he wished to depart on certain campaigns near the month of Ramadan, was approached by his companions, who said: "O Messenger of Allah, we used to fast with your fasting and break our fast with your breaking of the fast — so what will our situation be in your absence?" The Messenger of Allah — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him — replied: "Fast upon seeing it and break your fast upon seeing it." The report indicates that fasting is obligatory by following the Messenger when he is present, or whoever takes his place when he is absent; and breaking the fast is obligatory by following his breaking of the fast. Fasting upon the sighting of the crescent is therefore one of the matters of necessity only

<!-- page 248 -->
during the absence of the Messenger and the Imam who takes his place. When the Messenger is present — or when the Imam is present — the ruling is to fast by following his fast and to break it by following his breaking of it, as the people said to the Prophet — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him — "We used to fast with your fasting and break our fast with your breaking of the fast."

As for the claim that the Prophet — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him — himself fasted upon seeing the crescent moon, this is flawed on three counts: two legal and rational, and one other. The first is that it is well established that the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him — while he was sincere, had the angel Gabriel — peace be upon him — coming to him morning and evening with revelation and the Holy Quran. Since Gabriel — peace be upon him — would come to him morning and evening with news from the heavens, the Prophet had no need to turn his face toward the sky in search of the crescent, for he had one who brought him certain news. Had he been observed looking toward the heavens seeking the crescent, it would have contradicted his statement that Gabriel was descending to him — and had anyone asked him about that, it would have been established that he was self-sufficient in that regard. Moreover, it is narrated from him — peace be upon him — in a report where he said: "I know the pathways of heaven as you know the pathways of the earth." If, after that great saying, he had been seen seeking the crescent to be told about it, people would have risen and said: "Where is this from what you said yesterday — that you know the pathways of heaven as we know the pathways of the earth?"

As for the rational argument: it is well established that the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him — cannot be hidden from him the differing conditions

<!-- page 249 -->
of crescent sightings and their gradations — a matter that is barely concealed from a mine-worker or one who knows the positions of the earth. The earth's positions differ: some are elevated enough to sight the crescent

faster, such as the peaks of mountains and what follows the same course, while others are lower and slower in sighting. If this is known to be his condition — that such things were not hidden from him — then to suppose otherwise would be the gravest of deficiencies, and the Prophet is free from all deficiency. How then can reason demand that he, who is required to know the difference between the positions, should impose upon all people an obligation of fasting tied to the obligation of Hajj on the same single basis — knowing full well that this is untenable? For some people sight the crescent on a given night while others do not. It cannot be right that one day is counted as belonging to Sha'ban by those who cannot see it, and as belonging to Ramadan by those who can. This is something no sane person doubts, and only an ignorant person would dismiss it.

Beyond this, Allah the Almighty said in His book: *"Fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may be pious."* Those before you refers to the Christians, and their fasting is not based on sighting but on calculation. Then He confirmed this by saying *"a number of days"* — and a numbered count of days is one that is already determined and requires neither sighting nor looking. If it were possible that Ramadan is sometimes thirty days and sometimes twenty-nine, He would not have said "a number of days" with certainty — just as one does not say "these are a calculated account" or "these are a few dirhams" about an amount that varies. Furthermore, according to the established Sunnah, six months of the year are complete and six are short. The original and foundational arrangement is built on completeness without deficiency: the first month, Muharram, is complete; Safar is short; the first Rabi' is complete; the second Rabi' is short;

<!-- page 250 -->
Jumada al-Ula is complete; Jumada al-Ukhra is short; Rajab is complete; Sha'ban is short; and the month of Ramadan is complete. The Prophet — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him — said: "Sha'ban is short, not Ramadan." The proof of Sha'ban's shortness is the night of its middle — its fifteenth night is indeed the middle in fact, because fourteen nights precede it and fourteen follow it, making it the true center. This cannot hold for Ramadan: if you make the fifteenth night its midpoint, it fails because fifteen days would remain after it, and if you make the sixteenth night the midpoint, that too fails, since sixteen is not half of thirty.

What further confirms the completeness of Ramadan is the ruling on expiation for one who deliberately breaks his fast on a single day: he must fast two consecutive months as repentance to Allah — that is, sixty days equivalent to Ramadan. If he cannot do that, he feeds sixty poor people. If it were possible that Ramadan is twenty-nine days, then the expiation would allow for feeding fifty-eight poor people rather than sixty. So praise be to Allah, O believers — be aware of the completeness of your religion and your success in faithfulness to it, by holding to the cords of faith through following your guardians, the guardians of the Most Merciful.

Allah the Most Holy said in a similar case in his book of noble biography: he came to the court of King Abu Kalijar al-Daylami, the ruler of Shiraz, Iraq al-Ajam, and its kingdoms, and there the king brought

<!-- page 251 -->
a nobleman from the nobles of the Zaydi school of thought, who delivered a statement among them on the matter of fasting — whether its obligation is tied to the sighting of the crescent or not. Al-Sharif reportedly said that he ruled in favor of the Messenger of Allah — peace and blessings be upon him — and then gathered his five fingers and said: "We are unlettered and do not count," repeating the gesture until he had completed thirty counts with his fingers. He then began again with his fingers, and when he finished the second round, he fell one finger short. Our master the Supporter of Religion — may God sanctify his soul — then said: God forbid that the Prophet — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family, whom God honored and who could see beyond the veil with full awareness and consciousness — should be described as unlettered to the point of being unable to distinguish thirty from twenty-nine with his tongue, such that he would need to resort to counting on fingers and moving his hand in the manner of a shepherd counting cattle and sheep. Moreover, the Prophet — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family — said: "I know the pathways of heaven as you know the pathways of the earth." If Allah's blessings be upon him and his family, had this great saying been confined to something that drove him to look to the sky seeking the crescent, people would have risen and said: "Where is this from your statement yesterday that you know the pathways of heaven as we know the pathways of the earth?" Beyond this, if seeking the crescent were something obligatory and necessary for us in particular, he would have been free of that need — for

<!-- page 252 -->
Gabriel — peace be upon him — informs him of the universe, and if Gabriel informs him that the crescent has appeared and then he proceeds to ask about it,

he would be more likely to have revelation about his own affairs explained to him. Even if one of us had access to a king of angels whom he could question about unseen matters, he would not rely on himself in asking and striving — this is something upon which no proof or evidence can be founded.

From the shame of this answer, the man stood up while stumbling in his clothes until the people rose in the courtroom. The two scholars laughed at him and mocked him, and each of them went to his home.

One of the preachers — may God sanctify their souls — posed the following question in argument against those who deviate from the path of guidance:

What do the learned scholars of all the people of Islam say regarding the words of the Lord of Majesty and Honor: *"O you who believe, fasting is prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may be pious — a number of days. If any of you is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days. If any of you witnesses the month, let him fast; and whoever is sick or on a journey, then a number of other days. Allah wants ease for you and does not want hardship for you, and that you complete the count and glorify Allah for having guided you, and perhaps you will be thankful."*

What is the meaning of *"so that you may be pious"*? And who are *"those before"*? How many are *"a number of days"*? And what is the meaning of *"witnessing the month"*? Scholars of the Arabic language said that *al-shahr* means the crescent moon; others said it means the full set of days that constitute the month — and on the second reading, the phrase *"the month of Ramadan in which the Quran was revealed"* is evidence that the Quran was revealed during days

<!-- page 253 -->
that are numbered, not during the crescent itself. And Allah said: *"The number of months with Allah is twelve months in the Book of Allah, on the day He created the heavens and the earth, of which four are sacred."* Only a single crescent is known — one that waxes and becomes a full moon, then wanes throughout the days of the month. The months themselves are known from Muharram to Dhu al-Hijjah.

Some scholars said regarding His saying *"whoever among you witnesses the month"*: it means whoever among you sees the crescent — and one who witnesses it is the one who is present in the city, as opposed to the absent person who is on a journey; it is he who must fast and is not permitted to break his fast.

As for His saying *"and complete the count"* — what is that count? How much is it? And the takbir on Eid al-Fitr after the completion of fasting — is it complete? Is the prescribed waiting period for the month thirty days, twenty-nine days, or twenty-eight days — and which of these is truly *"a number of days"*?

The Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him — said: "Whatever reaches you attributed to me, present it to the Book of Allah; if the Book of Allah does not agree with it, then I did not say it and I am not contradicted by it." So how could I contradict the Book of Allah, given that fasting by the crescent sighting may prevent the fulfillment of the count of thirty days? They have used as their evidence the saying of the Prophet — peace and blessings be upon him — "Fast upon seeing it and break your fast upon seeing it" — yet, by their own account, this does not agree with the completion of the count as expressed in the Holy Book. This is a reminder for the people of understanding hearts.

It was then narrated from the Prophet — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him and his family — in what was narrated by al-Sadiq Ja'far ibn Muhammad — peace and blessings of Allah be upon him —


<!-- page 254 -->
It is established that Sha'ban had passed and the month of Ramadan had not yet begun, and the book aligns with this.

It is clear and true that the completion of a month is thirty days, and we find the saying of the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family — "Fast at the sighting of it and break the fast at the sighting of it" — points to the reception of that sighting.

In light of what the scholars of language have mentioned regarding the term *lamas*, we found this *lamas* to be one of reception. Some of them also cited the following lines of poetry:

*As for the destruction of the house, the house is built — for death, mothers feed their children.*

Another said: "They were born to death and were born to ruin." And this aligns with the word of Allah, Exalted be He: "This is what you were promised for the Day of Reckoning." The reckoning made the consequence bear the blame, and this is in keeping with what we have mentioned — which is the most fitting interpretation.

The correct view is that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family fasted at the sighting of it — meaning at its emergence — and that you fast and break your fast at the sighting of it, meaning so that it may be seen; and Allah knows best.

If you hold otherwise, then bring proof from the Qur'an for the completion of the month as mentioned therein, and explain why the Messenger of Allah (may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him) did not say: "Fast after seeing it and break the fast after seeing it," so that the matter would be clearer to his community. And it might be said that one acts for the day on which one will be resurrected, and the Hereafter holds the reward for what one does, and the Book of the House aligns with the saying of the Prophet sent to us.

So grant us a sound and healing answer, for we do not travel the road of arrogance and dismissiveness. Praise be to Allah, Most High, Almighty, Most Exalted, and may the blessings of Allah be upon His Messenger to all humankind and jinn, and upon his family and companions, who are clear vessels of the meanings of

250

<!-- page 255 -->
the Qur'an, unchanged through the passage of ages — peace, mercy, and generosity be upon them. Allah is our Protector.

Our master Zayd ibn Muhammad said: His soul, in his message attached to the hidden interpretation of the eclipse prayer — that Allah, Blessed and Exalted, when He created creation, decreed among them wonders; the first to believe ended it and made all that was created in the first age a proof for the people of the last age. He made the eclipse of the moon a covering for it and decreed that its covering falls on the fourteenth night of the month, though it may sometimes fall on the thirteenth night. The majority of eclipses, however, occur on the fourteenth night, and all of this contains signs and arguments for Allah.

Evidence lies in the fact that the moon is absent and hidden from the world for twenty-eight nights of every month, and it is absent and concealed for two days and two nights, which is forty-eight hours, and then it reappears after those forty-eight hours.

This blessed message on the matter of the month of Ramadan is attached to the obligation of fasting the month of Ramadan, and it is addressed to our master and Mawlana, the summoner of the age, Ahmad Hamid al-Din ibn Abdallah al-Kirmani — may his intercession avail us — who is the proof of our master al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah, the Commander of the Believers, may God bless him and grant him peace.

251

<!-- page 256 -->
In the name of God, the Merciful, the Compassionate — and in Him I place my trust.

Praise be to Allah, by whose grace good deeds are accomplished, and through obedience to His guardians, blessings prevail. May Allah bless His messenger and His servant Muhammad, the best of people, the pride of those who came before him among the noble messengers, the righteous among the righteous, the light of guidance and the luminaries in darkness. And thereafter —

Brother, you wrote to recall that the people of division and disagreement — in the habits they have adopted — have driven a wedge against the people of truth and the coalition, and have said much in challenging them regarding the community, opposing them in that they began fasting before the general public. You asked me to clarify the matter of entering the fast among the Shi'i community of the Commander of the Faithful, may God's peace be upon him, specifically the fast of the year four hundred, which was obligated by commands received from the Immaculate Prophet — entering it two days before the crescent moon was sighted with our own eyes — and to address the problem with a clear statement, explanation, and proof.

I therefore resolved to set that forth in a letter in which I cite some of the proofs. The healing answer does not alarm the discerning eyes of the saints in the structure of its arguments, and it warms the hearts of the notables while those who violate its meanings are left behind. I therefore say, with the help of Allah and His guardians in His land — peace and blessings of Allah be upon them — and I label it: the necessary message on fasting the month of Ramadan. For it contains arguments that bind all Muslims, and no one loyal to the principle of reason and sense can turn away from them. Allah is the guardian of right success.

**Chapter:** Know — may Allah protect you from ignorance and guard you from the way of misguidance — that the people of

252

<!-- page 257 -->
opposition, had they possessed discernment and insight, or had they a share in reason and understanding, or been divided from it, the devil's machinations against them would have been repelled, their concern would have been for the truth, and they would have been among those whom Allah excepts in His Book, saying: "They will always be in disagreement except those upon whom your Lord has mercy." But when they mounted the steed of passion and the causes of personal opinion overwhelmed them, the devil gained hold of them and they followed him — all except a company of true believers. Allah has said regarding them: "Have they not traveled through the earth so that they may have hearts with which to reason, or ears with which to hear? For it is not the eyes that go blind, but it is the hearts within the breasts that go blind." They are like camels in what they wallow in, yet like the dead — they do not feel. As Allah Almighty said: "Dead, not alive, and they do not perceive."

They died to the knowledge of realities and turned a blind eye to them, driven by arrogance toward the people of truth, by desire and contention. They became like beasts in their disputes — hesitating in their blindness, running without discernment or insight, unaware of the division and separation that Allah has forbidden, as He said: "And do not be like those who dispersed and disagreed" — except out of their licentiousness, arrogance, and envy, and their refusal to obey the one whom Allah has made obligatory to obey from among the rulers of the Command. Allah Almighty, in His glory, called for returning to them in all matters of dispute, saying: "Whatever you dispute about, refer it to Allah and the Messenger." And He said: "And if they had referred it to the Messenger and to those in authority among them, those who can draw conclusions from it would have known it."

253

<!-- page 258 -->
Arrogance, licentiousness, envy, and disobedience to the people of truth prevent one from standing upon the facts and emerging from the company of the people of ignorance. Whoever opposes the people of truth and persists in his blindness and misguidance brings himself to ruin and evil. The word of punishment justly applies to him and he earns the verse of chastisement — we seek refuge in Allah from division after unity, from tyranny after faith, and from disobedience to His guardians — Allah's prayers be upon them — after submission and belief. We ask Him to make us among those who win His mercy by obeying Him and obeying His guardians, for He is ever good and responsible.

He then made it obligatory upon His servants in the explicit text of His Book to fast, saying: "Fasting has been prescribed for you as it was prescribed for those before you, so that you may become righteous."

The Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family said: "Sha'ban is a month and Ramadan is the month of Allah, and fasting is obligatory." The Imams (peace be upon them) said: "Fasting the month of Ramadan is obligatory upon every Muslim." When Allah Almighty imposed the fast of the month of Ramadan upon His servants, He did not leave them — the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) and his family — blind to the knowledge of its time, just as He did not leave them in ignorance of how to fulfill other obligatory duties, such as the number and times of prayers. When He imposed fasting and the amount of zakat on its applicable categories, He said: "Fast at the sighting of it and break the fast at the sighting of it." On this narration there is scarcely any disagreement among the sects of Muslims, and it is the pillar upon which the people of the outward rely in their argument. We therefore say that Allah Almighty created all things in pairs, so that nothing in creation is left in solitary oneness.

254

<!-- page 259 -->
Nothing shares with Him in His oneness. Allah said: "Glory be to Him who created all the pairs," and He said: "And of all things We have created pairs, that you may remember."

We observe that all things come in pairs and forms — such as essence and extension, sky and earth, land and sea, plain and mountain, night and day, good and evil, body and soul, outward and inward, this world and the next, and male and female, along with other things that could be enumerated at length. We also observe that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) established the foundation of his law and its governing principle by pairing commands and obligatory duties with their counterparts — pointing thereby to what we have said about the singularity of Allah's oneness — as with prayer, which he paired with zakat; the call to prayer, which he paired with the iqama; Hajj, which he paired with Umrah; al-Safa, which he paired with al-Marwa; the obligatory prayer, which he paired with the Sunnah prayer; bowing, which he paired with prostration; and the testimony that there is no god but Allah, which he paired with the testimony that Muhammad is the Prophet of Allah — and so on with many other examples that could be mentioned at length.

We then say that sight, too, is of two kinds, each coupled with the other: natural vision and intellectual vision. Natural vision is that of the eye; it perceives colors — white, black, red, yellow, and others — and forms — circular, triangular, square, and so on — and on this basis it is relied upon because it falls within the domain of the senses. Intellectual vision, by contrast, belongs to the realm of the heart and the soul; it perceives what natural vision cannot perceive —

255

<!-- page 260 -->
from the causes that are hidden from natural perception. Allah said: "The heart did not lie in what it saw," and He said: "Did you not see how your Lord stretched out the shadow?" and "Have you not seen what your Lord did to the companions of the elephant?" We know that the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) was not alive at the time of the companions of the elephant and thus did not see them with his eyes — rather, what is meant is the vision of the soul, which belongs to the domain of the heart and knowledge. It is also said in proverb that such-and-such a person is blind, meaning that he is slow and late in knowing things and seeing them in their absence.

Since it is established that sight is of two kinds — like all things, which are divided into two states and two aspects to complete the oneness of Allah so that nothing shares in that oneness — we say that natural vision, which belongs to the side of the eye, only perceives things that are physically present within limits and time, such as colors and forms. Intellectual vision, which belongs to the side of the heart and knowledge, perceives what natural vision cannot perceive and what the eye misses from sight. Given all of this, it follows that intellectual vision — which belongs to the heart and knowledge — is more precise than natural vision, which belongs to the eye and sight. It is more refined in its perception of things, for its knowledge of things reaches their realities rather than merely perceiving their outward appearance. By way of example: if a person takes from a pool a single drop of water, the wisdom of intellectual vision holds that the amount of that water has decreased by a drop, and it perceives this as a true perception — whereas this decrease is not perceptible to the outward eye and sight.

Since intellectual vision is the more prior and more refined in perception, it is all the more obligatory to act upon it in fulfilling

256

<!-- page 261 -->
the duties imposed by Allah Almighty. The first principle is to restrict oneself not to what is inferior — that is, the level of the ignorant — but to what reason demands, which binds people to the truth: namely, to take the more precise and superior, which is the vision of knowledge and the soul.

Thus, if a person knows in his heart and perceives that the time for one of the prayers has come, the prayer becomes obligatory for him even if he has not heard the call to prayer. Similarly, one who is fasting — if he sees with his heart and knows that dawn has arrived, even though his eyesight is prevented from seeing it — would be obligated to abstain from food, even if his eyesight does not behold it. And if that is the case, then fasting is obligatory upon one whose sight is obscured from food — because he knows that dawn has arrived even though his eyesight is prevented from seeing it.

By the same logic, we say that the beginning of the fast becomes obligatory with the knowledge that the cause of fasting — which is the appearance of the crescent moon — has occurred at its greatest strength, even if sight is prevented from seeing it due to light, clouds, dust, or mountains. For it is customary that when the cause occurs, its effect becomes obligatory — so that neither should precede the other except in rank. The call in their entering the fast by virtue of intellectual vision is one in which they scarcely fall into error, on the basis of knowledge and understanding.

Then, when the Prophet (peace and blessings of Allah be upon him) made fasting obligatory in certain months of the year, it is reported that Allah Almighty made the sun and the moon the means by which years, months, their times, and their people are known, and He said:

257


<!-- page 262 -->
Allah is the One who made the sun a radiance and the moon a light, and apportioned for it phases so that you might know the number of years and the reckoning. And He said: "They ask you about the crescents" — the year consists of twelve months, as Allah said: "Indeed, the number of months with Allah is twelve months in the Book of Allah from the day He created the heavens and the earth." These months are not complete until their appointed periods are fulfilled, and a lunar month is not perfect in its days except through the moon's journey from the point where it is one degree and one minute ahead of the sun — this is the conjunction recognized by astronomers — until it completes its full arc and returns to that same design and conjunction. From this knowledge it follows that when the moon's degree and minute within its zodiacal position become identical to those of the sun, it has departed from the old month; and when it leaves the sun on its way to the next month, it is moving toward the conjunction. At the conjunction, the light of one half of the moon's mass is drawn away from the side facing the earth until it passes over, and the upper half of the mass is illuminated. At that moment, the ocean side reflects light the way each half of its mass does on the night of opposition when facing the center — that is, the earth — and the light of the half facing us appears at the crescent and grows as the moon increases its distance. The causes that prevent the eye from perceiving all of this are many. We say that the eye's inability to perceive all these conditions arises from within itself, and from the fact that it sees light emanating from the moon's body as the moon moves toward conjunction at the end of the month. When the moon departs at the beginning of the new month, the condition is one of light.

<!-- page 263 -->
The sun prevents the eye from seeing the moon's light, just as other things prevent the eye from seeing and knowing — such as clouds, dust, a wall, or a mountain. Do you not observe that the planets are not visible during the day because of the sun's light and its overwhelming radiance, yet when that light is broken, the eye can perceive them? This is because light is diminished by refraction. If it is true that the amount of light from the moon at its departure from the sun would not exist were it not for the sun's own light and rays — then what the eye sees is that the sun's light is itself the barrier, just as other barriers are, like clouds and the atmospheric cycle. Since the eye is prevented by sunlight from seeing the crescent after its departure, the soul — from the perspective of knowledge — is not prevented from seeing it as it truly is. Fasting therefore becomes obligatory through the vision of knowledge, which is derived from the established signs we have outlined. By these testimonies, the people of the Sayyid's call are on the truth and the right path in their entry into fasting through the soul's vision. And if they are correct, then whoever disagrees with them is in error. We then ask: where does the person of right — and others who differ — derive the basis for seeing by sight alone, without the vision of the heart and knowledge? The evidence rests on seeing from two angles, and no one denies this except out of apparent arrogance. You say that a person does not know anything until his sight falls upon it, and that once he sees it, the ruling of knowledge-vision is lifted from him. We say your argument is corrupt and invalid.

<!-- page 264 -->
For one thing: if we ask you whether you have seen that Allah Almighty has made this obligatory upon you, you must say no — in which case we say: we have not established it upon you, yet you have made it obligatory that a person does not know anything until his sight falls upon it, and your sight does not fall upon it. You are therefore compelled to abandon the position of sight-seeing and return to the hadith of knowledge. You say: we know, and we have the proofs, and we have confirmed it — that is our position and our foundation. Secondly: the learned man has commanded the world regarding its events, and if we had asked you about something, you would have to tell him that you are wise enough to employ the vision of knowledge and proof, and that proof does not rule by the vision of the eye alone — which is precisely what we say. Thirdly: we ask what you say about a man who broke his fast and did not see the crescent moon with his own eye, but thirty days of Ramadan had passed — was his breaking of the fast valid or not? If you say it was valid, we ask: how so, when the crescent was not seen with the eye? And yet your doctrine holds that fasting and breaking the fast are not permissible until after seeing the crescent with the eye. You are therefore compelled to say that we know that after thirty days the crescent has been seen, even though it was obscured — which means you have abandoned the ruling of sight-vision and returned by necessity to the hadith of knowledge-vision, and that is our position. If you say it is not valid, then you must hold that one cannot know anything except through the physical senses that gather sight and other faculties — things that are physical in their form and quality only. Yet the soul dispenses with the aid of the senses in its knowledge as a result of making use of other faculties within it. The fate of the senses and their yield is that the soul rises above the need for them.

<!-- page 265 -->
Within man, the soul is one of the instruments by which he perceives things — such as hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch — and it has a capacity that none of these other faculties possesses: the ability to rise above all of them, after the images of their perceptions have been received by it, and to perceive what hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch do not perceive. Just as hearing perceives what sight, smell, taste, and touch do not perceive, and smell perceives what hearing, sight, taste, and touch do not perceive — so too, in the wisdom of Allah Almighty, each sense has its own domain, lest man be deprived of perceiving some part of what Allah has created in the worlds, and lest he remain unaware of it as others are. His perception is thus multiple: perception through the five senses, as in his perception of colors and shapes through sight, of indicative and non-indicative sounds through hearing, and so on for each of the other senses. Beyond this, his perception extends to what the senses do not perceive, and he perceives through inference what the senses do not directly reach. By inference, what he realizes through the senses — all that falls within the domain of the senses and what they perceive — can also be perceived by him independently through inference, without the senses. In such cases he dispenses with them in the first instance, perceiving by a second perception on its own. When it is necessary to know something, the soul accepts the image of the thing as perceived by the senses at the first perception, and that image remains present to the soul in itself when it returns to that thing without needing to use the senses again — just as images of the planets in themselves and their qualities

<!-- page 266 -->
were perceived through sight, so the soul then dispenses with that sight for a second perception of the image in itself, preserving it by accepting it. The soul then takes the place of the senses by itself, just as the soul of one whose eyes have been enriched by images of what he has known through his sight gains another perception — the soul's acceptance of that image and its retention in its specificity, based on its form, without any impossibility, when a second perception by sight is required. What is meant here is not that one should seek the crescent moon and see it with the eye every month and year, knowing its appearance in its form, precision, and coarseness — for that would require sight as the instrument for perceiving such a thing. What is actually meant in seeking it is to know the validity of its being in the west in front of the sun after having been in the east. And perceiving this validity of its being in the west is a matter for the soul, not for sight — because sight, even without obstructions, only perceives shapes and colors.
